xiv

Abstract
Methods of instruction which are intended to facilitate understanding tend to
incorporate all of the elements required for understanding in the instructions.
Frequently, these types of instructions may overwhelm a learner’s limited working
memory and hinder learning. The five experiments reported in this thesis were
generated by cognitive load theory. Cognitive load theory is based on the
assumption that features of human cognitive architecture have important
implications for how we learn and understand. The experiments examined an
alternative approach to instructional design which had the goal of promoting
schema construction in order to foster understanding. A two phase, isolatedinteracting elements learning approach was developed which, in the first phase,
artificially reduced the element interactivity of complex material by presenting the
material as isolated elements of information that could be processed serially,
rather than simultaneously, in working memory. In the second phase of
instruction, all the information for understanding was presented. The control
group’s

instruction was

a more conventional approach to promoting

understanding where all the information for understanding was presented in both
Phase 1 and 2. Although a possible consequence of artificially reducing the
element interactivity of material may be an initial decrease in a student’s capacity
for understanding, it was hypothesised that over the longer term it would lead to
an increase in learners’ understanding.
The results reported in this thesis provide powerful evidence that for certain
groups of learners, information is better learnt through the isolated-interacting
elements instructional method. Evidence is also provided in support of the
proposal that schema construction is the mechanism underlying the success of
the isolated-interacting elements instructional method. Experiments 1 and 3
demonstrated that when novice students are required to learn complex
information, the isolated-interacting elements method of instruction was superior
to the control method. In
xv

contrast, no evidence was found in Experiments 2 and 4 to suggest a difference
between these instructional techniques. These contrasting results can be
explained by the different levels of domain knowledge possessed by the
participants of each experiment. The results of Experiment 5 were similar to
those of Experiments 1 and 3, namely that students performed superiorly if they
had studied the isolated elements followed by interacting elements instruction.
These results provide significant theoretical support for the claim that expertise is
the key factor implicated in the beneficial effects associated with the isolatedinteracting elements instructional technique since the participants in Experiment
5 had previously participated in Experiments 2 and 4. The difference in the
outcomes of these experiments can be explained by the level of domain specific
knowledge the participants of Experiment 5 had in each of the knowledge
domains tested by the various experiments.

Chapter 1

Human memory & problem
solving

1.1

The information processing view of
memory

1.1.1

An overview of the information processing view of
memory: the modal model

When developing the information processing model of memory, the cognitive school of
psychology was strongly influenced by a computer metaphor. In the information
processing model, the process of memory is seen to replicate the three stage process
enacted upon data entered into a computer: encoding, storage and retrieval. Encoding
is the process whereby information from the environment is transferred into the
memory system;

the information is then stored for a variable period before it is

recovered or ‘retrieved’ back into consciousness.

During the 1960s, the field’s understanding of memory was revolutionised by the
proposal of a memory system that contained both a long and short term memory store.
Examples of this style of model are those proposed by Atkinson and Shiffrin (1968) and
Norman and Rumelhart (1970). Over the next thirty years the debate about the
processes of memory have not subsided, and research in this area continues to
flourish. Despite differences in the models that have been developed to explain the
operation of memory, a strong consensus exists among researchers in the field over

1

some essential features of the memory system (Ericsson and Kintsch, 1995; Eysenck
and Keane, 1990; Norman, 1970). For instance, modern conceptualisations of memory
still maintain the basic division of memory into a long term and a short term store,
despite their increased complexity of explanation. In fact, Shiffrin has argued that “(T)he
concept of short term memory has virtually become a universal feature in theories of
cognition” (1993: 196). Most modern theories can be viewed as refinements rather than
rejections of the basic storage model of memory (Eysenck and Keane, 1990). Given
this situation, the constraints and capabilities of the basic information processing
model will be examined before looking at modern variations.

The model divides human memory into three interacting stores: sensory memory,
working memory and long term memory. Figure 1.1 represents the modal model of
memory. The arrows between the boxes indicate the flow of information. To summarise
the figure, information from the environment is perceived by sensory registers, it then
moves into short term memory were it is encoded and briefly stored. If attended to,
information will move into long term memory where more permanent storage occurs.
Information can be retrieved back from long term memory into consciousness or short
term memory. Each section of the model will now be examined in more detail.

1.1.2

Sensory memory

Sensory memory is a holding system where information from the environment is
able to enter into the human information processing system. Modality specific
sensory registers exist in sensory memory and their function is to perceive
information from the environment. These registers have the capacity to transform
physical stimuli from the environment into physiological representations; in this
form information is able to be temporarily retained in the memory system (Norman,
1970). The majority of research in this field has focused on the visual and the
auditory sensory registers, although each of the senses theoretically does have
this capability (Eysenck and Keane, 1990).

Although the sensory registers are capable of recording a vast number of stimuli at
any one time, the system has severe limitations in terms of the duration it can
retain information. Sperling’s (1960) research on the visual register, using letter
arrays, indicated that although most visual information was initially registered, the
majority was lost in less than half a second. Sperling (1960) concluded that little
recognition or assignment of meaning had occurred by this point; however, Butler
(1974) disputed this contention. Butler’s results suggested that the number of
letters that could be remembered increased if the letters in the array resembled
English words.

In a manner similar to Sperling (1960), Darwin, Turvey and Crowder (1972)
investigated the capabilities of the auditory sensory register. Similarities between
the function and process of visual and auditory registers were found to exist.
Darwin et al.’s (1972) results indicated that as the time delay between the
experimental stimuli and the instruction to recall the information increased,
memory for auditory stimuli decreased. Auditory registers were found to have the
ability to
3

retain stimuli for periods of 2-3 seconds (Darwin et al., 1972; Treisman, 1964),
which is longer than visual stimuli can be held.

To summarise, the key function of the sensory memory system is its ability to retain
some representation of stimuli after the physical stimuli no longer exists in the
environment: hence the conceptualisation of sensory memory as a ‘holding tank’.
Temporary retention of stimuli allows the transferral of information to other parts of
the memory system and facilitates the process of meaning extraction from these
stimuli within the memory system as a whole (Eysenck and Keane, 1990). Norman
and Rumelhart (1970) contended that recognition and identification of meaning
take place in the transferral of stimuli between sensory and short term memory
because their findings suggested that it was the meaning rather than the physical
characteristics of the stimuli that was present in short term memory.

4

1.1.3

Short term or working memory

Limitations are also integral to defining the operation of short term memory. Miller’s
(1956a) landmark paper identified the severe capacity limitations of short term memory.
In fact, Miller asserted that our immediate memory system is limited to dealing with
around only 5 to 9 items or chunks of information at any one time. In subsequent years,
this estimate of short term memory capacity has been further reduced: Simon (1974)
suggested working memory capacity was closer to five chunks; while Halford, Maybery
& Bain (1986) and Sweller & Chandler (1994) proposed this number was reduced
further if the information elements needed to be combined or processed concurrently.

Short-term memory also possesses limitations in the duration information can be
retained. It can only be held for a few seconds unless rehearsal takes place; if attention
from rehearsal is diverted, the information currently stored in short term memory
becomes unavailable (Ericsson and Kintsch, 1995). Peterson and Peterson (1959)
illustrated this phenomena by asking participants to recall a string of letters after a
delay ranging from between three and eighteen seconds. During the delay period,
participants completed an alternative task which hampered their ability to rehearse the
letter string. The researchers observed a strong relationship between the amount
recalled and the length of the delay before recall. As the period of delay increased there
was a rapid deterioration in the participant’s ability to recall the letters; in fact, after
eighteen seconds only about ten percent of the original material was recalled.

5

1.1.3.1

Working Memory

“The concept of working memory has increasingly replaced the older concept of shortterm memory” (Baddeley, 1992: 556). The first cognitivists viewed short term memory
as a unitary system whose major role was temporary storage (Baddeley, 1992); thus
short term memory was viewed as playing a relatively passive role in cognition.
However, during the 1970s, evidence from the field of problem solving emerged
indicating short term memory’s involvement in information processing rather than just
information storage (Greeno, 1973; Simon, 1975). These findings fuelled an evolution
of the concept of short term memory into ‘working memory’: a system of memory that
processed or ‘worked on’ information as well as temporarily storing it. It was
hypothesised that while working memory executed processes such as addition or
comparing the premises of arguments, it also kept the information required by the
processing accessible (Haberlandt, 1994). For example, a piece of text often refers to
its subject by using a pronoun such as ‘it’ rather than repeating the noun. Working
memory is involved in keeping information important for comprehension, such as the
referent of ‘it‘ active, as well as executing the cognitive process of reading. The
limitations of the short term store are still hypothesised to be critical to its method of
functioning, despite the transformation in nomenclature. For example, the storage and
processing functions of working memory directly compete for resources if necessary:
Posner and Rossman (1965) demonstrated this characteristic by showing that as
processing became more cognitively demanding, the ability to store information
decreased.

In contrast to short term memory, working memory’s role in the cognition process was
viewed as extremely active. The ‘active processor’ view has progressed in the field to
the extent that it has been proposed that all cognitive operations are carried in the short
term memory system (Schneider, 1993). Shiffrin questions the ability of the short term
system to cope with all these functions “Is there room in the short term system for

6

all the necessary information and machinery?” (1993: 195). He has suggested instead
that many of these operations are automatic, and therefore are not using short term
capacity. Other theorists have taken the view that although processing and storage are
important functions of working memory, they are certainly not the only functions.
Baddeley (1992) and Schneider and Detweiler (1987) have contended that it is the
coordination of memory resources that is the prime function of working memory.

Modern theories of memory also contend that the working memory system is divided
into multiple components: each component has different capabilities to deal with
certain types of information. An example of the multi component approach is
Baddeley’s research (1981, 1990, 1992). He divided working memory into three major
parts: the central executive, the articulatory or phonological loop and the visual spatial
sketch pad. It is proposed that the central executive has an overseeing role: it is the
control centre of the system, it therefore selects and operates the appropriate cognitive
processes. Additionally, the central executive has a storage function with the result that
capacity limitations are applicable to this part of the system. Storage also occurs
elsewhere in the system, the articulatory loop maintains verbal material through sub
vocal rehearsal and the Visuo-Spatial Scratch Pad conserves imagery and spatial
material through visualisation.

Whichever detailed model of working memory operations is accepted, the most
important point about working memory for this thesis are the limitations of capacity and
duration that apply. It is these limitations that have the most readily visible impact upon
memory and learning.

7

1.1.4

Chunking

Although Miller (1956a) initially received much acclaim for placing a numerical
value on short term memory capacity, Baddeley (1994) has contended, when
assessing the impact of Miller’s work for the centennial issue of Psychological
Review, that his enduring legacy is the concept of chunking. A chunk is any
stimulus that has been unitised through previous experience (Simon, 1986).

Miller (1956a) stated that short term memory was limited to dealing with 5-9 chunks
of information at any one time. However he further proposed that the amount of
information in a ‘chunk’ was not set: the amount was dependent upon the amount
of previous knowledge a person had in the specific area. Thus, although the
memory span is fixed by the number of chunks, the amount of information involved
can increase by building larger chunks.

Humans form chunks by grouping

information according to patterns or meaning. As learning in an area increases so
does the information that can be grouped into a chunk and therefore the amount of
information the person can remember. For example, a chunk of information could
be single letters, single words or whole phrases, yet native language speakers
would have the ability to remember approximately 7 items from each of these
categories. Thus while most English speaking people would remember around
seven of these twelve letters K, B, A, N, E, Y, E, K, C, L, H, S; if the same letters
were presented in another order, as English words, LAKE; BENCH; SKY, the same
participants would have little difficulty remembering all twelve letters. Miller (1956b)
used the analogy of a money purse and coins to explain chunking and the capacity
limitations of short term memory: short term memory is a like a small purse that
can only ever hold around seven coins. What can change however, is the
denomination or value of each of the seven coins: they can be of low value (single
letters or numbers) or of a much greater value (whole words concepts or
relationships). There is some evidence however, that as the information contained
8

in a chunk increases, the number of chunks retained in memory decreases
(Simon, 1974). The ability to chunk information is an essential part of learning; in
fact, Newell (1990) contends it is the most essential process of learning.

A valid criticism, however, has been levelled at the concept of chunking:

since

there is no way to determine the size of a chunk independently of the measurement
of memory span, the contention that the number of chunks retained is fixed, loses
it’s empirical validity. In fact, the definition of a chunk becomes quite circular since a
chunk is the amount of material that short term memory has the capacity to hold five
of (Simon, 1974).

Despite these criticisms, several individual’s remarkable memory performances,
based on chunking strategies, have been recorded. Ericsson, Chase and Faloon
(1980) investigated the digit span ability of a person over the course of eighteen
months. The participant, a university athlete, practised 3 to 5 hours a week over the
period and improved the number of digits he could remember from seven to nearly
eighty digits. The researchers investigated the strategies the participant had used:
he had related the strings of random digits to running times. The participant was
able therefore, to make meaningful units from the lists of numbers. As the list of
numbers continued, he constantly recoded and regrouped numbers into a set
hierarchical pattern, while rehearsing the most recently presented numbers. These
results do not point to an improvement in the subject’s working memory: when
presented with strings of letters his performance was average. What had changed
was the amount of a certain type of information the participant could include in a
chunk.

Gobet and Simon (1996) explored the ability of the first author, a former Chess
Master, to remember chess positions. Over 150 sessions his retention of pieces
improved from 40 pieces to an average of 123 pieces. In 4 cases he was able to
correctly place more than 160 pieces. Insight into the memory process was
9

provided by this relatively unique experimental methodology. Initially, the subject did
not use any memory techniques so a baseline measure was recorded. He then
developed the strategy of meaningfully associating set chess positions with the
names of world champion chess players. Over the trials, the subject shortened the
list of names to their first syllables in order to facilitate subvocal rehearsal of the list
during the association phase.

Ericsson and Polson (1988) investigated another case of amazing memory ability.
The memory skills of a waiter, who could remember up to twenty food orders at
once without the use of external memory aids, were examined. The waiter’s
strategy involved regrouping the presented information categorically; for example,
rather than remembering all the aspects of a meal in the order they were presented
by each diner (as the control group of university students did), he grouped, for
example, the whole table’s orders for salad dressing into one category. Each
category was then linked to a mnemonic strategy. The researchers probed the
generalisability of the waiter’s encoding strategy by changing the order information
was presented, the type of information presented and the category structure of the
information. It was found that the waiter used his original encoding strategy
regardless of the type of information involved or the order information was
presented in. As the categorical structure of the information was randomised and
the information became less meaningful, the waiter’s memory advantage was
increasingly eliminated. In fact, when the information became the most random, the
participant was recorded using a novice recall strategy. This example of skilled
memory performance illustrates the role of prior knowledge in chunking: underlying
the waiter’s performance was a strategy to recode the given information into
meaningful chunks, based on prior learning. Results from an analysis of the
waiter’s encoding and retrieval processes allowed the authors to reject the theory
that information was chunked in short term memory; it was their conclusion that
skilled memory performance involves the use of long term memory.

10

1.1.5

Long term memory

After being processed in working memory, information may be passed on to long
term memory for storage. Where sensory memory and working memory are
characterised by limitations, long term memory is characterised by a lack of
restrictions in two critical respects. Firstly, long term memory has the capability to
store information in a relatively permanent manner; and secondly, it seems to have
a vast, virtually unlimited capacity to store information. Humans are generally
unaware of the vast bulk of information they have stored in long term memory
because this information does not impede upon conscious awareness until
required. When certain information is

required, it is retrieved back into

consciousness from long term memory stores.

Storage in long term memory is much more durable than in the sensory or short
term stores; yet, it remains true that a massive percentage of the information we
encounter in our daily lives is quickly forgotten. Generally, it is accepted that storage
in long term memory occurs only after storage in short term memory as was
originally proposed in early cognitive models of memory such as Atkinson and
Shiffrin’s (1968) model. These researchers also considered storage to be a
function of amount of time stimuli had been held in short term memory. On the
other hand, Anderson (1983) has suggested storage in long term memory is a
function of the amount of times information enters short term memory. Debate
upon this issue still continues, however, research has been able to more clearly
define factors that improve the probability of information recall.

One proposal for improving the probability of information being stored and
subsequently recalled from long term memory is the encoding specificity
hypothesis (Tulving and Osler, 1968 and Tulving, 1983). This states that if the
conditions that were present at encoding are present at retrieval, memory will be
11

enhanced. The hypothesis has been shown to hold true for a large range of
circumstances such as location, mood, presence of cues (Godden & Baddeley,
1980; Overton, 1985; Tulving, 1983). For example, information recall improves if
cues provided at encoding are also present at retrieval. This point was strongly
illustrated by Mantyla’s (1986) research where participants studied lists of six
hundred words and generated either one or three associated word cues to the
target words. Previous research has shown that the average recall rate, without any
cues at retrieval, would be about 5-7% of 600 words. At retrieval, subjects were
shown the cue words that they had generated: in the three cue group, recall for the
600 word list averaged at 90%; while in the one cue group, recall stood at 62%.

Another theory explaining the influences upon long term memory recall is the
‘levels of processing’ theory by Craik and Lockhart (1972). Their argument
suggested that when information was encoded, the more time spent thinking about
the meaning of the information and its semantic relationship to previously learnt
knowledge, the more likely information was to be recalled. Semantically driven
processing was labelled ‘deep’ processing, while processing which focussed on
superficial characteristics of the information, such as the number of words starting
with capital letter, was labelled as shallow processing. Deeper processing
increased the likelihood of information recall because it produced a stronger
memory trace. The theory has been made more substantial in subsequent years
through the inclusion of other factors which influence long term memory recall such
as the elaboration of the processing and the distinctiveness of the information to be
remembered.

Apart from the relative permanence of long term memory storage, the

other

remarkable feature of this system is its seemingly unlimited capacity to store
information (Newell & Simon, 1972). Evidence of the storage potential of the
system came from research in the field of problem solving, an area previously

12

regarded as having little relationship with long term memory. De Groot’s (1966)
results suggested that the only factor differentiating more and less expert chess
players was their ability to accurately recall realistic chess board configurations. de
Groot concluded chess expertise was developed through an ability to recognise
most of the configurations encountered in a typical game, consequently allowing
the player to plan the best move associated with each particular configuration. Thus
from this finding, de Groot (1966) hypothesised that expertise was a function of the
amount of prior knowledge of the subject area stored in long term memory. Simon
and Gilmartin (1973) built a computer model to reflect this process and were able
to estimate that a chess Grand master holds between 10 000 and 100 000 chess
board configurations in their long term memory. Research in many other subject
domains supports de Groot’s hypothesis that long term memory plays a critical role
in expertise (Egan & Schwartz, 1979; Jefferies, Turner, Polson, & Atwood, 1981;
Sweller and Cooper, 1985).

1.1.5.1

Types of knowledge stored in long term memory

Long term memory has a vast storage capacity; in addition, it is able to store many
different types of knowledge. Distinctions have been drawn between declarative
and procedural knowledge; semantic and episodic knowledge and implicit and
explicit knowledge. Anderson (1983) drew a distinction between declarative
knowledge

and

procedural

knowledge. Declarative

knowledge

is

factual

information such as ‘blue and yellow are primary colours that when mixed together
form the colour green’. Procedural knowledge is the knowledge that tells you how
to perform certain activities; for example, what proportion of blue and yellow oil paint
is required to mix an emerald shade of green. Tulving (1972) has made a
distinction between episodic and semantic knowledge. Episodic knowledge is
information that has some autobiographical component because it is associated

13

with a certain time or place in your life while semantic knowledge is general factual
knowledge that has no binding ties. Thus, a person may have episodic knowledge
of the city of Jakarta through memories of a holiday spent there, while their
semantic knowledge may include the fact that Jakarta is the capital city of
Indonesia.

The final distinction in the type of knowledge stored by long term

memory is implicit and explicit memory as proposed by researchers such as
Jacoby and Witherspoon (1982). Explicit memory involves conscious recall of
information such as recognising words on a list from a previous list. Implicit
memory occurs when behaviour is affected by a previous experience despite a lack
of conscious memory for that experience. For instance, Jacoby and Witherspoon
(1982) concluded that participants spelt a homophonic word (such as read or reed)
in the less common way because in a previous unrelated task the less common
word had been referred to. The authors concluded that this previous exposure to
the less common word had unconsciously biased the participant’s subsequent
free interpretation of the homophonic word.

1.1.5.2

How knowledge is stored in long term memory

Long term memory has a vast capacity to store many types of information relatively
permanently. Many researchers have explored the mechanisms that allow this
storage. It is generally held that storage in long term memory is associative, where
items related in meaning are stored together (Ericsson and Kintsch, 1995). In
addition, a metaphor is commonly drawn between knowledge storage and the
chemistry of matter. The most basic units, atoms, combine together in various
ways to form increasingly complex molecules of knowledge (Eysenck and Keane,
1990). Despite this commonality of approach, many theories have been proposed
to explain how information storage is stored in long term memory.

14

One theory is of ‘atom’ like units called concepts. A concept is a mental structure
representing a meaningful category of information such as dog or colour. The
question of how knowledge is organised into concepts is also much debated. It
has been proposed it is rule governed (Bruner, Goodnow and Austin, 1956),
dictated by the similarity of the current example to a prototype of the concept in
memory (Rosch, 1978) or verified by a probabilistic weighing up of defining and
characteristic attributes of the concept (Tversky, 1977). In fact, it may be the case
that categorisation of information into concepts is dependent upon all these
mechanisms, altering in response to the nature and difficultly of the knowledge to
be categorised (Medin, Wattenmaker, and Hampson, 1987).

Propositions are another way that knowledge can be represented. They represent
the relationship between concepts (Anderson, 1985) such as ‘the door is open’.
The concept of door has a relationship to the concept of open in this proposition.
Productions are if/then statements and represent a manner in which procedural
knowledge can be organised (Anderson, 1985). They state an action to be
performed if a certain set of conditions exist, for example, ‘IF the door is open and it
starts to rain, THEN shut the door’.

Schemata are another manner in which knowledge is hypothesised to be
organised. Schema theory has become a central concept to cognitive psychology
since the 1970s, although the concept was originally proposed much earlier by
Bartlett in 1932. Schemas are complex cognitive constructs that store knowledge
(Rumelhart and Ortony, 1977; Rumelhart, 1981) although it is proposed that they
are also involved in the cognitive processes of encoding and retrieval of information
(Rumelhart, 1984). Schema theory will be discussed in more depth in section 1.2
as it is central to this thesis.

15

1.1.6

Beyond the modal model of memory

Since the 1970s, ideas about memory have continued to evolve. The prominence of
a stage like approach to memory is decreasing as memory researchers move
towards network models of memory and consider connectionist models of
cognitive processing. Network or connectionist models of memory aim to simulate
the parallel processing humans seem to be able to undertake: in these models
processing occurs along many dimensions at the same time; in contrast, the
computer metaphor dictates serial processing (McCelland, Rumelhart and Hinton,
1986).

Despite these new directions in memory research, modern conceptualisations of
memory still include the stages of memory as has been discussed in this thesis;
for example, Anderson’s ACT* network model of memory (1983) has used these
distinctions. The model, however, emphasised the interrelations between the
memory systems. Thus, as a search of memory is undertaken, activation spreads
from working memory to units storing information related to the concept in long
term memory.

The distinction between the long term and short term stores is further blurred by
Ericsson and Kintsch’s (1995) proposal of a long term working memory. This
memory store has the processing characteristics of working memory but the
stability of storage of long term memory. Long term working memory can account
for expert performance which places large demands on working memory.

Despite recent advances in memory theory, for the purposes of this thesis it is
useful to understand the different stages of the modal model of memory. The key
features of each stage of memory: the limited capacity of working memory to
process and store information in comparison to the relatively unlimited capacity of
16

long term memory are salient to this thesis as are the theories of chunking and
schemas.

17

1.2

Schemas

1.2.1

Schemas: an overview

Human cognitive architecture circumvents the problem of an extremely limited
capacity working memory by constructing schemas. A schema is a cognitive
structure that organises elements of information categorically and stores them in
long term memory (Thorndyke and Hayes-Roth, 1977; Chi, Glaser & Rees, 1982;
Sweller, 1993). Schemas reduce the impact of working memory’s limited capacity
by shifting the burden of memory storage to the seemingly limitless storage
reservoir of long term memory. A single schema contains an organised network of
knowledge, ideas and processes related to a particular concept. Bartlett provided
an early conceptualisation of schema theory that forms the foundation of modern
work: “ ‘Schema’ refers to an active organisation of past reactions, or of past
experiences ... (these) operate, not simply as individual members coming one after
another, but as a unitary mass” (1932/1995: 201).

Schemas represent and organise knowledge in long term memory according to the
manner in which it is used since a schema represents knowledge from some or all
of a stimulus domain (Howard, 1987). Schemas are involved in all stages of
cognitive processing. They allow new information to be interpreted and encoded,
stored, organised and importantly retrieved back into consciousness (working
memory) in the event of the information being required. They also play a role in
determining goals, allocating memory resources and more generally facilitate the
flow of information through the information processing system (Rumelhart, 1980).
In fact, Rumelhart has stated that "schemata are truly the building blocks of

18

cognition. They are the fundamental elements upon which all information
processing depends" (1980: 33).

Schemas allow us to recognise information from different contexts and categorise
it accordingly. Consequently, the infinite variety of information captured by our
senses can be ignored, with the result that working memory capacity is not
overburdened (Sweller and Chandler, 1994). For example, we have a schema for
the letter ‘f’ which allows immediate recognition of it, irrespective of the countless
range of formats the letter can be presented in - f, f, f, f, F, F, F, f, f, f. The marks on
the page creating the letter f correspond to our previously acquired schema for this
letter, allowing us to recognise and assign meaning to it; the recognition process
does not tax working memory however, since the stimuli activates the vast amount
of knowledge we possess about the letter f in long term memory. Thorndyke and
Hayes-Roth (1977) have suggested that since schemas are an abstraction of
knowledge from multiple and diverse contexts they therefore possess structural
integrity, that is, they are independent of any particular instance of stimuli
represented by the schema. The functionality of schemas is being able to use
knowledge represented from one context in many contexts. In addition, the dynamic
nature of schemas results in constantly updated knowledge; Sweller (1994)
contended it is this ability to construct and modify schemas that underlies learning.

Schemas also reduce the processing burden on working memory because
interacting elements that would otherwise overwhelm working memory can be
incorporated in a schema that acts as a single element and imposes a minimal
working memory load. For example, algebraic schemas tell us that a/b = c can be
transformed into a = cb . Both equations and their transformation can be processed
easily in working memory by anyone who is competent at elementary algebra
despite the large number of elements. (Elements include relations between
symbols as well as the symbols themselves.) A higher-order schema tells us the

19

location of each symbol and the mathematical procedure required to transform the
first equation into the second. The presence of schemas allows pieces of new
information to be chunked together, thus, the otherwise difficult task of processing
all the interacting elements in working memory becomes a relatively simple task
because the elements are incorporated in a schema that itself acts as an element
and can be processed readily in working memory.

The availability of a certain schema in memory depends on a combination of
factors such as strength of the stored information, the degree of the match between
the incoming information and the schema as well as the recency and frequency of
previous activations (Thorndyke and Hayes-Roth, 1977). The details of each
particular representation of a schema are tied to the general schema or
representation of that knowledge through a ‘context-preserving relation’ (Thorndyke
and Hayes-Roth, 1977). This relation dictates that in order to remember a specific
detail from a specific occurrence of an event the general schema as well as the
specific schema is recalled.

20

1.2.2

The features of schemas

Rumelhart and Ortony (1977) have identified some key features of schemas. Firstly,
schemas are composed of variable slots; for example the schema of a car
includes wheels, body, doors, seats, a steering wheel and a windscreen. These
variables can be filled by a wide variety of values; for example a car body can vary
from a small, sleek, enclosed sports car to a large bodied utility with a cabin and
attached tray. Rumelhart (1980) used the analogy of a play to describe this
structural characteristic of schemas: a play is composed of different roles played by
a variety of actors just as schemas are composed of different variables that can be
filled by a variety of data or situations. An example of this analogy is the schema for
‘buy’. Rumelhart has suggested that our schema for ‘buy’ is composed of roles
such as buyer, seller, merchandise and money; these roles can be filled by a wide
range of people or situations without changing the essential nature of the schema.
It is the case, however, that a prescribed order or relationship between schema’s
variables does exist; for example, in the ‘buy’ schema, buyers will always be buying
from sellers regardless of any unique features of a specific ‘buy’ situation.

Secondly, Rumelhart and Ortony (1977) stated that schemas can embed within one
another; for example, wheels form part of our schema for car, but this concept itself
is formed by sub-schemas such as axle, hub, tyre, rim and so on. Thirdly, the level
of abstraction of the knowledge represented by schemas can vary widely: a car
schema is a concrete object, yet we have schemas for concepts such as
‘compassion’ or ‘creative’. And finally, Rumelhart and Ortony (1977) suggested that
schemas encompass all knowledge rather than just conceptual definition of
objects or situations. Thus, a schema contains both factual knowledge as well as
personal experience; additionally, a schema is flexible enough to allow for the
enormous range of stimuli that exist within a category. Rumelhart (1980) has
expanded upon this list of schema features by adding two points: he suggested
firstly that schemas
21

are active processors; and secondly their function is to evaluate whether the
activated schema can adequately account for the pattern of data under processing.

Information comprehension is one of the key functions of schemas. "To understand
something is to select a schema that provides a plausible account of it, and thus
allows us to assimilate it to something we know" (Howard, 1987: 39). When a
schema is selected, it is said to be instantiated, that is, a specific instance of the
schema is created. Schemas guide the organisation of incoming knowledge since
information appropriate to the various variable slots of the schema are actively
sought (Thorndyke and Hayes-Roth, 1977). The schema, when some or all of the
variable slots are filled, allows comprehension of the stimuli to occur. Rumelhart
(1980) viewed the interpretation and comprehension role of schemas as
analogous to parsers as they are a device that sorts through the stimuli in order to
verify if the information variables that constitute the schema are present. He also
suggested schemas are like procedures because an active process occurs where
the component parts of the schema are involved in determining whether the
schema itself can account for the stimuli it is seeking to explain.

If information to fill various slots in the schema is unavailable, the presence of this
feature is still able to be inferred because the slot is then filled by the default value
of the schema. Thus, when passing a restaurant and witnessing food arriving at a
table, one is able to understand this event because the presumption is made that
the food has been ordered, by the diners, from a menu. The default value of the
eating out schema is that food is ordered by diners. The default values of schemas
aid understanding and reduce the information processing burden of working
memory because information can be inferred from the generalised case. However,
the presence of default values may lead to errors; for example, it may be the fact the
these particular diners have chosen to partake of a degustation menu and thus the
chef, rather than the diners was responsible for ordering the food. Rumelhart
(1980) likened this characteristic of schemas to a theory: schemas, like theories,
22

attempt to describe the nature of things and allow humans to make predictions
about unobserved events.

The knowledge contained in schemas is induced or abstracted from many
experiences (Eysenck and Keane, 1990). Rumelhart and Norman (1978) and
Rumelhart (1980) further proposed that schemas evolved in three ways. Firstly,
accretion occurs when a new experience is recorded and added to the bank of
information held within the schema. Secondly, tuning occurs when the information
maintained in the schema is refined, elaborated or made more generally
applicable. Finally, restructuring of schemas involves the formation of new
schemas either through the copying and subsequent modification of an existing
schema or through the repetition of a certain spatial-temporal configuration of
stimuli. Thorndyke and Hayes-Roth (1977) investigated how memory was affected
by repeatedly using the same schema to remember different instances of a type of
information. Given the slot structure of a schema, repeated use of the schema,
within a certain time period, entails many pieces of information of the same type in
each slot. The results of this research indicated that as use of the single schema
increased, memory for shared information was strengthened, increasing its
accessibility in memory. However, the repeated use of the schema did cause
interference in learning because the experiment’s participants showed difficulty
retaining the information that was unique to each of the contexts. Furthermore, the
authors contended that as the discriminability between the learning contexts
increased, the interference to learning was reduced. In fact, Thorndyke and HayesRoth (1977) were able to correctly predict the facilitative and inhibitory effect that
repeated use of the same schema had on learning.
Therefore, the nature of schemas as described above allows them to play an active
role in such processes as perception, understanding, remembering, learning and
problem solving (Rumelhart, 1980). Moreover, through the knowledge that they
hold,

schemas

ultimately

guide

our

(Howard,1987).
23

learning,

actions

and

behaviour

24

1.2.3

Types of schemas

Mandler (1984) has suggested we possess three different types of schemas:
schemas for scenes, schemas for events and schemas for stories. Scenes involve
the physical or spatial relations of objects in space. For example, the schema for
kitchens involves the relations among typical objects found in that room such as
oven, sink and refrigerator, while a landscape includes hills that rise from the
ground and rivers that flow down stream (Mandler, 1984).

Events involve a sequence of events in time such as catching the bus, which
requires checking the time the desired bus leaves; getting to the bus stop;
signalling the bus driver; boarding the bus; and paying the fare. Events could also
be classified as scripts in the manner of Schank and Abelson (1977). Bower, Black
and Turner (1979) investigated Schank and Abelson’s proposal of a restaurant
eating script by collecting a list actions and sub-events involved in this topic. They
found considerable agreement in the actions that were nominated to be part of this
event. Rabinowitz and Mandler (1983) examined how event schemas influenced
memory and retention of information. Their experiment contained two groups: one
group received an organised sequence of phrases describing the actions involved
in various events (schema consistent information); for example, the phrases
making up the “going skiing” event schema was as follows: go to mountain; put on
down jacket; buy lift ticket; ski down slopes; drink hot chocolate. The other group
received the same phrases, yet the order of the topics the phrases belonged to
was random (information inconsistent to schema); for example, this group may
receive a phrase from the “going skiing” event followed by a phrase from the “going
to a baseball game” event schema and so on. The groups were compared for their
memory of the phrases; memory for the phrases was significantly increased if the
phrases had been presented in the schematically organised fashion.

25

Kintsch has proposed that a story schema is “a set of expectations about the
structure of the story” (1977: 40). Story schemas aid in understanding of stories of
cultural relevance because the story schema possesses slots for variables such
as setting, characters, episodes of the story such as beginning or end as well as
the goal of the story (Howard, 1987). Research has also indicated story schemas
benefit learning in an educational setting (Yates & Chandler, 1991; 1994); for
example, university level science students, when taught a schema for the
construction of typical journal articles, increased their reading speed, conceptual
understanding and ability to recall information from scientific papers (Samuels,
Termyson, Sax, Mulcahy, Schermer and Hajovy, 1988). Similar results were
recorded by Cook and Mayer (1988); they found recall and problem solving
performance significantly increased after college science students were taught six
common text structures underlying scientific text. These findings have been
repeated with other forms of expository text (Pinchus, Geller and Stover, 1986). The
successful results arising from teaching schemas for certain specific categories of
stories seem similar to the concept of a ‘problem’ schema: a schema that contains
the information on solution strategies for specific categories of problems (Hinsley,
Hayes and Simon, 1977; Chi, Feltovich and Glaser, 1981; Gick and Holyoak, 1983).
Indeed, Hinsley et al.’s (1977) research on the existence of schemas for types of
th
algebra word problems actually viewed these problems as a type of 20 century

story.

Evidence suggesting the existence of a problem schema is provided by the speed

1

2

and relative lack of information by which experts are able to categorise problems
(Hinsley et al., 1977; Chi et al., 1981). Hinsley et al. (1977) showed that even when
a substantial part of the words in a problem were nonsense words, schema

1

Lesgold, Rubinson, Feltovich, Glaser, Klopfer and Wang (1988) found that for expert radiologists faced with a
diagnostic task, a problem type schema was triggered within the first 2 seconds of viewing an X-ray.
2
Some subjects in Hinsley et al.'s (1977) experiment were able to categorise a problem as a certain type after
only reading the first phrase of the problem statement (less than 18% of the total problem statement). This was
also apparent in the verbal protocols taken from the expert in study 4 of Chi et al.'s (1981) work.

26

knowledge about a problem type was still used to aid comprehension and solution.
In order to assess the knowledge associated with the problem type labels expert
and novice physicists had given to categories of problems, Chi et al.’s (1981)
subjects were given three minutes to talk about the category label, the problems
included in that category and the solution methods involved in these problems.
Subsequent analysis of these data revealed that for physics experts, problem
category labels activated basic physics principles, procedural knowledge about
how to apply these principles for solution as well as conditions under which they
apply. This was not the case with more novice physicists. Expert subjects also had
access to IF - THEN specific problem solution action rules that did not appear to be
present in the novice’s problem category knowledge. It seems likely, given these
results that part of an expert’s problem solving success is the presence of detailed
problem schema, containing knowledge about the solution paths. Chi et al. (1981)
hypothesised that for expert problem solvers in a domain, the problem statement
leads to a partial cognitive representation of the problem, which activates a
problem schema. The schema, with its various information slots, then guides
further information gathering toward a solution path. Progressively, the schema
slots require information to be derived from the information given in the problem
statement rather than just this information itself. An algebraic representation of the
problem can then be developed and the problem solution achieved.

Gick and Holyoak (1983) have further developed the idea of a prob lem type
schema. These authors suggest specific problem schemas contain a network of
information about a certain type of problem such as problem goals and constraints
as well as procedural knowledge about the problem solution. For example, the
problem schema for algebra tells us that a/b = c is equivalent to a = cb and b =a/c, it
provides information about how to execute these transformations as well as the
knowledge that a,b ,c are symbols with the ability to be replaced by any numbers.
Problem schemas also fill in information not provided as well as distinguishing

27

between information that is necessary or unnecessary for problem solution (Low
and Over, 1992).

Other researchers have suggested a categorisation of schema types should also
include schemas for persons (Hastie, 1981; Anderson, 1980). A general person
schema has variable slots for things like motivations or personality traits and this is
instantiated when a personal relationship is developed. The instantiated schema
allows predictions about the behaviour of others to be made, resulting in the ability
to judge if the person is acting out of character (Anderson, 1980). Additionally, the
person schema can be instantiated to represent stereotypical groups of people
such as librarians or surfers.

28

1.2.4

Schemas & remembering

Bartlett (1932) published a large series of experiments that pioneered research
into the nature of remembering. Due to the predominance of Behaviourist thought
during this period in psychology (Mayer, 1992), in addition to Bartlett’s imprecisely
controlled and measured experimental techniques (Kinsch, 1995), the work did not
receive the attention it deserved. This situation changed after the 1970s when
cognitive psychology focused on schema theory. Bartlett’s (1932/1995) contribution
to memory research cannot be understated (Kinsch, 1995): he was the first to
3
identify in psychological terms how a person’s previous experience of the world

affected their understanding and memory of new events. In fact, Bartlett took the
view that “(R)emembering obviously involves determination by the past. The
influence of schemata is influence by the past” (1932/1995: 202). Bartlett also
pioneered the concept of memory as a reconstructive process, a notion that enjoys
widespread support today.

Bartlett’s most famous experiments used the method of serial reproduction and a
native American folk tale, The War of the Ghosts. The method of serial reproduction
is similar to the game of Chinese whispers: recollections of a story are passed
along a chain of people. Bartlett got British University students to read a story and
write out their recollection of the story after a delay. This version of the story was
handed to the next person to read and so on. Bartlett looked at the changes to the
story that resulted. He had deliberately chosen a piece of text that would be
culturally unfamiliar to the participants in his study as cultural experiences and
expectations form part of the schematic knowledge he was interested in exploring.

29

Bartlett found that the folk tale was transformed down the serial chain: it became
shorter and more coherent, more ordinary, more rational and culturally palatable
than the initial folk tale. For instance, supernatural elements of the tale such as the
mention of ghosts and the something b lack that had come out of one of the
warrior’s mouth were quickly conventionalised then expunged altogether. To
explain this Bartlett stated “any element of an imported culture which finds very little
background in the culture to which it comes must fail to be assimilated”
(1932/1995: 125). This proposal, that appropriate schemas must be available for
information to be retained, underlies learning and schema theory today (Mayer,
1992). Bartlett also found details from the original story were transformed, canoes
and paddling were conventionalised into the more familiar terms of b oats and
rowing. This example further clarifies the role of schemas in Bartlett’s notion of
reconstructive memory: memory does not store an exact duplicate of a stimuli,
rather it retains the gist or central meaning of stimuli. When required, the retained
information melds with a person’s previous experience, that is, the schema to
which the new learning was assimilated to; this schema then facilitates the
reconstruction of the memory. Previous knowledge therefore, exerts influence upon
the contents of our recollections (Mayer, 1992).

Memory for a stimuli is attached to a schema and it is the general knowledge and
default values stored in the schema that help to reconstruct a detailed memory.
Baggett (1975) has provided further evidence that it is the gist of the information that
is encoded rather than the exact details. In this experiment, participants viewed a
series of cartoon images which told a story; for example, Figure 1.2 tells the story of
a long haired man going to the barber shop and then leaving the shop with short
hair. The actual act of the haircut was not depicted, although this event

The philosopher, Kant, in 1787, had proposed that humans possess innate structures f or organising their

can be inferred from the sequence of images and in fact, this event forms the gist or

image of the haircut actually occurring was included (see Figure 1.2). Immediately
after the study, participants were able to recognise that they had not seen this
image; at a post test a week later however, the majority claimed they had seen this
image. These results suggest that over time, the distinction between the actual
event and the inferences drawn at the point of encoding blur. The meaning behind
the images was retained and when required, the haircut schema was reactivated
and it was this schema that filled in the details of the memory and shaped what
was recalled. Further evidence that memory retains a general impression of
meaning rather than detailed accounts has been reported by Bransford and Franks
(1971), Bransford, Barclay and Franks (1972) and Johnson, Bransford and
Solomon (1973). Each of these studies used language rather than images as the
basis for their research, adding to the robustness of this proposal.

Bartlett

further

as a dumb b ell for the other group. When the figures were reproduced by the
participants from memory, it was found that the label attached to each figure clearly
influenced the manner in which it was recollected (see Figure 1.3). Thus, schemas
influence how information is recalled; there is also much evidence to suggest that
schemas greatly influence how information is interpreted, comprehended and
therefore remembered.

Carmichael

research.

Pichert and Anderson’s (1977) research provided a strong illustration of the
capacity of schemas to influence what information is attended to and
therefore, what information is retained in memory. Students were asked to
read a passage describing a house from the perspective of either a potential
home buyer or a burglar. The researchers felt these instructions would
activate different schemas hence influencing the information recalled. The
results supported this hypothesis since the home buyer group remembered
significantly more home information such as the number of bedrooms and the
presence of a leaky roof while the burglar group recalled other details such as
the number and locations of bikes and jewellery. Given the capacity limitations
of working memory, the results of Pichert and Anderson (1977) point to
another function of schemas: schemas act as a guide, directing attention to
the most relevant information.

35

1.2.5

The

role

of

schemas

in

interpretation

and

comprehension
Anderson, Reynolds, Schallert and Goetz (1977) investigated how schemas can
affect the interpretation of information and hence memory and learning. The
authors suggest that a piece of text has no ab solute meaning because the
meaning of the text will be influenced by the schemas that the person reading the
text holds. The type of schemas and the complexity of those schemas are positively
4

related to an individual’s expertise in a given domain . The authors further predicted
that text which is ambiguous will be interpreted by the learner’s higher order or
more complex schemas. Ambiguous words and phrases can have several
meanings: within a passage of text it is possible to deduce their meaning from the
context of the surrounding sentence or sentences. In order to test these
hypotheses, Anderson et al. (1977) constructed two passages of text which could
have two sensible interpretations. One passage described a prisoner planning his
escape, yet it could also be interpreted as a passage describing a wrestling match
by those who understood the terminology and nuances of wrestling. The second
passage described a group of friends playing cards but could also be understood
as a group of friends playing musical instruments together. Two groups of
participants were recruited: music experts and wrestling experts. The results, from
both recall and recognition tests, supported the authors’ hypotheses. Wrestling
experts interpreted the first passage to be about wrestling, the second about cards;
in contrast, the music experts interpreted the first passage to be about a prison
escape and the second to be about music playing. Interestingly, the researchers
also found that even when explicitly asked, less than twenty percent of all the
participants had any awareness that the passage could be interpreted differently:
thus generally, the activated schemas were so dominant that other possibilities

4

See the next section of this thesis, section 1.4, for further details on expertise and schema development.

36

were not even entertained. Howard (1987) contended the difficulty in dislodging the
interpretation of an activated schema is one of the few drawbacks schemas have
for information processing. Overall however, these results elucidate the active role
schemas play in the interpretation, understanding and learning of information.

Bransford and Johnson (1972) provided some interesting evidence about the
influence of schemas upon learning and understanding. Their thesis stated that
comprehension of text is achieved through the joint product of the input (sentences
of the text) as well as prior knowledge. This hypothesis was tested by manipulating
the prior knowledge of the participants, in regard to the context of the text. Figure
1.4 depicts the scene ruminated upon in the passage of text the participants of the
experiment were asked to study (also see Figure 1.4). This figure therefore
provided contextual information relevant for understanding the passage of text and
thus the presence or absence of Figure 1.4

allowed prior knowledge to be

manipulated. One group of participants, the context b efore group, received the
figure before reading the passage; another, the context after group, received it after
reading the passage; another group, the partial context group, received it in a
partial form (the elements of the figure were rearranged in a manner that did not
explain the text passage) and the final group of participants, the no context group,
did not view the figure at all. In comparison to all the other groups, the context
b efore group showed significantly better comprehension and recall of the text. This
result was achieved despite all participants being familiar with the words and
lexical structure used in the passage. The figure provided the context b efore group
with a schema which allowed them to understand and remember the passage
more effectively. The poor results of the context after group suggest that it is not
merely contextual knowledge itself which improves performance but contextual
knowledge at the time of learning that is vital for comprehension and thus
recollection. Rumelhart (1980) suggested a failure of comprehension occurs when
a schema has not been activated because either a person cannot locate or cannot
construct an appropriate schema. This explanation seems valid when applied to
37

Bransford and Johnson’s (1972) experiment. The context after, partial context and
no context groups all struggled to understand and remember the text passage, yet
the group that was provided with an appropriate schema, the context b efore group,
were able to perform these activities.

38

If the materials popped, the sound wouldn’t be able to carry since everything would be to far
away from the correct floor. A closed window would also prevent the sound from carrying, since
most buildings tend to be well insulated. Since the whole operation depends on a steady flow of
electricity, a break in the middle of the wire would also cause problems. Of course, the fellow
could shout, but the human voice is not loud enough to carry that far. An additional problem is
that a string could break on the instrument. Then there could be no accompaniment to the
message. It is clear the best solution would involve less distance. Then there would be fewer
potential problems. With face to face contact, the least number of things could go wrong.

Figure 1.4: The text passage and the figure used to manipulate the presence or absence
of context in an experiment from Bransford and Johnson (1972).

39

Bransford and Johnson (1972) replicated and extended upon the previous results
using different textual passages. These passages described a series of
commonplace procedures such as washing clothes or making and flying a kite; the
action itself however, was not named within the passage. Context was manipulated
through the presence or absence of a descriptive title to the text passages. Three
experimental groups with different levels of prior knowledge were compared in this
series of experiments: the context b efore, context after and no context groups. In a
similar manner to the previous experiment, the context b efore group averaged
significantly greater comprehension and recall scores than the other groups.
These experiments illustrate the profound impact prior knowledge or context can
have on learning: the activation of schematic knowledge aids comprehension of
information at the time of learning and thus increases later recollection of it.

40

1.2.6

Schemas: a conclusion

Together, the studies discussed in this section reveal the enormous role schemas
play in information processing. Humans are not passive learners; they are active
learners, whose prior knowledge influences their perception, learning and
recollection of knowledge. Schemas not only provide the structure for long term
memory but form the basis of our intellectual capacity (de Groot, 1966; Sweller and
Cooper, 1985). This notion will be explored further in the next section.

41

1.3

Problem solving

1.3.1

Parts of a problem

Despite differences in terminology, cognitive psychologists have identified the main
parts of a problem to be:
• Initial state – the problem begins with certain givens such as information on
values, objects or conditions;
• Goal – the desired state to be achieved upon successful completion of the
problem;
• Operators – objects or concepts that can be manipulated to reach solution;
• Constraints on operators – include those factors in the problem that restrict
the use of the operators in the problem (Mayer, 1992; Brunning, Schraw
and Ronning, 1990).

A problem exists therefore when the initial state differs from the goal state. For
example, with the problem:

‘you are required to get to Perth from Sydney within 12 hours’ .

The initial state of this problem is Sydney because this is where the problem solver
begins from; the goal state is Perth, as this is the desired point of completion to
this dilemma; possible operators include means of transport from Sydney to Perth
such as plane, bus, car, bike, helicopter or boat; a constraint on the operators
exists because there is a time limit of less than 12 hours allowed for travelling
between the locations. Given the time constraint on this problem, the only possible
solution
42

is to travel by air either by plane or helicopter. All of the parts of the problem,
together with the possible paths to solution between the initial state and the goal
state form the problem space (Newell and Simon, 1972).

1.3.1.1

Ill Defined and Well Defined Problems

The degree to which the parts of problem are defined varies greatly. Reitman
(1965) has identified two variables to classify problems types: the degree to which
the problem’s initial state is defined and the degree to which the problem’s goal
state is defined. Thus, problems can be categorised into one of four states:
• well defined initial and goal state, for example, ‘x= y+3, y=4, find x ;
5

• ill defined initial and goal state, for example, ‘create something b eautiful
6

using useful materials’ ;

• well defined initial state but ill defined goal state, for example, ‘using an oven
proof dish and a gas oven comb ine a tomato, an onion and garlic to make
7

a good meal’ ;

• ill defined initial state with well defined goal state, for example, ‘explain the
8

factors most responsib le for glob al warming ’.

5

Both the initial state (x= y+3, y=4) and the goal state (find x) are clearly defined, with only one possible
interpretation.
6
The initial state (useful materials) and the goal state (something beautiful) are both vague, subjective terms
open to multiple interpretations.
7
The initial state (an oven proof dish, a gas oven, a tomato, an onion and garlic) is clearly defined yet the goal
state (a good meal) is vague and subjective.
8
The goal state (global warming) is clearly defined yet the initial state (responsible factors) is vague and allows
multiple interpretations.

43

Although simplistic, this categorisation does serve to highlight the real difference
that exists between problem types.

Most information processing and expertise research has concentrated upon well
defined problems and the manner in which humans attempt to solve them. In fact,
most problem solving research as concentrated upon transformation problems
(Greeno, 1978). Transformation problems have a well defined initial and goal state
and a set of operators, constrained by certain rules, which can be manipulated to
transform the problem through a set of intermediate problem states to reach
solution (Mayer, 1992; Ellis and Hunt, 1990). A wide variety of problems can be
classified as transformation problems such as the algebra example used to
illustrate the well defined problem type category mentioned above, physics or
chemistry problems as well as puzzle and maze type problems.

1.3.1.2

The processes of problem solving

Two important processes occur in problem solving: firstly, a representation of the
problem space needs to be generated and secondly, the solution process,
requiring a search through the problem space must be undertaken (Gick, 1986). A
representation of these two stages of problem solving are presented in Figure 1.5.
The problem solver must initially construct a representation of the problem; it is at
this point that Gick (1986) has drawn the distinction between schema driven
problem solving and other problem solving. If, during the representation phase the
appropriate problem schema is activated, then, the correct solution strategy can be
implemented. This would allow the solution to be reached unhindered. If no
schema for the problem exists or it has failed to be activated, then the problem
solver must search for a solution (perhaps using general problem solving
strategies); if this search is successful, the task has been completed. If the
solution
44

fails, the problem solver must retrace their steps: either by representing the
problem in a different manner or by using a different solution. Schema driven
problem solving and problem solving using general problem strategies will be
discussed later in this section.

Schema activated

Construct

Search for a

a

representation

Implement a SUCCEED
STOP

solution
No schema

solution
FAIL

activated

Figure 1.5: A representation of the problem solving process (adapted from Gick, 1986).

45

1.3.2

Problem solving research in the second half of the
twentieth century

In the 1980’s, the focus of problem solving research shifted from ‘semantically
impoverished’ areas of knowledge to ‘semantically rich’ areas (Chi, Glaser and
Rees, 1982): thus, the domain the stimulus problems of investigation have been
drawn from has shifted towards knowledge filled domains such as chess,
mathematics or physics. Before the mid 1970s, the favoured stimulus problem of
investigation was artificial puzzle type problems (Gick, 1986). Problems of this type
include transformation problems such as the water jug problem (Luchins, 1942),
the Tower of Hanoi problem (Newell and Simon, 1972) and the Missionaries and
Cannibals problem (Reed, Ernst and Banerji, 1974). These artificial problems were
employed because they provided an opportunity for the investigation of problem
solving skill development in areas to which subjects were unanimously naive.

1.3.2.1

The golden age of heuristics

The era before 1970 has been labelled as the golden age of heuristics (Perkins &
Salomon, 1989). Heuristics are rules of thumb or general problem solving
strategies such as breaking a problem into sub-problems; using diagrams to
represent a problem; the generate-test strategy; working backwards to the solution
or limiting the search space through such strategies as constructive search or
analogy (Wessells, 1982; Ellis and Hunt, 1990). These strategies do not guarantee
a solution but they often facilitate the problem solving process (Polya, 1957). It was
commonly thought, before the 1970s, that problem solving was a general ability
that was transferable across domains (Perkins & Salomon, 1989), hence general

46

problem solving strategies were embraced and promoted. Further support for this
cause was contributed by the field of computer science, as work in Artificial
Intelligence (AI) developed and was used to investigate problem solving; for
example, the Logic Theorist, an early AI program, modelled human problem solving
in the domain of symbolic logic. This program did not depend on the speed of its
processing ability but upon its use of heuristic strategies (Newell, Simon and
Shaw, 1965). In 1972, problem solving simulation improved with the development
of the General Prob lem Solver (GPS) computer program; this program could solve
problems in many domains using the general problem solving strategy of means
end analysis (Newell and Simon, 1972).

1.3.2.2

Means End Analysis

Means end analysis, although just one of several general problem solving
heuristics, is the most commonly used general problem solving strategy
(Wessells, 1982); it aims to reduce the difference between the initial state and the
goal state (Newell and Simon, 1972). The following steps apply:
• note the difference between the current state and the goal state;
• develop a subgoal to reduce this difference;
• select an operator to reduce this subgoal;
• repeat the process until the current state is equal to the goal state (Eysenck
and Keane, 1990).

To illustrate the use of means end analysis, the solution steps for the Tower of
Hanoi problem will be discussed. This problem involves three pegs and in its
simplest format, three disks of different sizes (see Figure 1.6). The task involves
moving the disks from peg A to peg C in the least number of moves. Two

47

constraints exist: only one disk can be moved at a time and a larger disk can never
be placed on top of a smaller disk.

Peg A

Peg B

Peg C

Figure 1.6: The initial state of the tower of Hanoi problem.

In order to solve this problem using means end analysis, the difference between
the current state and the goal state must be examined. Initially the disks are on Peg
A, while the goal is place them, in the same size order, on Peg C. Moving the
smallest disk to Peg C could reduce this difference, and as this move does not
violate the constraints on the operators this move is established as a subgoal and
carried out. Differences still exist so the process continues until these differences
are resolved. Researchers have found evidence not only for the existence of
subgoals but also for a series of minor subgoals attached to a major subgoal
(Egan and Greeno, 1974). For example, a major subgoal in the current problem
would be to get the largest disk onto Peg C. The move described previously in this
paragraph forms one of a sequence of subgoals designed to achieve this major
subgoal. Placing the middle disk on to Peg B, and the smallest disk on top of that
are also examples of the minor subgoals that may be attached to the major
subgoal of moving the largest disk onto Peg C. After all three of these subgoals
have been completed, the major subgoal can be achieved. Since only one subgoal
can be achieved at a time in means end analysis, subgoals must be stacked for

48

future moves in the order that they are obtainable (Newell and Simon, 1972; Mayer,
1992).

Means end analysis is an efficient problem solving strategy but it will be contended
later in this thesis that it may not be an efficient strategy for learning about problem
structure and solution paths (Sweller, Mawer, Ward, 1983). Humans often
interchange between several strategies when solving problems and this is where
AI programs such as the GPS deviate from replicating human performance since
they rigidly apply one strategy (Wessells, 1982). Furthermore, the application of
general problem solving strategies may not necessarily be a conscious decision, it
may merely be a label that can be applied post hoc by a researcher to the steps
undertaken in the problem solving process by the problem solver (Gick, 1986).

More effective methods of problem solving are used as knowledge is acquired in a
domain (Gick, 1986). For example, Anzai and Simon (1979) examined a single
subject complete a five disk version of the Tower of Hanoi repeatedly; they found
evidence for a shift from general problem solving strategies to domain specific
strategies that were influenced by a knowledge of the problem, such as the
sophisticated pyramid-subgoal strategy. Domain specific problem solving will be
discussed in more detail presently.

1.3.2.3

A shift towards domain specific knowledge as the basis
of problem solving ability

From the early 1970s, problem solving research shifted towards studying problem
solving behaviour in semantically rich areas or knowledge-rich domains, that is,
tasks that require hundreds perhaps thousands of hours of practise (Glaser & Chi,

49

1988). Part of this shift involved the realisation that learners’ prior knowledge
“rather than being a ‘nuisance’ factor to control, could itself be seen as a powerful
independent variable in research design” (Yates and Chandler, 1991:131). Expertsnovice differences in semantically rich domains were examined with the aim of
understanding the nature and acquisition of expertise (Green and Gilhooly, 1992).

Findings in heuristic research and artificial intelligence also contributed to this shift
in the research paradigm of problem solving. Firstly, further research in heuristics
seemed to indicate that students taught Polya's heuristics for mathematical
problem solving did not gain performance benefits:
(t)hey didn’t know what to do with the heuristics. They understood the
heuristics in broad terms but didn't seem to understand the mathematics
well enough to apply them in the rather complex and content sensitive way
required (Perkins & Salomon, 1989: 19).
In addition, research indicated that heuristic instruction when taught in a very
contextualised way (so that the general problem solving strategies were linked to
the domain knowledge) yielded a significant performance gains (Schoenfeld, 1979;
Schoenfeld & Herrmann, 1982). Secondly, AI programs such as the GPS, despite
their initial success on multiple puzzle type problems, performed weakly in complex
knowledge rich problem solving domains such as chess (Perkins & Salomon,
1989); in fact, it was found that humans could still outperform computers despite
their enormous search capabilities (Glaser & Chi, 1988). In contrast, programs
designed specifically for these knowledge areas proved to be successful (Rich,
1983).

Research into expertise suggested experts developed an enormous database of
knowledge related to their domain which allowed them to rapidly recognise
situations and patterns (de Groot, 1966; Chase & Simon; 1973a, b) as well as
appropriate rules and solution paths (Perkins & Salomon, 1989). The implication of

50

this research was that knowledge was organised into specialised structures or
schemas (Glaser & Chi, 1988). AI programs were developed mimicking this
approach: this signalled a shift to a knowledge based approach in AI rather than
the processing power approach followed previously (Posner, 1988). Programs
such as ACT (Anderson, 1983) and SOAR (Laird, Newell & Rosenbloom, 1987)
learnt from experience, chunking sequences of actions into procedures that
achieved a goal. A database of chunks could then develop, creating, in time, a
knowledge store that becomes the power behind the problem solving: thus,
“cognition is replaced by recognition” (Gabrys, Weiner and Lesgold, 1993: 131).
With programs such as SOAR, if the specific moves stored in its knowledge bank
fail, it will resort to general problem solving strategies (Laird, Newell &
Rosenbloom, 1987). The machine is therefore replicating the approach experts
seem to take in a situation where they are asked to solve unfamiliar problems
(Clement, 1991).

51

1.4

Expert-novice Differences

1.4.1

An introduction to expertise

The shift to the investigation of problem solving in semantically rich domains such
as chess, maths, physics and computer programming also heralded a shift
towards the study of experts and novices in these various domains. An expert
possesses a large body of relevant knowledge and procedural problem solving
skills. The proficiency of an expert, however, is limited to a defined domain (Green
and Gilhooly, 1992; Glaser and Chi, 1988; Chi, Glaser, Rees, 1982). In contrast to
an expert’s performance, a novice lacks speed, accuracy and efficiency in problem
