solving. Expertise, however, is a relative concept: a chess grandmaster is likely to
be more expert than an unranked but competent chess player, however this player
is relatively more expert than a beginner player. In addition, the expert chess player
is unlikely to show the same proficiency at chemistry or motor mechanics given the
domain specific nature of expertise (Green and Gilhooly, 1992). The concept of
problem solving as a general ability, capable of transference across domains has
been overtaken by the multitude of evidence from many domains that suggests
expertise is developed as domain specific knowledge is acquired.

It is now widely accepted in the field of psychology that expertise is heavily reliant on
the acquisition and formation of a large and interconnected body of knowledge that
has little or no application to other domains (Perkins & Salomon, 1989; Glaser &
Chi, 1988). An expert’s body of knowledge is hypothesised to exist in knowledge
units or schemas (previously discussed in section 1.3 of this thesis); these
schemas reside in long term memory. The expert’s possession of a detailed and
hierarchically organised network of knowledge stored in schemas (Reif, 1995;

52

Solway, Adelson and Erhlich, 1988; Larkin, McDermott, Simon and Simon, 1980a,b;
de Groot, 1966; Chase and Simon, 1973a,b) can explain the robust and
generalisable performance differences that have been found between expert and
novice problem solvers (Glaser and Chi, 1988). The following sections of this
thesis will look at each of the qualitative differences between experts and novices.

53

1.4.2

Memory of problem states

Early work to investigate the basis of expert performance centred on the domain of
chess, since this game provided a model task environment for the investigation of
problem solving (Charness, 1992). De Groot (1965, 1966) investigated the
difference between expert and novice chess players through the collection of verbal
‘think aloud’’ protocols as players choose moves during a real game. The players
represented various skill levels such as grandmaster and master level, expert
players and weekend or novice players. De Groot analysed many aspects of the
player’s style and thought processes in an attempt to uncover the basis of chess
skill. For example, he studied the number of moves considered before a move was
chosen, the degree to which the players searched the board and looked ahead for
potential moves as well as their use of heuristics relevant to chess. Although the
9

skill of the expert players was evident from their superior choice of moves , no
quantitative differences were evident in the play and thought patterns of the
differentially skilled players. In one investigation however, de Groot briefly exposed

10

the players to a chess board with 20-24 pieces configured to represent a point
11

midway through a real game . After they could no longer see the board the players
were asked to reproduce it. Large performance differences on this task were
evident between the skilled and less skilled players. Experts showed superior
performance in terms of the number of pieces they could replace as well as the
accuracy of this placement. Experts (Grandmasters and Masters) averaged over
90% accuracy, strong players around 50% and weekend players around 40%.
These figures were replicated over many experiments. To summarise de Groot’s
findings, the differences between chess experts and novices are that chess experts

9

As rated by independent experts.
For five seconds only.
11
Such as the twentieth move of a real chess master’s game.
10

54

make superior moves and they have a superior memory of real chess board
configurations.

Chase and Simon (1973a, 1973b) further explored the nature of chess expertise. A
chess master, a strong A grade player and a beginner were tested on their ability to
recall actual board configurations; the results were similar to de Groot’s: the
master’s recall was superior to the strong player and the strong player was
superior to the beginner. In fact, the master player took four trials to reproduce a
configuration with 100% accuracy, the strong player took seven trials while the
beginner player still had not achieved this level after 7 trials. However, in addition to
remembering real chess board configurations, the players in these experiments
were also asked to reproduce the pieces of randomly configured chess boards. If
the results for memory of real board configurations reflected the superior general
memory skills of chess experts, then the chess experts should excel on a memory
task

regardless

of the

meaningfulness

or

meaninglessness

of chess

configurations. However, the data collected challenged this view since no
significant difference in the number of pieces recalled was found between the
players of different skill levels. Players from all three skill levels were able to place
about 4 chess pieces, a level that corresponded the beginner player’s initial
performance on the real board configuration memory task. The authors concluded
that an expert’s superior memory ability is limited to meaningful information
concerning their domain; when a problem state is unfamiliar, an expert is reduced
to a level of memory performance similar to a novice.

On the basis of their results and those of de Groot (1965, 1966), Chase and Simon
contended (1973b) that memory is the determinant of chess skill. More specifically,
an expert player’s ability to retain and make sense of large patterns or chunks of
chess pieces in memory underlay their ability to search for and choose good
moves when playing chess. A chunk, in this instance was conceived as a group of
chess pieces configured in a pattern that was encountered frequently when
55

playing. Therefore, a chess expert, when viewing a board configuration of a real
game, was able to perceive and recognise these chunks allowing superior recall of
the board configurations at a later interval. When faced with a randomly configured
chess board, the skilled player’s memory superiority vanished because they
recognised few if any patterns on the board. The recognition of chunks is critical to
skilled performance because skill involves the ability to search for and evaluate
good moves. The ability to recognise more and larger patterns on a board allows
an expert player to access information about chess moves that may be
advantageous when a particular chunk or configuration of chunks are present on a
board (Chase and Simon, 1973b). Chunks were discussed earlier in this thesis
(section 1.1.4) and the notion raised by Chase and Simon in terms of chess, is
similar to this earlier discussion. Chunking involves the meaningful grouping of
information into a single entity; expert chess players in this case were grouping
together several chess pieces into a meaningful single configuration.

Chase and Simon (1973a) further defined what constituted a chunk in chess
through a perceptual experiment. Players, while able to view a board configuration,
placed pieces on their own board to replicate this configuration. The researchers
measured the length of each glance at the experimental board and how many
pieces were placed on the subject’s own board as a result. In addition, they
delineated chunks of pieces by setting a 2 seconds as the length of time between
the placement of pieces: that is, a piece was considered in the same chunk as the
previous pieces if it was placed within a 2 second period. The chess master spent
less time looking at the experimental board yet included more pieces in a single
chunk. Moreover, the expert remembered more chunks per board. This evidence
further suggested that an expert player was able to recognise the relationships
among the chess pieces faster.

The achievement of expertise is not effortless. In fact, it is estimated that a chess
master has spent between 10 000 - 20 000 hours looking at chess boards (Chase
56

& Simon, 1973a). A chess playing AI program, MAPP, developed by Simon and
Gilmartin (1973) was able to accurately simulate the expert-novice related
12
differences in recall found in human subjects . MAPP modelled the process of

expertise development in chess and thus made it possible to attempt an
estimation of amount of chess chunks stored in long term memory; this figure is
estimated at between 10 000 and 100 000 chess board configurations (Simon and
Gilmartin, 1973). Given this range, the authors contended a skilled player would
have built up a store of approximately 50 000 chess chunks (Simon and Gilmartin,
1973). This number is equivalent to the number of English words an average
person would recognise.

Chase and Simon (1973a,b) originally conceived an expert’s ability to group a
stimuli into less chunks containing more information, as a working memory
advantage. Evidence, also from the domain of chess, suggested the basis of the
superior memory skill was long term memory. Charness (1976) found that recall of
a chess board configuration was little affected by increasing the delay between the
display of the stimulus and its recall to 30 seconds, even if this delay involved an
interference task which used short term memory resources. In addition, Frey and
Adesman (1976) showed that subjects could recall configurations from one of two
boards they had studied, without prior knowledge of which one they would be
asked to recall. Recall on this task was only affected in a minor way by increasing
the display-recall delay to 30 seconds and requiring subjects to undertake an
interference task during the delay, in comparison to a group that only had to
remember a single board configuration.

Gobet and Simon (1996) have revised Chase and Simon’s (1973a,b) original
chunking hypothesis as the basis of chess expertise. In their multiple template
theory, the author’s postulated that some chunks evolve into complex long term

12

such as de Groot’s 1965, 1966 findings and Chase and Simon’s 1973a,b findings

57

memory retrieval templates or schemas whose slot structure can be filled with
various types of information such as the location of pieces (chunks), the opening
move a particular configuration is likely to have come from and potential moves
related to a position. Time taken to fill a slot in an existing template is rapid, as is
the time taken to access a template for recall. In contrast however, the time taken to
acquire these templates is lengthy.

The domain specific basis to expertise and the ability of experts to recall more
information was illustrated clearly by Chi (1978). University students were
compared to ten year old children on two memory tasks: digit span tasks and
memory of chess board configurations. On the digit span task, traditionally
accepted as a baseline measure of working memory capacity, the students could
recall more numbers than the children. On the chess task however, this difference
was reversed. The results can be explained by a difference in the expertise level of
the two groups: the children were all experienced chess players, while none of the
students played. The results of this experiment also clearly illustrate how
information stored in long term memory manifests in working memory advantages.

The superior ability of experts to recall patterns of information has been shown in
many domains apart from chess such as bridge (Charness, 1979); electrical
circuitry (Egan and Schwartz, 1979); algebra (Sweller and Cooper, 1985); computer
programming (Jeffries et al., 1981; McKeithen, Reitman, Reuter, and Hirtle, 1981;
Adelson, 1981; Soloway, Adelson, & Ehrlich, 1988); x-ray interpretation (Lesgold,
Rubinson, Feltovich, Glaser, Klopfer and Wang, 1988) and architectural plans (Akin,
1980). An expert’s superior memory skills for real information from a domain as
opposed to their novice equivalent performance for random information has also
been replicated in other domains such as computer language recall (McKeithen et
al., 1981); algebra (Sweller and Cooper, 1985) and electrical circuitry (Egan and
Schwartz, 1979).

58

With the research in other domains suggesting the validity of the existence of an
expert’s database of domain specific information in long term memory, the
simplicity of a chunk as defined by a time partition has also been challenged.
Reitman’s (1976) investigations into the game of GO indicated that a chunk could
involve complex, overlapping patterns. Egan & Schwartz (1979) found that as
expertise increased the patterns constituting chunks were increasingly abstract.
Evidence was found indicating the existence of higher order or conceptual category
chunks in architecture (Akin, 1980). Further evidence for the meaningfully organised
categorisation of knowledge held in long term memory comes from McKeithen et
al.’s (1981) study where expert programmers recalled important words from the
ALGOL W programming language in meaningful clusters. In addition, Soloway et
al. (1988) found expert programmers recall related programming steps as
subroutines.

The findings of de Groot (1965, 1966) and Chase and Simon (1973 a,b) had wider
implications for the field of psychology in terms of the long debated question over
the principal basis of human intelligence: nature or nurture. While the view that
problem solving skill was general in nature had fed into the argument that nature
was the dominant factor, the evidence from chess clearly challenged this by
showing that experts are made rather than born. Possession of domain specific
knowledge was clearly more important than potentially inheritable global factors
such as IQ (Yates & Chandler, 1991) or superior memory skill (Charness, 1992)
and this type of knowledge and problem solving skills were acquired through
thousands of hours of practise.

In conclusion, research into memory of problem states has suggested experts
consciously or unconsciously chunk information into semantically meaningful
units. Experts can hold more information in a single chunk leading to an effective
increase in the amount of information an expert can hold in working memory within
their domain. Yet it is not working memory capacity that defines expertise but the
59

large and highly organised body of knowledge stored in long term memory that
allows experts to recognise these chunks or meaningful configurations of
information in their domain. This ability leads to expert’s superior recall of real
problem states in their domain. Importantly, this ability also forms the basis of their
problem solving prowess as information on successful moves is activated upon
recognition of these chunks or patterns. Thus, expert’s performance does not rely
upon superior search skills or use of heuristics but upon the ability to recognise
patterns of information. This ability is just one of the several differences that has
been shown to exist between experts and novices. The literature also shows at
least two other differences: categorisation and solution strategies. These
differences will be discussed in the next sections of this thesis.

60

1.4.3

Categorisation techniques

Experts spend a great deal of time analysing a problem (Glaser and Chi, 1988).
Evidence from verbal protocols suggests that experts initially spend time trying to
represent and classify a problem whereas novices attempt to solve the problem
unknowns immediately. A problem representation is a cognitive construct
corresponding to the problem that is developed by the problem solver. Several
groups of researchers have investigated how experts and novices represented
problems and whether problem representation changes with expertise. A focus of
this work was to investigate whether problem solvers categorised problems as a
certain type of problem before they solved it. If this sequence of events was
established, it would indicate that problem solvers have stored knowledge about
different types of problems in their domain and furthermore, this knowledge could
be a major influence upon problem representation and solution.

Hinsley et al. (1977) asked a group of high school and college algebra students to
sort a variety of algebra problems according to their problem type. The participants
were able to successfully complete this task. Importantly, there appeared to be
clear agreement between participants about the categories of problem types.
These categories tended to be indicative of the solution equations required such
as area or prob ab ility. In a second experiment, the authors investigated the amount
of information required from a problem for a categorisation decision to be made.
The problem statement was segregated into parts such as noun phrases,
dependent clauses or sentences. On average, categorisation occurred after less
than 18% of the problem statement had been presented. The speed and relative
lack of information required for a categorisation decision to be made seemed to
indicate that a problem schema was invoked before problem representation and
solution could have occurred, suggesting that the problem schema facilitates these
processes.
61

Chi, Feltovich and Glaser (1981) investigated the hypothesis that problem
representation differed according to the degree of expertise held by a problem
solver. The researchers suggested that representation of a problem was due to the
knowledge a problem solver had of a particular type of problem, thus the
categorisation of a problem as a certain type may be a key difference in the
organisation of knowledge between experts and novices.

In one of Chi et al.’s (1981) studies, expert physicists (doctoral students) and
novice physicists (undergraduate students) were asked to sort 24 physics
problems on the basis of their solution. No differences were found in the gross
quantitative measures taken such as the number of categories or the ability to
13

replicate the sorting pattern . A significant difference did exist in the time taken to
sort the problems, with the expert group taking longer. The most marked difference
between the groups however, was the type of problems experts and novices
categorised together. Using a cluster analysis to establish which problems were
most frequently grouped together, the researchers observed that novice physicists
tended to categorise problems by surface problem features such as objects
referred to in the problem (i.e. a spring), literal physics terms mentioned in the
problem (i.e. friction) or the physical configuration described by the problem (i.e. a
block on an inclined plane). In contrast, the expert physicists seem to group
problems together because of the underlying physic principle that would be used in
solution such as Newton’s second law, F=ma. The authors labelled the expert’s
categorisation as deep and the novices categorisation as surface. An example of
both an expert and a novice problem category is shown in Figure 1.7. From the
14
expert problem category depicted in Figure 1.7, it is possible to see that the two

problems grouped together have different surface features since one involves a

13

This finding that the sorting strategies used by both groups could be replicated and the replication occurred
within a faster time frame suggested the sorting strategies used by both groups were meaningful (Chi et al,
1981).
14

6 of the 8 experts in this experiment grouped these problems together (Chi et al, 1981).

62

spring while the other involves a block on an inclined plane. Yet, both these
problems can be solved by the conservation of energy theorem and this is the label
experts gave to this group. In contrast, the novices grouped the block on an inclined
plane problem with another block on an inclined plane problem

15

(see Figure 1.7).

Subsequent verbal protocols confirmed this interpretation as the novices labelled
groups of problems with labels such as b locks on inclined planes and prob lems
that have something rotating.

15

7 of the 8 novices in this experiment grouped these problems together (Chi et al, 1981).

63

Figure 1.7: An example of an typical novice and expert problem categorisation and
explanation for this grouping (from Chi et al, 1981).

64

The expert and novice groups in Chi et al.’s (1981) experiment showed reasonable
consistency across groups in terms of the categories they developed to
differentiate problems. Importantly, there was also little overlap in the type of
categories used by the different groups of participants (Chi et al., 1981). The
differences in categorisation were also confirmed by a second study (Chi et al.,
1981) and by further studies (Chi, Glaser and Rees, 1982). In the second study of
Chi et al. (1981), problems were deliberately picked so that they contained a variety
of surface features for each underlying solution law. Similar categorisation
differences between the expert subject and the novice subject resulted: the novice
student sorted the problems according to the surface features while the expert, a
physics professor used physics principles. An intermediate subject, a physics
graduate student, seemed to fall between these two since he used physics
principle categories but misgrouped several problems. The authors suspected this
occurred as a result of the problem statement miscuing the correct solution
principle. In a related study, Hardiman, Dufresne and Mestre (1989) found that
novices who made a greater attempt to use solution principles in their problem
categorisation decisions were more capable at both problem solving and their
ability to categorise problems. The authors concluded that as expertise develops,
so does a student’s tendency and ability to categorise on the basis of deep
structures.

The difference in the principles by which experts and novices categorise problems
has been replicated in several domains. For example, Schoenfeld and Herrmann’s
(1982) research in the domain of mathematics indicated that novices sorted on
surface features of the problem such as objects in the problem like polynomials
while the experts based their categories on deep solution principles. The effect has
also been found with computer programmers categorising lines of code. Adelson
(1981) reported that novice programmers organised lines of computer code
according to the control word in the line of code such as IF or FOR whereas expert

65

programmers grouped code according to its membership of a particular program.
Thus, the experts in this experiment seemed to be using semantics as the basis of
their categorisation while the novices seemed to be using syntactics. This finding
illustrates, as do the results from other domains, that novices and experts have a
conceptual system organising their knowledge of a domain; it seems however, that
as knowledge increases in a domain, the framework organising the knowledge
changes to become more conceptually deep (Green and Gilhooly, 1992).

The way in which knowledge is organised may be according to the way in which it
is used rather than according to deep principles or surface features of problems.
Smith (1992) observed the categories into which genetics academics, genetics
counsellors and genetics undergraduate students organised genetic problems. In
this situation, both academics and counsellors can be seen as an expert group,
however, their specialisation in this domain can roughly be seen to vary on a theory
versus practical dimension. For genetics academic experts and genetics novices,
Smith’s findings resembled those of Chi et al. (1981): novices used superficial
aspects of the problem, such as the wording of the problem or the type of organism
involved to categorise the problems while experts organised them according to the
deep principles of the discipline. Genetic counsellors however, seemed to use a
mixture of both organisational strategies. Some deep principles such as the
method of inheritance formed a key part of their organisational strategy, yet
superficial problem variables such as the type of organism involved in the problem
16

were also deemed important . Smith suggested that the category groups of
genetics counsellors reflects in the way they typically use knowledge from the
domain in their professional lives. For genetics academics, their knowledge is
organised around the concepts and principles forming their pedagogy; problem
solving methodology is more important than correct answers and even then
problems are only an adjunct to their role of promoting the learning

16

Although the counsellors seem to focus on different superficial problem variables to the students (Smith,
1992).

66

of genetic concepts. For genetics counsellors however, problems are an integral
part of their professional role where the answer to the genetic problem itself is a
goal. Smith concluded that “genetic counsellors would be expected to focus on
knowns and unknowns (in the problem) because of the manner in which they are
required to apply their problem-solving skills in the course of their daily
professional lives” (1992: 199).
Several

other

studies

also question Chi and colleague’s

(1981, 1982)

interpretation of the organisation of problem solving knowledge. For instance,
Sweller et al. (1983) tracked the development of expertise of novice problem
solvers in a domain that had been artificially limited through the restriction of the
number of equations that could be manipulated to achieve problem solution. In this
study, it seemed that the participants did not organise knowledge according to the
broad solution principles that Chi and her colleagues (1981, 1982) have suggested
but rather on highly specific condition-action solution sequences. The authors
hypothesised that problem schemas form a hierarchy ranging from broad
principles down to specific solution procedures; expertise develops as schemas
are acquired and it is Sweller et al.’s (1983) contention that the schemas that are
most readily acquired are of the specific solution type. In addition, Wineburg (1991)
offers an alternative proposal for the organisation of expert knowledge. He
investigated the differences between experts and novices in the ill defined area of
history problem solving. He did not find evidence of a specific schema for the
specific battle that formed the content material of the experiment nor did he find
evidence for a more general American Revolutionary Battle schema. Instead,
Wineburg proposed that expertise lay in the ability of the historians to construct “a
context specific schema tailored to a specific event” (1991: 83). Wineburg also
contended the expert group did not show signs of automation. However,
Wineburg’s observations did suggest that the expert historians were able to go
beyond the face value of the information provided by the historical documents since
they took into account the author’s motivation in writing the document and the
circumstances that surrounded the generation of the
67

document. As a result, the expert group were judged as having a more
sophisticated understanding and interpretation of the historical event. In
comparison, the novice group seemed to view the texts merely as a vehicle for the
provision of factual information. Wineburg interpreted this difference as a result of
the ability to develop an elaborate schema of this event. However, another
explanation of the experts’ superior performance exists: the experts’ performance
could be driven by general and automatic critical approach and analysis of
information schemas that, regardless of the specific content of a historical
problem, guide the experts’ approach to problems in this domain. This explains
how they were able to develop a more sophisticated answer to the problems by the
same set of documents, in comparison to the novice group. This interpretation also
is aligned more closely to Chi and colleague’s (1981, 1982) position since the
critical analysis of information is an important principle underlying historical
explanation.

1.4.3.1

Problems, schemas and knowledge

The knowledge contained in problem schemas was investigated by Chi et al.
(1981). They hypothesised that the names experts and novices used to label their
problem categories were representative of knowledge units or schemas. This
schema knowledge was investigated by giving expert and novice physics subjects
a variety of problem type category labels

17

and asking them to talk for three minutes

on a topic, relating it to the problems involved in that category as well as the
solution methods required for problems of this type. Obvious knowledge
differences were evident in between the groups. Both expert and novice subjects
could describe the variables and structural features involved in certain topics.
However, the experts immediately focused upon the physics principles involved in

68

solving problems, solution procedures and conditions constraining the applicability
of the physics principles. Figure 1.8 represents a network depiction of the
knowledge verbalised by an expert and a novice participant on the category label
‘inclined planes’. Although the novice participant mentioned the physics principle, it
was not framed as an explicit solution procedure. In contrast, this knowledge
formed the core feature of the expert subject’s knowledge network and was greatly
elaborated upon. The authors further analysed the verbal protocols into production
rules. The majority of the expert’s production rules could be seen as explicit
solution action rules. This was not the case when the novices protocols were
analysed; in fact, it was suggested that one novice student “knew what problem
cues are relevant, but did not know what to do with them” (Chi et al., 1981: 18).

17

The list was developed from the responses of the both expert and novice subjects in previous experiments (Chi
et al, 1981).

69

EXPERT

NOVICE

Figure 1.8: A depiction of an expert and a novice knowledge about the problem category
label ‘inclined planes’ (from Chi et al., 1981).

70

The finding that an expert’s knowledge about a problem category is focussed
around solutions was further verified by the verbal protocols collected from experts
and novice students about their basic approach to solving specific problems (Chi et
al., 1981). Experts interpreted the b asic approach instruction as an opportunity to
elaborate upon the major physics principle they would use to solve the problem
and the steps they would use to do this. In contrast, novices “were unable to
produce any abstracted solution method” (Chi et al., 1981: 20). Furthermore, when
solving the problems, novices concentrated on surface features included in the
problem statement such as the presence of the word friction. In comparison,
experts identified conditions and states of the physical situation described in the
problem. Some of these states including second order features, that is features
derived from the problem givens, such as the presence of a b efore and after
situation in the problem. The authors concluded that experts broader domain
knowledge allowed them to understand more from a problem statement than just
the words. They were able to understand the implications of the words for problem
solution purposes (Chi et al., 1981).

Knowledge differences as a factor in expertise were also evident in the verbal
protocols Chi et al. (1982) collected from experts and novices asked to summarise
the contents of a textbook chapter they had read. Each group produced summaries
of a similar length and both mentioned the three physics laws that formed the
basis of the chapter, yet the physics experts clearly displayed a broader and deeper
knowledge about the topics. The differences in knowledge seem to reflect the
differences in categorisation of problems by problem solvers. For example, the
findings of Chi et al. (1981) showed problem solvers of three different levels of
expertise categorised problems differently. In addition, Schoenfeld and Herrmann’s
(1982) results illustrated that student’s categorisation of problems changes as
expertise in a specific domain is developed. These researchers asked novice
mathematicians to categorise maths problems; the results were similar to the

71

results for novices of Chi and her colleagues (1981, 1982). The students were then
split into two groups: one group spent 45 hours studying computer programming
while the other spent 45 hours studying mathematical problem solving using
problems similar to those used in the sorting task. After their training, the students
were asked to recategorise the same set of maths problems. The results indicated
that the only group to change their categorisation of the problems was the group
who had undertaken mathematical training. This group’s subsequent method of
categorisation was classified as deep by the researchers since it was based
around solution principles; it was also similar to the categories a group of
mathematics professors (experts) had sorted the problems into.

Methodology other than problem categorisation has been used to probe the
differences in the differential manner in which individuals at different levels of
expertise organise their knowledge. Freyhof, Gruber and Ziegler (1992) analysed
the differences in 3 level (superordinate, basic and elementary) hierarchical
categories that chess experts (chess masters) and novices (Class B players)
grouped the pieces of a real board configurations of chess into as they were able to
view the board. At each level of the hierarchy, experts had more pieces in their
categories than novices. This result increased as the typicality of the board position
increased. In addition, the categories novices developed for the basic level and the
superordinate level were very similar. In contrast, the experts grouped basic
categories together to form superordinate categories: experts therefore, had the
18
ability to meaningfully relate chess chunks . These results lead to the conclusion

that experts are able to find more connections between the pieces as well as
perceiving relationships between the chunks. The authors concluded that “(T)he
ability to perceive or to produce such (domain specific) connections between
chunks distinguishes experts from average players” (1992: 36) and this is what
also distinguishes the hierarchical organisation of knowledge in experts to novices.

72

1.4.3.2

Categorisation, problem representation and problem
schemas

Several ideas on the organisation of knowledge arise as a result of work in the
area of expert-novice differences in problem categorisation (Hinsley et al. 1977; Chi
et al., 1981; Adelson, 1981; Chi et al. 1982; Schoenfeld and Herrmann, 1982). This
area furthers research in chess by de Groot (1965, 1966) and Chase and Simon
(1973a,b) since the categorisation of problems as types requires that meaningful
patterns or problem states have to be recognised. Experts and novices tend to
categorise problems differently: experts on the deep physics principles that would
be used to solve the problems and novices on surface or literal features of the
problem statement. Thus experts are able to recognise and categorise pattern
types or problem states much more specifically. As a consequence, experts also
represent problems in their domain at a deeper (solution based) level than novices
(Glaser and Chi, 1988).

The labels given to categories of problems by experts and novices seem to
represent a schema containing the problem solver’s knowledge about that topic
(Chi et al., 1981). Categorising a problem as a certain type activates the body of
information relevant to that problem state. In experts, this knowledge seems to be
related to the principles, strategies and actions that would be used to solve
particular problem states. Novice problem solvers lack schemas for specific
problems, instead, literal objects in a problem or problem terms form the basis for
their schemas in a domain. These schemas contain some solution equations but
a specific action rule solution processes, such as those held by expert in the
domain, are absent. The activated problem schema guides the problem solving
process, because the information slots of the schema direct the information
collection

18

The basic level categories described in Freyhof et al’s (1992) work had many similarities to the chunks
identified in Chase and Simon’s (1973a,b) work.

73

process that allows the solution to the problem to be gained. The influence of a
problem schema in the problem solving process was illustrated by Hinsley et al.
(1977) in their final experiment. The researchers developed a problem that
included information that suggested membership of two possible problem type
categories, one of which was incorrect. The experiment’s participants were equally
divided in the category to which they allocated the problem. The problem category
chosen however, was integral to the problem solver’s subsequent treatment of the
problem in terms of the information that was attended to, the information regarded
as relevant and the type of errors made. This finding has been replicated in other
domains. For instance, Lesgold, Rubinson, Feltovich, Glaser, Klopfer and Wang
(1988) found that the early categorisation of a problem guided expert radiologists
subsequent diagnostic search of an X-ray film.

The schema activated by the process of problem categorisation is self-regulating
because if information cannot be extracted from the problem to fit its slots, the initial
categorisation decision may be rejected. For expert problem solvers in a domain,
the information slots of the schema require information given in the problem but
also information to be derived from the problem: thus, the schema contains
procedural knowledge about the information and manipulations required for
problem solution. Problem solving is therefore both bottom up and top down: the
initial choice of category activation is data driven, yet once this has occurred the
schema is

heavily involved in developing the problem solver’s problem

representation, their choice of solution path and the procedural knowledge that
allows the solution to be achieved (Chi et al., 1981). It appears it is the presence of
problem schemas containing both declarative and procedural knowledge about a
problem’s solution that allows an expert problem solver to work forward through a
problem (Larkin et al., 1980a,b; Larkin, 1979; Simon and Simon, 1978) since the
schemas guide the solution procedure undertaken. The difference in the solution
strategies of experts and novices will be discussed in the following section.

74

1.4.4

Problem solving strategies

Several groups of researchers extended the findings that experts have the ability to
recognise problem states in their domain (de Groot, 1965, 1966; Chase and
Simon, 1973a,b) by investigating the strategies experts and novices use to solve
problems. This work concentrated upon the problem solving behaviour of experts
and novices within the domain of physics in areas such as kinematics and
dynamics (Simon and Simon, 1978; Larkin, 1979; Larkin et al., 1980a,b; Larkin
1981). Methodology used in these investigations generally included the collection
of written work sheets and verbal protocols from participants. These sources of
evidence also formed the basis for the development of several computer programs
which mimicked the strategies experts and novices used to solve problems (Simon
and Simon, 1978; Larkin et al., 1980a). Two consistent findings have emerged from
these studies of expert and novice problem solving. Firstly, experts solve problems
19
faster than novices . Secondly, novices tend to work backwards from the unknown

problem goal to the quantities given in the problem while experts consistently
worked forward from the information given in the problem to the desired problem
goal (Simon and Simon, 1978; Larkin, 1979; Larkin et al., 1980a,b; Larkin 1981).

Generally, the starting point for novices when faced with physics problems, was the
selection of an equation containing a problem goal. The second step involved
searching the problem statement for the values of the other variables required by
the chosen solution equation. If the value of these variables remained unknown,
then they would be nominated as subgoals for the problem solver to identify in
pursuit of the goal. The problem solver would then choose another equation that
would provide values for some, or all, of the unknown variables in the previous

19

In Larkin et al (1980a) the expert solved kinematic problems 4 times as fast as the novice.

75

equation. These backward steps leading away from the problem goal would
continue until it was possible to solve the subgoals set by the problem solver,
using the values contained in the problem statement. Once this had occurred, the
novice was in a position to work forwards through the equation path they had
established. The novice therefore, worked from the unknowns required by the
problems towards the information given in the problem (Simon and Simon, 1978;
Larkin, 1979; Larkin et al., 1980a,b; Larkin 1981). Larkin et al. contended “(n)ovices
… seem to require goals and subgoals to direct their search (of the problem
space)” (1980a: 1338).

The following problem in kinematics:

A car uniformly accelerates for 89 seconds. It travels 756 metres. What is
20

the car’s final velocity, V, if average velocity, v, = 0.5V and s = vt? ;

contains two pieces of given information: s, the distance travelled and t, the length
of time of acceleration. To solve this problem, a novice would typically note that V,
the car’s final velocity, is the problem goal and this quantity can be obtained by
using the equation v=0.5V. Since the quantity of v is unknown but is necessary for
the calculation of V, v becomes a subgoal:

STEP 1:

Goal V:

STEP 2:

v is unknown:

v= 0.5V;

Sub goal v.

This is a backward working step because the novice is moving backwards from the
problem goal. The novice then looks for an equation containing v such as s=vt.

20

This problem has been adapted from Sweller et al (1983).

76

Since s and t are given in the question, v can be calculated and the novice starts to
work in a forward fashion:

STEP 3: Sub goal v:

STEP 4: Goal V:

s=vt ;

756 = (v) 89;

v=0.5V;

v = 8.5.

8.5= 0.5 (V); V = 17.

Larkin et al. (1980b) claim the novice’s strategy typifies a means end strategy. This
general problem solving heuristic (described previously in section 1.3.2.1)
establishes goals and subgoals in order to reduce the difference between the
given state and the goal state. The management of goals and subgoals places a
considerable burden on limited working memory, in some way accounting for the
longer problem completion time required by novices (Larkin et al., 1980a).

In contrast to a novice, an expert would tend to start by using an equation that
involved quantities given in the problem statement. He or she would work forwards
from the given information to the unknown by systematically calculating new
quantities from the given information that allowed the goal quantities to be defined
(Simon and Simon, 1978; Larkin, 1979; Larkin et al., 1980a,b; Larkin 1981). In
relation to the example problem outlined in the previous paragraph, an expert’s
problem solving strategy would typically differ from a novice’s because the expert
would choose an equation that uses quantities provided by the problem.

STEP 1:

s=vt;

756 = (v) 89;

v = 8.5

This is a forward working step because the expert has moved forwards from the
problem givens. Using the value calculated from this equation, the expert would
solve the problem for the unknown in less solution steps than the novice.

STEP 2:

v=0.5V;

8.5= 0.5 (V); V = 17.
77

The expert group did not take the initial backwards step, although they did use
identical equations to the novice group to solve the problems (Larkin, 1981).

Production models have been developed to replicate the different problem solving
strategies of experts and novices (Simon and Simon, 1978; Larkin et al., 1980a).
The key difference between the expert and novice problem solving models is that
the expert model produced answers for any equations for which independent
variables were given or established, while the novice model started by trying to
solve the problem for the desired variable and if the action was not possible they
created subgoals to find the value of unknown variables in the solution equation.

It has been observed that experts used a forward working strategy only on easy
problems (Larkin et al., 1980a). However, the validity of the distinction between
expert and novice solution strategies remains since although experts trialed
several different schemas as they attempted to solve harder problems, when the
correct schema was located, they then worked forwards to solution (Larkin, 1983).
This finding relates to Sweller et al.’s (1983) contention that as expertise builds so
does the number of schemas based around solutions to specific problem types
rather than general solution principles. Part of an expert’s strategy for solving
problems involves attempts to locate an appropriate schema which can then direct
the solution process.

The difference in strategy use by experts and novices is hypothesised to be
evidence of schemas. The evidence has suggested that experts possess large
quantities of domain specific knowledge stored in schemas. Part of an expert’s
schematic knowledge in a domain deals directly with typical problems as well as
the solutions to these problems. Therefore, it is rarely necessary for experts, when
faced with a typical problem from their domain, to go through the general search
processes novices rely on to solve problems (refer back to Figure 1.5) (Gick,

78

1986). A problem schema also allows the expert to construct a detailed physical
representation of the problem in memory (Simon and Simon, 1978). The problem
representation holds the information given in the problem statement and so makes
clear the steps to be taken to achieve the desired goal: thereby promoting a forward
working strategy. Sweller et al. have summarised this position: ”(b)ecause the
schema is based on problem givens, a forward working strategy is used” (1983:
640). As a result of their more rudimentary knowledge about a problem and it
relations, a novice’s representation of the problem is much less detailed than an
expert’s. A novice is therefore unable to work forwards and so uses means end
analysis as a guide to solution.

Although the initial findings for the difference in expert and novice solution
strategies were in the domain of physics, these findings have been replicated in
other skilled domains such as medicine. For example, Patel and Groen (1986)
observed that experts in the domain of cardiology making correct diagnoses used a
forward reasoning strategy. In comparison, those who made unsuccessful
diagnoses tended to use backward strategies. In addition, Sweller et al.’s (1983)
use of a longitudinal methodology to investigate the issue of expert-novice
differences in solution strategies is a different approach to the majority of research
in this area. They observed that as a participant’s expertise developed (through
problem solving practise), a significant shift towards a forward working strategy
occurred. Moreover, the number of moves the participants required to achieve
problem solution significantly decreased as expertise increased. This finding also
provided evidence of the participants forward working as the initial backward steps
are no longer required.

A variety of other evidence also points to the presence of problem solving schemas.
For example, the speed with which experts solve problems in comparison to
novices (Larkin et al., 1980a) suggests that experts have no need to search for
potential solution paths; rather, they merely enact them. Also, experts
79

and novices seem to differ in the amount they explicitly mention a formula equation.
Larkin et al. (1980a) observed that while their novice participant stated each
equation she was about to use and then substituted values into the equation, their
expert participant only mentioned the end result of the substitution. Similar results
have been reported by Simon and Simon (1978) while Sweller et al. (1983)
observed a significant decrease in this behaviour as their participant’s expertise
increased. It is proposed that experts have made the process of locating an
appropriate equation, substituting independent variables and solving the equation
for the dependent variables into a one step process (Larkin et al., 1980a). In
comparison, for novices this process is still composed of several independent
steps (Larkin et al., 1980a). This difference is explained as due to the expert’s
automation of key sections of the sequence (Larkin et al., 1980a) (automation is
discussed in section 1.4).

The difference in experts and novices solution process behaviour can also be
explained by the organisation of their stored knowledge. Larkin (1979) contended
that experts had stored information about problem solution in large functional units
while novices possessed just single, isolated units of knowledge. This hypothesis
was based upon the observation that novices would produce equations as single
units at a random rate while experts would generate equations in clusters. These
findings about explicit reference to equations and the manner in which equations
are clustered by experts and novices seemed to indicate that novices access
individual equations and then go through a step by step process of solving each
equation before then searching for an equation appropriate for the next step of
solution. On the other hand, experts are able to access a body of knowledge that
contains several equations, knowledge about the order the equations need to be
solved in as well as procedural knowledge about how to solve equations.
Therefore, the use of external memory aids by experts has become unnecessary.
The knowledge contained in an expert’s problem schema reduces the amount of
working an expert requires to reach solution.
80

Evidence from other problem solving research has also found evidence for the
active role problem schemas play in the solution process. Soloway, Adelson, &
Ehrlich (1988) hypothesised expert computer programmers have knowledge of
programming plans which are governed by the rules of programming discourse.
They suggests that a programming plan is a schema of stereotypical type of
program that guides a programmer’s expectation of that particular program type.
They designed programs that were typical and complied fully with the rules of
programming discourse (schema conforming) as well as programs that would
work yet violated the rules that typically govern programming conventions (schema
non-conforming). The problem solving task required participants to fill in a blank
line of code in different types of programs with the code that would best complete
the program. The results of the experiment suggested when a program was
atypical (schema non-conforming), expert programmers were reduced to a
performance level similar to novices in terms of problem solving. Atypical problems
forced the expert programmers to go back to bottom up novice strategies rather
than relying on stored knowledge of typical programs. In addition, further evidence
of schemas in the problem solving process was indicated by the finding that the
majority of incorrect responses made when solving atypical program problems
would have been correct responses to corresponding typical program (schema
conforming) problems. These results provided further evidence that schemas
guide problem solving responses.

Other research using non-standard problems to investigate the presence and role
of problem schemas has also been fruitful. For instance, Hinsley et al. (1977)
compared expert’s performance in solving standard and non standard algebra
word problems. The evidence suggested schemas were used when students
solved standard problems since the students were able to solve standard problem
in many less steps than non-standard problems. In results that have similarities to
Soloway et al.’s (1988) findings, it seemed that when problems were non-standard,

81

processing of the problem statement occurred line by line. The author’s have
suggested this is a default strategy that is used when a problem is difficult to
categorise as a type and thus suitable a schema is not available to aid solution.
Hinsley et al. (1977) also found evidence for the use of problem schemas to solve
nonsense problems. The participants in this experiment solved a problem more
easily when they were able to categorise it as a certain type (because of the
presence of an intact problem statement) despite the fact that the problem did not
make sense because it included nonsense words. In fact, one participant of the
experiment explained their approach to solving the nonsense problems this way:
What I was trying to do all the time was to try and fit it (the problem)
into some normal schema…..As soon as I could pop it into a
frame…..it was much easier to deal with” (Hinsley et al., 1977: 101).
This finding is reminiscent of Adelson’s (1981) work where expert programmers
recalled lines of randomly presented code in program type groups suggesting their
schemas for program types helped them to organise and thus remember the
21

random information more effectively .

Some research has investigated how experts solve problems when a problem
schema is not available. Clement’s (1991) work with physics experts suggested
that experts turn to general problem solving strategies when they are faced with
unfamiliar, unusual problems within their domain, for which straightforward
approaches have yielded no solution path. Strategies such as generating
analogies to familiar objects and the examination of extreme cases (parameters
set to zero or infinity) for certain variables as well as searching for misanalogies in
the analogy developed for the problem were undertaken. Although the strategies
utilised for problem solving were general, their use was not divorced from the
subject’s domain specific knowledge since their application was driven by the
fundamental

82

laws of physics. Gick (1986) also argues that an expert’s domain knowledge also
allows him or her to successfully use strategies, including general problem solving
strategies, that a novice uses infrequently or poorly.

To summarise, underlying experts’ success at problem solving is their possession
of large functional units of solution knowledge related to specific problems. This
knowledge guides problem solving performance, making it forward working; fast;
and less prone to errors. Therefore for novices to achieve problems solving
success, they need to be taught problem solving knowledge such as appropriate
equations, procedural knowledge about how to use them and the conditions under
which certain solution procedures are applicable (Smith, 1992).

21

Experts recall performance averaged 33% better than novices for the randomly presented information (Adelson,
1981).

83

1.4.5

Other differences between experts and novices in
problem solving

Quantitative evidence has been found to support several other differences between
expert and novice problem solvers. These findings relate back to the differences
between experts and novices discussed earlier in this section. For instance,
experts spent a more time analysing a problem qualitatively than novices (Chi et al.,
1982). Part of this time is used to categorise the problem as a certain type, a
process which aids in the problem solver’s mental representation of the problem.
Using their problem representation, experts also seemed to have a superior ability
to infer relations about the problem and additionally think about possible
constraints. For example, using ill-defined problems, Voss and Post (1988) found
almost 25% of their expert participants elaborated on the initial state of the problem
while only 1% novices did. The elaborations allowed the experts to identify possible
constraints of the problem resulting in a reduction in the problem search space.

In addition, evidence has suggested that experts have stronger self-monitoring
skills than novices since they are better at judging the difficulty of problems (Chi,
Glaser & Rees, 1982). This ability appeared to be related to experts’ superior ability
to categorise problems as certain types. The ability to make a judgement on the
difficulty of solving a problem also meant that experts are better at allocating their
time during problem solving. Experts also seem more able at judging the relevance
of problem information. In the domain of internal medicine, Groen & Patel (1988)
found that experts make inferences about a problem from relevant information
while novices base their inferences on less relevant material. The authors
suggested that an expert’s problem schema is responsible for filtering out
irrelevant information. It also appears that an expert’s ability to judge information
relevance is integral to their successful decision making in the problem solving

84

process and thus successful problem solving. Shanteau (1992) concludes an
expert’s superior decision making during problem solving is not due to the amount
of information an expert uses to make a decision but to the relevance of the
information he or she uses.

85

1.4.6

Summary of sections 1.3-1.4: Problem solving and
expert-novice differences

Humans face and solve a broad range of problems constantly throughout their
lives. Psychological and educational research into problem solving has tended to
focus on well defined problems. It was believed, before the 1970s, that problem
solving skill was a general ability which was useful for solving problems across
many domains. During this time in education, the teaching of heuristics or rules of
thumb for problem solving was heavily promoted. From the mid 1970s, a multitude
of evidence suggested that problem solving expertise was not a general ability. It
was, in fact, domain specific.

Problem solving research shifted during this period into the semantically rich
domains of chess, maths and science and the evidence suggested that the
superior skill of a problem solver was restricted to their single domain. Subsequent
research investigated the difference between experts and novices in specific
domains. Several findings emerged: experts possess better memory skills for real
problem states in their domain than novices; they are able to recognise and
categorise problems on the basis of their solution rather than their surface
features; and they work forward to solve problems, reducing the amount of steps
they require to solve a problem in comparison to novice problem solvers.
Cumulatively, this

evidence

points

to the existence of large, functional,

interconnected units of knowledge holding relevant declarative, strategic and
procedural knowledge about problems and their solution. These units of
knowledge are consistent with schema theory. Expertise can be understood to
develop as an expert acquires a hierarchy of problem based schemas in their
domain. Evidence suggests that expertise is also dependent upon the transfer of
schematic knowledge from controlled to

86

automatic processing. Automatic processing will be discussed in the following
section.

87

1.5

Automation

1.5.1

Automatic and controlled processing

The evidence presented in this thesis to this point has indicated the important role
schemas play in learning, the development of expertise and problem solving
prowess. Another factor has also been shown to play a critical role in these
processes: the automation of skill. It seems that with extensive practise, certain
processes become automatic, that is they demand few if any attentional and
processing resources (Schneider and Shiffrin, 1977; Shiffrin and Schneider, 1977).
Automation facilitates problem solving and the development of expertise because a
problem solver can progressively solve larger and more difficult problems since
basic problem solving skills became automatised. Attention can therefore be
focused simultaneously on newer or more complex tasks, despite the limited
capacity of the attentional resources and the working memory system (Shiffrin,
1988).

A distinction needs to be made at this point between automatic processes and
controlled or attentional processes. This distinction has been supported by the
history of psychological research into attention (Shiffrin, 1988); in fact, Shiffrin
argues that “(m)ost researchers and theorists agree that a distinction between
automatic and attentive processes is useful, necessary, and valid” (1988: 774).
Controlled processes are any processes that require deliberate thought (Sweller,
1993); new skills therefore, tend to require controlled processing. Since they
requires conscious attention, controlled processes use working memory capacity
and so are constrained by the capacity limitations of this system (Schneider and
Shiffrin, 1977; Shiffrin and Schneider, 1977). An example of a controlled process

88

would be trying to locate a particular street in an unfamiliar town, using a map.
Conscious attention is paid to each street and landmark and these are matched to
the map’s representation. This task consumes all attentional resources. On the
other hand, automatic processing is relatively effortless since it is largely
unconscious. It is driven by long term memory and processing occurs in parallel
and is not restricted by capacity limitations. An example of automatic processing is
finding the street you have lived in for many years from the shops that you frequently
visit. No attention is required to follow this route and in fact capacity is left over to
carry out other tasks such as holding a conversation or completing some addition
problems mentally to calculate the sum of your shopping purchases.

To reiterate, automatic processes typically develop only on highly practiced tasks
(Detweiler and Schneider, 1991; Schneider and Shiffrin, 1977; Shiffrin and
Schneider, 1977). Schneider and Shiffrin (1977) and Shiffrin and Schneider (1977)
showed that an adult’s ability to discriminate letters from numbers is automatic. In
fact, on average 2100 trials were required for a participant to discriminate between
letters and letters as fast as he or she could between letters and numbers. This
result also demonstrates that with sufficient practise, automatic processing can be
established with sufficient practice. Automatic processing is also much faster than
controlled processing. Schneider and Shiffrin’s (1977) results indicated that a
group that developed automatic processing skills on a task needed approximately
a fifth of the decision making time a group forced to use controlled processing
required to achieve 95% accuracy on a visual search task. Evidence from other
fields has also indicated the speed of experts using automatic processing. For
example in medicine, novice radiologists require more time than experts to detect
abnormalities in an X-ray (Christensen, Murry, Holland, Reynolds, Landy & Moore,
1981) and in fact, expert radiologists were able to detect abnormalities with 70%
accuracy from X-rays they saw for only one fifth of one second (Kundel and Nodine,
1975). The author’s conclusion in this case was that experts have automated
recognition procedures. Wiedenbeck (1985) reached the same
89

conclusion about experienced FORTRAN computer programmers. In comparison
22
to a group of much less experienced programmers , expert programmers were

25% faster and made 40% fewer errors when judging the grammatical correctness
of lines of FORTRAN code, a basic programming skill.

Another critical difference between automatic and controlled processing is the
ability of these processes to alter as circumstances change. Automatic processes
are extremely inflexible since they are so highly practiced (Detweiler and Schneider,
1991); while controlled processes prove to be much more flexible in regard to
changing circumstances. For example, Shiffrin and Schneider (1977) observed that
if a participant was using automatic processes to search for a target in a visual
search task, if circumstances such as the definition of a target or the type of stimuli
used as distracter variables altered, nearly 1000 trials were required to regain the
initial performance level of the participants. If participants had always used
controlled processing because of the constantly varied nature of their search task,
performance was effected to a much lesser degree and the search processes
were able to be modified much more quickly.

Some debate has occurred over the nature and roles of automatic and controlled
processing despite the wide acceptance of this distinction (Shiffrin, 1988). For
example, Shiffrin, Dumais and Schneider (1981) argue against the often mooted
belief that automation is a continuum. These authors contended that automatic and
controlled states of processing are qualitatively different. Logan (1988a,b)
proposed automation is a memory phenomenon rather than a processing
phenomenon. He proposed as practise increases so does the domain specific
knowledge stored about a stimulus. If a task is consistent, processing can rely on
stored responses to a stimulus, thus processing becomes automatic. Logan
summarised his position thus: “Automaticity is memory retrieval: Performance is

22

Mean programming experience was 11 000 hours as opposed to 200 hours (Wiedenbeck, 1985).

90

automatic when it is based on a single-step direct-access retrieval of past
solutions from memory” (1988a: 493). Although more explicit about the effects of
practise on developing automation, Logan’s contentions to not seem to differ
dramatically from the commonly held beliefs about automation.

The controversy in the area of automation has even lead Shiffrin (1988) to revise his
original theory of automation (Schneider and Shiffrin, 1977; Shiffrin and Schneider,
1977) to now include automatic processes and attentive mechanisms rather than
controlled processing. In essence, the distinction is similar to Shiffrin and
Schneider’s earlier model since automatic processes are innate or learned; are
not restricted by capacity limitations and can operate in parallel to other automatic
or attentive processes without interfering in the execution of these processes.
However, attentive processes are hindered by capacity limitations and interfere,
leading to their use in a serial manner.

91

1.5.2

Automation and problem solving

Automatic processing plays a major role in skilled problem solving performance
and the development of expertise. Kotovsky, Hayes and Simon (1985) developed
‘isomorphs’ of the Tower of Hanoi problem (discussed earlier in section 1.4.2.2.).
An isomorph is a version of a problem that has different surface features but has
the same underlying problem space in terms of the size of the problem space, the
number of branches the sequence of operations and the minimum solution path
length (Kotovsky, Hayes and Simon, 1985). Despite the underlying similarities in
the structure of the isomorphic problems, large differences in the time taken to
solve different versions of the problems had been observed. For instance, some
isomorphs took up to 16 times as long to solve (Kotovsky, Hayes and Simon,
1985). As these differences cannot be attributed to the problem structure, the
authors speculated they were due to the way the problem solver modelled or
thought about the problem. The results of their experiments provided evidence for
this contention. Various factors such as the ease of learning and applying problem
rules; the plausibility of problem solving rules, given real world knowledge; and
provision of a physical representation of a problem all were shown to affect the
difficulty of a problem.

Evidence was also found suggesting several other elements of problem solving
behaviour affected the difficulty of solving problem isomorphs. For example, the
authors found that explicit training on problem solving rules, even if this training
was removed from knowledge about the problem and its setting, facilitated
subsequent problem solving. The researchers microanalysed the participants’
problem solving moves and found that initially participants seem to make a
substantial number of exploratory moves. Following this behaviour, the problem
solver entered the final solution path and quickly and directly solved the goal.
Kotovsky et al. (1985) postulated that these initial moves are rule familiarisation
92

and this behaviour actually leads to rule automation. Automation of the rules of the
problem led to a reduction in working memory load imposed by the problem’s
variables such as information on the current state, the goal state, the rules and
testing for move legality. As a result of this reduction in working memory load, the
participant had the ability to plan ahead for moves and therefore the final solution
could be reached relatively quickly. Automation also accounts for the improved
problem solving performance that resulted from explicit rule training mentioned
earlier in this paragraph. Kotovsky et al. summarised their findings:
We have shown that the solution process depends on becoming
expert at utilising the problem rules to make moves, that the
automation of the rule using behaviour is a necessary precursor to
planning behaviour, and that a small amount of planning capability
enables a rapid solution” (1985: 290-291).
In a related finding, Cooper and Sweller (1987) in the domain of algebra, found that
schema acquisition occurs before rule automation. Rule automation was an
important step involved in problem solving transfer. Indeed without automation,
problem solving is likely to be awkward and error prone (Sweller, 1993).

To summarise, the automation of skills and problem solving rules with practice
frees

up attentional and working memory capacity for the simultaneous

consideration of other aspects of the problem solving process or the performance
of other tasks. Automation increases the speed and accuracy of performance and
is an essential element of learning and the development of skilled performance.

93

1.6

Summary of Chapter 1

To conclude, this chapter has discussed the fundamentals of human cognitive
architecture and information processing theory. Working memory is where
conscious processing occurs but is extremely limited in the amount of elements it
can process at any time. Long term memory is seemingly unhampered with
limitations and forms the basis of human intellectual prowess. Knowledge is
stored in long term memory in cognitive constructs called schemas. Schemas
develop through learning and hold vast amounts of information. They are
hierarchically organised and are involved in all aspects of cognitive processing
such as recognition, interpretation and problem solving.

Initially, research in problem solving postulated that problem solving was a general
ability. Evidence has now emerged to strongly suggest that the differences between
experts and novices is their domain specific knowledge about problems and
problem solutions stored in schemas. Experts can recognise and retain more and
larger problem states from their domain than novices, they represent and
categorise problems on their underlying solution principles and they work forward
to solution. All of these differences can be tied to an expert’s development of
domain specific schemas. Automation of problem solving skills is also critical to
problems solving expertise as it frees processing capacity to allow for more
complex tasks to be undertaken.

In summary, schema acquisition and automatic processing are the critical factors
involved in human learning, understanding and expertise development. Automated
schemas provide the structure for long term memory as well as allowing effortless
processing that bypasses the limitations of working memory.

94

In Chapter 2, a variety of techniques involving the design of educational instruction
will be discussed. These techniques have been developed as a result of cognitive
load theory (Sweller, 1999). The basis of this theory is the enhancement of learning
outcomes through the facilitation of the processes of schema acquisition and
schema automation.

95

Chapter 2

Cognitive load theory

2.1 An introduction to cognitive load theory
Cognitive load theory (Sweller, 1988; 1989; 1993; 1994; 1999; Sweller and
Chandler, 1991; 1994; Sweller, van Merrienboer and Paas, 1998; Chandler and
Sweller, 1991; 1992, 1996) has its foundations in the cognitive architecture
described in Chapter 1. It uses some aspects of human cognitive architecture and
of the structure of information to provide instructional designs that facilitate
understanding, learning and problem solving. The theory acknowledges the
limitations of working memory and the important role long term memory has as the
repository of intellectual skill. It asserts that the focus of instruction and, therefore,
instructional design should be the facilitation of schema acquisition and the
automation of those schemas. Cognitive load theory contends that the amount of
mental load placed on working memory by an instructional task will be the critical
factor in determining how effective learning has been. It further argues that many
common instruction techniques fail to acknowledge the fundamental features of
our cognitive architecture, and consequently tend to impose a heavy working
memory load. High working memory load has the effect of impeding learning. In
response to these issues, cognitive load theory has been used to generate a range
of alternative methods of instruction designed to enhance learning by making
make more efficient use of limited working memory. It is important to realise,
however, that cognitive load theory is not concerned with any particular
instructional design, rather its major concern is that the cognitive load imposed by
instruction on working memory is minimal. In particular, if working memory is high

96

because of the instructional format, cognitive load theory advocates the use of
alternative methods of instruction that reduce or ideally eliminate mental activities
that are extraneous to learning. In fact, Sweller contends:
an instructional design will be deficient to the extent that it
requires learners to engage in cognitive processes that are
irrelevant to schema acquisition and automation. Any such
cognitive processes will require working memory resources that
consequently are unavailable for schema acquisition and
automation and to that extent, the instructional design will be
inadequate (1999: 37).

2.2 Sources of cognitive load
Several factors are involved in learning instructions. Initially, Sweller (1993) and
Sweller and Chandler (1994) identified two separate sources of cognitive load:
intrinsic and extraneous cognitive load. The intrinsic cognitive load of instructional
material is generated by the intellectual complexity of the instructional material
itself (e.g. the difficulty involved in learning a particular physics theorem). The
extraneous cognitive load is determined by how the information is presented (e.g.
text format, diagrams or audio-visual). More recently, a third source of cognitive
load has been identified: germane cognitive load. Germane cognitive load is
related to the cognitive processing which is consciously directed toward the
construction of schemas (Sweller, van Merrienboer and Paas, 1998). Intrinsic,
extraneous and germane load together contribute to the total cognitive load
involved in a learning task. The factors that affect intrinsic cognitive load will first
be discussed.

97

2.2.1

Intrinsic cognitive load and element interactivity

Human cognitive architecture, as outlined in Chapter One, can be assumed to
have evolved in order to handle some critical characteristics of information. Human
cognitive architecture must mesh with the information structures it is required to
process. All information is composed of elements. An element is information that
can be processed by a particular learner as a single unit in working memory.
Elements include relations between symbols as well as the symbols themselves.
One aspect of the structure of information is the extent to which its various
elements interact. While all information will impose some degree of intrinsic
cognitive load, the degree to which information elements interact, rather than the
number of information elements present, is the key factor responsible for the
intrinsic cognitive load imposed (Sweller and Chandler, 1994; Sweller, 1993).
Some information can be understood and learned, individual element by individual
element because the elements do not interact. Learning a new vocabulary
provides an example. Each new vocabulary item can be learned without reference
to any of the other items. For instance, the Italian word for tab le can be assimilated
and learned independently of the word for chair. The information is, therefore, low
in element interactivity. It imposes a low intrinsic cognitive load since there is little
if any interaction between learning elements; as a result, only a limited number of
elements need to be processed in working memory at a given time. It may be
difficult to learn this type of information because there is a large number of words
to be learnt, however, since this information can be processed in a serial fashion,
the intrinsic load it imposes remains low.
In contrast, high element interactivity material consists of elements that cannot be
understood in isolation because they interact. We can learn vocabulary items

98

individually but we cannot learn grammatical syntax without considering several
vocabulary items and their relations: We can learn the names of various chemical
elements in isolation but we cannot learn to balance a chemical equation without
considering several elements and their relations simultaneously: We can learn the
names and perhaps even the functions of individual electrical components one at a
time but we cannot understand an electrical circuit consisting of thosecomponents
without simultaneously considering several components and their relations.
Understanding syntax, chemical equations or an electrical circuit are high element
interactivity endeavours for learners with little experience in these areas. As a
consequence, they impose a high, intrinsic cognitive load because manyelements
must be processed in working memory simultaneously. Difficult to understand
material is difficult precisely because it is high in element interactivity and imposes
a heavy load on working memory (Sweller and Chandler, 1994; Marcus, Cooper &
Sweller, 1996; Sweller, 1994; see also Halford, Wilson & Phillips, 1998, for a
similar formulation).
All information falls somewhere on a continuum of a small number of elements
with low interactivity to many elements with a high degree of interactivity. It is
possible to estimate the interactivity of material by counting the number of
elements of information learners will be required to simultaneously process
(Sweller and Chandler, 1994). This task, however, is made more difficult by
learners’ differing levels of prior experience; this issue will be discussed below
(section 2.2.1.2).

2.2.1.1

Research on element interactivity

Marcus, et al. (1996) investigated the effect of learning information in which the
information elements increasingly interacted. In this series of experiments, primary
school students were required to join resistors and connectors together to solve

99

problems in basic electronics. The researchers devised problems in which the
interactivity between a fixed number of information elements was varied. The
problems required the participants to construct a single series connection, a
multiple series connection and a parallel connection; the number of information
