elements requiring simultaneous processing in the parallel connection problem
was substantially higher than either the single or the multiple series connection
problem. The results indicated that as the interactivity of the problem was
increased so was the time required for solution. In addition, the researchers found
that instruction via diagram (a schema facilitating instructional technique) was of
most benefit for the higher element interactivity problems. Analysis of dual task
performance and subjective ratings of mental load also provided evidence to
support the authors’ conclusion as the degree of interactivity between the
elements increased so did the cognitive burden placed on working memory.
In related research, Halford, Maybery and Bain (1986) and Maybery, Bain and
Halford (1986) investigated the working memory load imposed by transitive
inference problems such as:
X is smaller than Y; Y is smaller than Z: which is the smallest?
The researchers found that working memory load was reduced if the two premises
were separated, however, in order to solve the problem, the premises must
interact. The data indicated that working memory load was highest when the
premises interacted and this was the point at which students had the most difficulty
solving the problem.

100

2.2.1.2

Element interactivity and expertise

Our cognitive architecture handles the problem of an excessively high working
memory load by constructing schemas 1. An expert is someone who through
extensive practice has acquired many schemas in a specific domain. The
acquisition of a schema allows the interacting elements of information that would
otherwise overwhelm the limited capacity of working memory to be incorporated.
The schema then acts as a single element and imposes a minimal working
memory load. Thus, relatively expert English speakers have a schema that tells
them that "the cat sat on the mat" is syntactically correct but "cat the on sat mat
the" is not. Algebraic schemas tell us that a/b = c can be transformed into a = cb .
Both equations and their transformation can be processed easily in working
memory by anyone who is competent at elementary algebra despite the large
number of elements. A higher-order schema tells us the location of each symbol
and the mathematical procedure required to transform the first equation into the
second. Once acquired, the otherwise difficult task of processing all the interacting
elements in working memory becomes a relatively simple task because the
elements are incorporated in a schema that itself acts as an element and can be
processed readily in working memory.
The presence of a schema or a network of schemas, therefore, has an enormous
impact upon the element interactivity and, hence, the intrinsic cognitive load
imposed by instructional material since “what constitutes an element does not
depend just on the nature of the material, it also depends on the expertise of the
learner” (Tindall-Ford, Chandler and Sweller, 1997: 260). The number of elements
and the degree to which they interact will vary greatly depending on the expertise
of the students involved; for example, a competent mechanical engineer required

23

For more information on schemas see section 1.2

101

to learn about the configuration and elements of a pneumatic circuit should be able
to process all of the elements that create a standard pneumatic circuit as a few
elements or even as a single entity. This processing can occur simultaneously in
working memory because of the mechanical engineer’s previously acquired
schemas. The presence of schemas has shifted much of the processing burden to
long term memory. In contrast, a relative novice (e.g., first year apprentice or
trainee) required to learn about the same pneumatic circuit would only be able to
hold and process a few of the circuit’s elements simultaneously in working memory
since he or she does not possess the requisite schemas and so is restrictedto the
limited processing capacity of working memory. Ausubel has concluded that “the
single most important factor influencing learning is what the learner alreadyknows”
(1968, vi).

2.2.2

Extraneous cognitive load

While intrinsic cognitive load is generated by the learning material itself,
extraneous cognitive load is generated by the instructional technique or the format
of the instructional material. A variety of methods can be used to present or format
instructions; examples of instructional techniques include written text, diagrams,
formal lectures and multimedia computer presentations. The extraneous load
imposed by each method of instruction varies. A central assumption of cognitive
load theory is that the most prominent consideration when designing instructions
should be the reduction of unnecessary extraneous working memory load(Sweller,
1993; 1994; 1999; Sweller and Chandler, 1994; Sweller, et al., 1998; Chandler and
Sweller, 1996). Cognitive load theory has identified many commonly used
instructional techniques that impose high extraneous load on learners. In response
to these findings, cognitive load researchers have generated several alternative
methods of instruction; these methods fulfil the same instructional needs as the
conventionally used techniques yet have been designed to make more efficient

102

use of limited working memory. Evidence from measures such as subjective rating
scales and dual task performance suggest that the alternative instructional
techniques developed from cognitive load theory impose much less extraneous
load and result in performance improvements (Cerpa, Chandler & Sweller, 1996;
Chandler and Sweller, 1996; Chandler & Sweller, 1991; Cooper & Sweller, 1987;
Jeung, Chandler and Sweller, 1997; Kalyuga, Chandler & Sweller, 1999; Kalyuga,
Chandler & Sweller, 1998; Mousavi, Low and Sweller, 1995; Owen and Sweller,
1985; Sweller and Chandler, 1994; Sweller & Cooper, 1985; Sweller, Mawer and
Ward, 1983; Tarmizi and Sweller, 1988; Ward and Sweller, 1990; Tindall-Ford et
al., 1997). These alternative instructional techniques will discussed in section 2.3.

2.2.3

Germane cognitive load

Germane cognitive load involves ensuring the learner is focused on processes that
are relevant to learning. This type of cognitive load should be taken into account
by instructional designers if the total cognitive load associated with the learning
task from other causes is not excessive. Learning benefits if the learner’s attention
is directed toward processes relevant to the construction of schemas, even if the
consequence of achieving this effect is an increase in the total cognitive load
imposed on working memory during learning (Sweller et al., 1998; Paas and van
Merrienboer, 1994). Some examples of increasing the germane cognitive load of
an instructional task are requiring learners to answer questions about an example
or finish the solution to an incomplete example. Tasks such as these ensure the
learner has properly processed the example before they are required to undertake
some further learning activity such as solving related problems. In addition,
increasing the interference imposed on the learner during training by presenting
random rather than blocks of similar solution problems can increase germane
cognitive load during training (van Merrienboer, Schuurman, de Croock & Paas, in
press). Total cognitive load may be increased by including tasks such as those

103

described above, however, the tasks ensure that the learner’s attention has been
appropriately engaged and have been shown to benefit subsequent performance
on similar and transfer problems (van Merrienboer, Schuurman, de Croock &
Paas, in press; Sweller et al., 1998; Paas and van Merrienboer, 1994).

2.2.4

Total cognitive load

The total cognitive load imposed on a learner’s working memory is a function of
intrinsic, extraneous and germane load. If total cognitive load exceeds the limited
capacity of working memory, schema acquisition and schema automation will be
impeded. Several experiments have shown that sometimes, despite the use of an
instructional technique that imposes a high extraneous load, learning remains
unaffected. In this situation the intrinsic load generated by the instructional material
itself is not sufficient to cause excessive total cognitive load.
When intrinsic load is high, research from a variety of domains indicates that the
manner in which instructions are formatted is critical to the processes of schema
acquisition and automation. Specifically, in this situation instructional techniques
that reduce extraneous load or increase the processing power of working memory
positively impact upon learning. When intrinsic load is low, the method of
instruction used is much less relevant (Cerpa et al., 1996; Chandler & Sweller,
2

1996; Marcus et al., 1996; Sweller & Chandler, 1994; Tindall-Ford et al., 1997) .
Increasing the germane cognitive load imposed by a learning task is similarly
detrimental for learning if total cognitive load from other sources is high; for
example, an increase in germane cognitive load was found to only be of benefit to
learning if the extraneous load imposed by the instructional material was reduced.
Paas and van Merrienboer (1994) compared a group that studied geometry

24

This research is discussed in greater detail in this chapter in section 2.8.

104

worked examples to a group that solved geometry problems under conditions of
high and low variability of practice. The learning benefits associated with
increasing germane cognitive load through the increase in practice variability
(Jelsma and van Merrienboer, 1989) only materialised when extraneous loadwas
low (the worked examples condition).

105

2.3 Instructional

techniques

generated

by

cognitive load theory

2.3.1

2.3.1.1

Goal free problems

Investigations of the effectiveness of means end analysis
as a problem solving strategy

From a cognitive load perspective, the effectiveness of a problem solving strategy
or an instructional technique is the degree to which this strategy or technique
facilitates schema acquisition and automation. This requires that extraneous
cognitive load is low and that a problem solver’s attention is duly directed at
features of a problem important for schema acquisition and automation (Sweller,
1999).
Means end analysis (see section 1.3.2.2) is a problem solving strategy commonly
used by novices. Problem solvers using this strategy are always focussed on the
goal since they are required to search for a legal move to reduce the difference
between the current state of the problem and the goal state. Once a move has
been undertaken, the problem solver can ignore the previous problem state and
simply focus on reducing the difference between the new problem state and the
goal (Gick, 1986). As a strategy, means end analysis is efficient at helping problem
solvers obtain the problem goal since it leads to behaviours such a reduction in
moves down dead end solution paths (Owen and Sweller, 1985); however, it has
been suggested that “one can achieve a solution to a problem using means end
analysis without ever remembering how that solution was achieved” (Gick, 1986:

106

109). These characteristics suggest that means end analysis is inefficient at
facilitating schema acquisition because problem solvers focus only on the goal not
on the sequence of problem states and their associated moves (Sweller et al.,
1983; Owen and Sweller, 1985). In fact, Sweller, Mawer and Ward state that:
The conditions that allow the goal to be obtained are not
necessarily those that will facilitate learning. When learning, it
may be important to consider how the problem state has been
obtained rather than the difference between the current problem
state and the goal" (1983: 648).
The well-developed problem solving schemas of experts contain explicit problem
solution information (Chi et al, 1981). To develop schemas containing this type of
information, the problem solver must be able to categorise problems as certain
types and associate certain solution moves with these categories of problem types
(Sweller, 1988). Means end analysis does not allow the problem solver to develop
an expert-like problem schema because of the heavy processing demands it
imposes on the problem solver. The problem solver is required to simultaneously
maintain information about the current problem state, the goal state, any subgoals
developed and the relations between these states. As well as maintaining this
information, the problem solver is also required to manipulate this informationand
search for an operator to reduce the difference between these various states
(Owen and Sweller, 1985). Given this working memory load and the previously
3

discussed limitations of the working memory system , the ability of the novice
problem solver to process information about problem states, their relationship to
the problem solving operators and fundamental solution principles is severely
impeded (Owen and Sweller, 1985; Sweller et al, 1983). Yet, for schema
acquisition to occur, the problem solver’s attention must be directed towards these

25

The limitations of the working memory system were discussed in depth in section 1.1.3.

107

parts of a problem rather than at the goal (Pierce, Duncan, Gholson, Ray, Kamhi,
1993; Gick, 1986)
Early research into what has become known as cognitive load theory reflected
these negative aspects of means end analysis. Using number transformation
puzzle problems, Sweller, Mawer and Howe (1982) and Mawer and Sweller (1982)
compared the performance of students using a means end strategy to students
who used a schema based strategy that directed attention to previous successful
moves in order to generate new ones. Sweller and colleagues found evidence that
means end analysis impeded learning since participants using this strategydid not
learn the solution rule or learn about problem structure despite often successfully
achieving problem solution. This contrasted to the other group, which had
developed a basic problem solution schema for these types of problems containing
information about the problem structure, the problem states and solution paths.

2.3.1.2

The concept of a goal free problem

Means end analysis requires the problem solver to focus on the problem goal and
reduce the difference between this state and their current state. A problem that
contains no specific goal would circumvent the use of this strategy (Sweller et al.,
1983). As a result, processing resources can then be directed toward the aspects
of the problem beneficial for the development of schemas and automation: namely,
problem types and their associated solution moves.
A typical problem includes a specific goal; for example:
In 18 seconds, a racing car can start from rest and travel 305.1 metres.
What speed will it reach?

108

In contrast, a goal free problem does not nominate a specific problem goal; for
example:
In 18 seconds, a racing car can start from rest and travel 305.1 metres.
Calculate the value of as many variables as you can.
(Adapted from Sweller et al., 1983: 643)
The format of a goal free problem, therefore, allows the problem solver to explore
the problem space, attending to individual problem states and their associated
move operators (Sweller, 1999) while simultaneously ensuring the cognitive load is
kept to a minimum (Ayres, 1993). These conditions should facilitate schema
acquisition and rule automation since a greater amount of cognitive resources are
available for these learning activities (Sweller, 1999).

2.3.1.3

Investigations of goal free problems and learning

Many studies have investigated the effect of goal free problems upon learning. For
instance, Sweller and Levine (1982) developed maze puzzle problems which
through the manipulation of knowledge about the goal allowed them to compare a
means end analysis group and a goal free group which was forced to generate
moves from an analysis of their previous and current moves. A simple alternation
rule (turn left, turn right) provided the solution to the maze, yet, the group who
were aware of the goal had not learnt this rule after 300 moves. The goal free
group learned this rule quickly indicating the presence of a schema.
Sweller et al. (1983) and Owens and Sweller (1985) expanded upon Sweller and
Levine’s research and demonstrated the significance of goal free problems for
education. Using physics kinematics problems, Sweller et al. (1983) found that

109

goal free problems facilitated the use of a forward working or expert problem
solving strategy (see section 1.3.3.4 for more information on forward working
strategies). The students in the goal free group solved the problems in fewer
moves and required a visual representation of the solution equation (problem
solving operator) less often than the group solving conventional goal problems.
These findings were replicated in the domain of circle geometry, even in a situation
where the goal free group calculated variables that were identical to those thegoal
group had to calculate in order to attain the goal (Experiment 4, Sweller et al.,
1983). In the domain of trigonometry, Owen and Sweller (1985) compared the
performance on a test of conventional problems of students trained on goal free
problems to students trained on conventional goal problems. The results indicated
that the goal free group solved more parts of the problem with fewer errors than
the group that had solved conventional problems in training. In further
experiments, the authors found firstly, that novice students developed greater
knowledge of fundamental trigonometric principles if they had trained on goal free
problems and secondly, that training on goal free problems improved ability to
solve a transfer problem. In summary, the authors contended:
over a very short acquisition period, no goal group subjects had
developed

superior

cognitive

representations

of

the

trigonometric ratios and had acquired the schemas to apply
them appropriately (Owen and Sweller, 1985: 278).
The finding that goal free problems benefit learning is robust since it has been
replicated and generalised in many domains; for example, in the domain of
geometry, Tarmizi and Sweller’s (1988) results indicated that the group trained on
goal free problems solved similar problems more rapidly and with fewer errors. In
addition, verbal protocols indicated this group was more likely to use a forward
working strategy than the group trained on conventional problems. Also in the
domain of geometry, Lim, Dixon and Moore (1996) compared students trained
using goal free problems to students trained using worked examples which

110

contained specific goals. Although hampered by a small sample size, the results of
this longitudinal study indicated that goal free problems were associated with
better test performance and that this performance benefit was maintained over
time.
Goal free problems have also been associated with some other learning benefits;
for instance, using two-stage problems that require the calculation of a subgoal
before the goal can be achieved, Ayres (1993) further investigated the benefits of
goal free problems for mathematics. A stage effect is commonly observed with
two-stage problems; this occurs when the number of errors on the subgoalstage is
greater than the number of errors on the goal stage. The stage effect occurs when
students are forced to used means end analysis because the problem is
unfamiliar. Ayres (1993) hypothesised that the stage effect would be reduced if
goal free problems were used for training on two-stage problems since they
eliminate the use of means end analysis. The results supported this hypothesis, as
a stage effect was not observed in the goal free group; the students in this group
also successfully completed more problems and negotiated more correct solution
paths. Pierce et al. (1993) have identified another learning benefit of goal free
problems. The researchers investigated analogical transfer using non-isomorphic
target problems. In order to solve analogous problems, a problem solver had to
notice the difference between the surface features of the problem in addition to the
underlying difference in the problem space. To solve the problem successfuly, the
problem solvers had to change their solution procedure to account for these
differences. Students in the goal free problem training condition, a condition that
emphasised problem space exploration, were more able to shift their solution
knowledge to the analogous problem. The authors regarded this as evidence of a
higher quality problem schema.
Evidence supporting the contention that solving goal free problems impose less
cognitive load than problem solving by means end analysis has also been

111

generated through computer modelling. Sweller (1988) developed a computer
driven production model that worked forward or backward depending on the
specificity of the problem goal. This system modelled a novice’s behaviour ongoal
specific and non goal specific problems. It was designed to compare problem
solving using means end analysis and the forward working problem solving
strategy enforced by goal free problems. Cognitive load could be estimated from
the number of production statements in the production system’s memory. The
model offered strong support for the reduced cognitive load imposed by goal free
problems: while means end analysis on a goal specific problem required a
minimum of 4 productions to be held simultaneously, the forward working strategy
generated by a goal free problem required only a single production to be held at
any one time. Sweller (1988) supported this computer model with empirical
research that investigated students solving geometry problems while performing a
secondary task. Secondary tasks are a commonly accepted measure of the mental
load associated with the primary task (Fisk and Schnieder, 1984). This experiment
showed that the group solving goal free problems performed significantly beter on
the secondary task: this result seemed to indicate that this task imposed a lower
cognitive load.

2.3.1.4

Shortcomings

Although goal free problems have clear benefits for learning, their use is
somewhat restricted by the nature of the domain. In some domains such as
algebra, a problem where a goal is not present to establish boundaries upon the
possible calculations can cause the creation of a virtually limitless problem space.
In this case, the problems solver is subjected to “fruitless calculations” (Ayres,
1993: 381) and a specific goal is a necessary limiting device (Sweller, 1999).

112

2.3.2

Worked examples

This thesis has previously discussed the fact that means end analysis interferes
with the acquisition of schemas and is therefore detrimental to learning. Goal free
problems were developed as a direct response to this fact. The experimental
results suggested that learning improvements resulted from this instructional
technique which, through the format of the problem itself, did not allow the use of a
means end strategy. If the success of goal free problems was indeed due to this
factor, as cognitive load theorists claim, it follows that any instructional method
which circumvents the use of means end analysis would also be beneficial for
learning.
A worked example consists of a problem paired with the steps to its solution (see
Figure 2.1 below for an example of a worked example). An instructional strategy
that requires students to review worked examples eliminates the need to use
means end analysis because, since the solution is provided, it is no longer
necessary to search for an operator to reduce the difference between the current
state and the goal state. In fact, the effect of presenting the problem solution is that
the student focuses on individual problem states, the problem solving moves
associated with them and the problem states resulting from these moves. Since
this information is the type of information contained in a problem solving schema
(Chi et al., 1981) it was hypothesised that reviewing worked examples would be
beneficial for activities such as schema acquisition and automation (Sweller &
Cooper, 1985; Cooper & Sweller, 1987). Moreover, these researchers contended
that since worked examples deconstruct a problem solution into its parts, working
memory load should be reduced which should further benefit learning. In this
situation, the worked example is acting as an external memory aid, allowing the
student to simply attend to a single problem state and its associated move. So
despite their surface dissimilarity, goal free problems and worked examples share

113

many similar features when they are viewed from a cognitive load perspective:
both instructional techniques reduce the use of means end analysis; both focus the
attention of the learner onto problem states and their associated moves and both
reduce the cognitive load associated with conventional problem solving techniques
(Sweller, 1999).

Problem: Calculate x when 10x = z +80 and z = 100.
Solution:

10x = (100) + 80
10x = 180
10x = 180
10
10
x = 18

Figure 2.1: An algebra worked example.

The effectiveness of studying worked examples for learning was investigated by
Sweller and Cooper (1985). In this series of experiments, the researchers
compared two groups of students with a limited knowledge of algebra on algebraic
transformation problems such as:
b (a+c) = d Express ‘a’ in terms of the other variables.
e
Exposure to worked examples was compared to the conventional strategy of
simply solving many problems (an activity that is likely to require novices to use
means end analysis). In the acquisition phase of the experiment, after a brief
introduction to this area of algebra, both groups were given a set of eight problems

114

to complete which contained 4 pairs of similar problems. The problems included in
the problem set were identical for both groups except that for the worked example
group the first problem of each group was a worked example (thus the solution
and the solution working was provided). In this group, the students were asked to
study the worked example before solving the subsequent problem of the problem
pair. In the conventional group, all eight problems from the problem set required
solving. It is noteworthy that the researchers did not compare a worked examples
only condition to a problem solving only condition. Although theoretically a worked
examples only condition should provide the most benefits for learning, the
researchers were concerned about the motivation of the students if they only
studied worked examples. The condition used in the experiment required students
to study a worked example and apply the same technique to a similar problem.
The design of this condition, therefore, provided the students with the motivation to
4

properly process the worked examples .
The results from Sweller and Cooper (1985) experiment indicated that the worked
example group required significantly less time than the problem solving group to
complete the acquisition phase. The acquisition phase was followed by a test
phase; it consisted of problems that were similar in structure to the problems used
in the acquisition phase. The worked example group completed the test problems
significantly faster than the problem-solving group and they made significantly
fewer errors. Thus, despite less time for learning, the students in the worked
example group were able to solve similar problems faster and with less errors than
a group that had more problem solving experience. These results seemed to
indicate that studying worked examples does facilitate the acquisition of schemas.

26

Paas and Van Merrienboer (1994) have reported the results of an experiment that compared a purely
worked examples group to a conventional problem solving group. Results similar to other studies where
worked examples are interspersed with problems were reported. The results indicated similar and transfer
problem solving performance was enhanced in the worked examples group.

115

In the experiment outlined above, the test phase used similar problems to the
acquisition phase. Sweller and Cooper (1985) also investigated the effect of
worked examples on ability to solve dissimilar or transfer problems. Studying
worked examples had no effect on the student’s ability to solve transfer problems:
the performance of the groups could not be differentiated.
Upon consideration of their previous research (Sweller and Cooper, 1985), Cooper
and Sweller (1987) hypothesised that although the worked examples had
facilitated schema acquisition as indicated by the results on similar test problems,
several aspects of the experimental procedure had hindered the ability of the
students to automate the problem solving schemas they had acquired. It was
contended that the automation of problem solving schemas was a key process
required for the transfer of knowledge since this resulted in a reduction in working
memory load. Indeed, Sweller proposed that “(a)utomation is likely to be a
necessary component of expertise. Understanding alone may be insufficient”
(1999:77). Cooper and Sweller (1987) identified factors such as the relatively
small number of worked examples and small amount of practice provided in the
acquisition phase combined with the large number of problem sub-types and
problem rules the students were required to learn about as factors from the
previous experimental procedure which had impeded the development of
automation. The authors hypothesised that by increasing the number of examples
and by reducing the number of problem solving rules required for solution,
improvements in schema automation and transfer problem performance would
occur. When schemas are not automated, a problem solver still has a relatively
high cognitive burden: although able to understand the problem, the problem
solver still needs to retrieve the appropriate mechanisms of the schema and to
consciously evaluate a problem solving move to ensure it is appropriate (Wardand
Sweller, 1990). If a problem schema is automated, however, extra cognitive
resources become available because resources are no longer required for schema
retrieval. These extra cognitive resources are presumed to be significant since

116

they provide the student with the ability to explore the new problem space and to
reflect on the differences between familiar problems and new, different problems
(Sweller, 1999).
Cooper and Sweller (1987) investigated the performance of a group of students
who studied worked examples (and solved a similar problem) in comparison to a
group who only solved problems on transfer problems. The procedure of the
experiment was similar to Sweller and Cooper (1985) except the changes to the
experimental design outlined in the previous paragraph were incorporated:
namely, the problems studied were less complex and more examples were
provided. In terms of acquisition time, the results of this experiment replicated the
findings of Cooper and Sweller (1985): the worked example group spent
significantly less time acquiring the concepts. In comparison, however, no
differences were found between the groups on similar problems yet large
differences were found on transfer problem. The worked example group performed
better on the transfer problems: in one experiment, while 75% of the worked
example group solved the transfer problem, no students in the conventional
problem solving group were able to solve it in the time available. In addition, the
worked example group also made significantly fewer errors on the transfer
problem than the problem-solving group.
The lack of difference on similar problems between the groups can be explained
through both the worked example group and the conventional problem group
having acquired appropriate schemas. This conclusion is supported by data
indicating that both groups solved the problems swiftly and with few errors. While
neither group had a schema for the transfer problem due to its structural
differences from the training problems, the transfer problem results suggested that
the worked example group had been able to automate their schemas. Although the
novel problem forced both groups to use a means end strategy, the worked
example group was able to solve the problem successfully because of the

117

processing advantage gained by the automation of several problem solving rules
required in the problem’s solution. In comparison, students in the conventional
group, without the benefit of this automation, were overwhelmed by the joint load
of a means end strategy and the need to search for appropriate rules (Sweller,
1999).
In another experiment, Cooper and Sweller (1987) compared a group of low and
high ability students who studied worked examples or solved problems. In addition,
the length of the acquisition phase of the experiment was also manipulated. The
results supported the researchers’ explanation of the effect of worked examples on
transfer problem performance. In the short acquisition phase condition,
performance of similar problems was improved by studying worked examples;
however, in the longer acquisition phase, performance of transfer problems was
improved by studying worked examples. The authors concluded that although
schema acquisition could occur relatively quickly, rule automation occurred much
more slowly. Automation increases a problem solver’s success in situations of high
memory load such as solving transfer problems. Practice, particularly if
interspersed with the study of worked examples, facilitates the automation of
schemas. Schemas must be acquired before they can be automated. Studying
worked examples facilitates the process of schema acquisition leading to faster
acquisition time as well as superior performance.

2.3.2.1

The effect of studying worked examples in real classrooms

While the experiments reported in the previous paragraphs indicated the beneficial
results of studying worked examples in a laboratory experimental situation, theuse
of worked examples in a classroom situation has also been assessed. Ward and
Sweller’s (1990) research used physics problems as part of a high school teaching

118

program. After initial instruction on two topics, two classes taught by the same
teacher received homework problems that consisted of a mixture of worked
examples and conventional problems on one topic and conventional problems only
on the other topic. The topic matched with the worked examples was
counterbalanced across the classes. On the following day, the students sat a
classroom test with questions on both topics. Significant class and topic interaction
effects were found indicating that studying worked examples improved subsequent
test performance. These effects were found for both similar and transfer problems.
The significance of these findings concerning worked examples lies not in an
advocacy of their use since worked examples are commonly used in classrooms
and in textbooks. The significance lies in Sweller and Cooper’s (1985) and Cooper
and Sweller’s (1987) suggestion that the number of worked examples used to
teach a concept be substantially increased and, indeed, that studying worked
examples should be used as a replacement for problem solving. This suggestion
has proved to be fruitful: test performance in many domains has been shown to
improve through increasing the provision of worked examples. In addition, problem
solving flexibility, as indicated by the results for transfer problems, has also shown
improvement. A remarkable testimony to the efficiency of a worked examples
instructional technique is the results of Zhu and Simon’s (1987) study. This study
found that a three year mathematics curriculum could be completed in two years if
worked examples were studied as opposed to learning via a more traditional style
of teaching that involved classroom lecturing and problem solving practice. In fact,
learning was not only expedited in this study, the test results indicated that
students in the worked example group actually performed slightly better than the
conventional group.
The finding of superior performance as a result of learning from worked examples
has been extended beyond the school classroom to include elderly learners. It was
predicted that since working memory resources decline with age, a training

119

technique that maximises the learners’ use of the remaining resources would be
beneficial. As predicted, a group of elderly learners studying worked examples
showed higher training efficiency than a group solving conventional problems (Van
Gerven, Paas, van Merrienboer, Schmidt, in press).

2.3.2.2

Extending the worked examples effect

Despite the learning benefits associated with studying worked examples, student
motivation is a key concern. Typically, worked examples are interspersed with
practice to confront this problem, however, even in this situations there is no way
to ensure that learners are dedicating appropriate attentional resources to study
the worked example before solving the accompanying problem (Sweller et al.,
1998). Completion problems provide some solution to this dilemma. A completion
problem is a partially solved problem that requires the learner to complete the
solution. While possessing the benefits of a worked example in decreasing the
extraneous cognitive load imposed upon a problem solver, completion problems
also solve the problem of motivating students and ensuring their attention is
focused on the solution strategy provided. Students are motivated to study the
example because they are required to solve it. They focus their attention on the
relevant part of the task, since, in order to complete a part of a solution, they must
study the pre and post problem states (Sweller et al., 1998). Paas (1992)
investigated training strategies for transfer in the domain of statistics and
compared students who solved conventional problems, students who solved
completion problems and students who studied worked examples. Both the
worked example group and the completion problem group performed significantly
better than the conventional problem solving group, however, no performance
differences could be differentiated between groups studying these different types
of worked examples.

120

Student motivation and the use of worked examples in instruction to develop
expertise has been further refined by Renkl, Atkinson & Maier (2000). These
researchers investigated a dynamic model of integrating worked examples into
instruction. This model used both complete worked examples, partially complete
worked examples (completion problems) and pure problem solving. Initially,
students studied complete examples. Gradually, the amount of problem solving
students were required to undertake within the examples was increased. The final
stage of the instructional procedure required the students to completely solve
problems themselves. The results suggested this instructional procedureproduced
greater near transfer performance than pairing a complete worked example with a
problem to-be-solved.
The ‘self-explanation effect’, the learners’ ability to explain the solution steps of a
worked example to themselves, has been identified as a critical factor influencing
learning outcomes associated with worked examples (Atkinson, Derry, Renkl &
Wortham, in press; Renkl, 1999; Renkl, Stark, Gruber & Mandl, 1998; Renkl,
1997). In particular, effective self-explanation leads to an increase in performance
on transfer problems. It is hypothesised this effect is achieved because selfexplanation encourages reflection about the more general aspects of problem
solving in a domain (Atkinson et al., in press; Renkl et al., 1998; Renkl, 1997).
Effective self-explanation includes behaviours such as anticipatory reasoning of
problem solving steps and identification of underlying problem-solving principles
and the connection between problem goals and operators (Renkl, 1997).
Moreover, through analysis of the qualitative aspects of learners’ self-explanations,
Renkl (1997) and Stark, Mandl, Gruber and Renkl (in press) were able to predict
the type of performance gains achieved by the learners.
Instructional

techniques

aiming

to facilitate self-explanation have been

investigated; direct training or elicitation of self-explanation have been shown to be
most successful (Stark et al., in press; Renkl, 1999). In fact, in Stark et al.’s (in

121

press) study, learners provided with this training produced twice as many
elaborations as those who were not provided with the training; in addition, interms
of deep cognitive elaborations, the learners provided with elaboration training
produced twice as many of these elaborations, twice as often.

122

2.3.3

Split Attention Effect

The previous section examined the extensive body of research that suggests
studying worked examples can benefit learning. It is the case, however, that
sometimes learning is not improved through the study of worked examples.
Tarmizi and Sweller (1988) investigated the usefulness of worked examples in the
area of circle geometry. Due to the nature of this content, the worked examples
consisted of two sources of information: a diagram and explanatory text. Both
sources of information were necessary for the concepts being taught to be
understood. Little performance differences could be detected between the groups:
the worked example group spent just as long as the problem solving group in the
acquisition phase (an extremely different result to Sweller and Cooper’s (1985)
and Cooper and Sweller’s (1987) findings); they spent longer in the test phaseand
their performance on the test was equivalent. These results seemed to contradict
the previous findings of Sweller and Cooper (1985) and Cooper and Sweller
(1987) which had been conducted in the domains of algebra and physics. The
difference between worked examples in the domains of algebra or physics and the
domain of geometry was that learners were forced to split their attention between
two sources of information which were both necessary for understanding. Although
the geometry worked examples stopped problem solvers using the high cognitive
load strategy of means end analysis, they required learners to process two
physically separate sources of information which referred to each other. This
requirement seemed to have dissipated the worked example’s benefits for
learning. Ward and Sweller concluded that “the critical factors are not whether
worked examples are used, but rather whether the (instructional) techniques used
appropriately direct attention and impose a relatively light cognitive load” (1990:4).
Traditionally figured geometry worked examples and even physics problems that
required students to split their attention between two sources of textual information

123

(the problem statement and the solution equations) did not seem to met this
criterion (Tarmizi and Sweller, 1988; Ward and Sweller, 1990).
In order to comprehend the geometry worked example in Figure 2.2, the learner
needs to understand both the diagram and the text below. The text does not make
sense without referring to the diagram and the diagram does not make sense
without referring to the text. The learners, therefore, are forced to split their
attention between the sources of information and to undertake a search andmatch
process whereby the diagram is searched and the corresponding text statements
are matched to the relevant angles in the diagram. This task imposes a cognitive
load; in addition, the learner is also required to hold all the matched informationin
working memory, imposing an additional cognitive burden. The result of the
‘search and match’ process is that the learner forms a mental picture in which the
two separate sources of information become integrated. This task also uses limited
working memory resources; as a consequence, less working memory resources
are available for learning. According to cognitive load theory, an instructional
format that imposes a cognitive load that is extraneous to the processes of
schema acquisition and automation is less than ideal. It follows, therefore, that
presenting information with separate sources of mutually referring information is
less than ideal since this instructional design requires the learner to undertake
many activities that are extraneous to learning.

124

Figure 2.2: A conventional geometry worked example. An example formatted in this fashion
requires the learner to split their attention between the diagram and the text.

The extraneous burden imposed by a split attention format can be eliminatedif the
process of integrating related information elements is done b efore learning
commences. Mutually referring information can be physically integrated obviating
the need for the learner to undertake this process. The worked example shown in
Figure 2.3 presents the same information as the worked example in Figure 2.2, yet
the two sources of information (text and diagram) have been physically integrated.
The learner’s attention can be focused on this single source of information rather
than having to undertake the extraneous process of matching the related

125

information from the different sources. According to cognitive load theory, the
worked example shown in Figure 2.3 will be of more benefit to learning since extra
cognitive resources will be available for learning.

Figure 2.3: An integrated geometry worked example. The text of Figure 2.2 has been
