Many studies support the notion that physical integration of split sources of
information results in reduced cognitive load and leads to improvements in
learning. Tarmizi and Sweller (1988) found that while split source geometryworked
examples produced performance at a level of the problem solving only group,
physically integrated worked examples produced superior performance. Split
source information is not restricted to diagram and text. Ward and Sweller (1990)
found that similar results to Tarmizi and Sweller with physics worked examples
that involved two sources of textual information. If the example was restructured so
that the two sources of information were physically integrated, the learning
improvements previously associated with worked examples were reinstated (Ward

126

and Sweller, 1990). Physical integration of the multiple sources of informationwas
achieved by integrating the solution equations into the physics problem statements
(Ward and Sweller, 1990).
The benefits of physically integrated instructional material in comparison to split
source instructional material is not restricted to worked examples; for example,
Sweller, Chandler, Tierney and Cooper (1990) developed typical text book
instructions for coordinate geometry that was formatted so that text was physically
separate from diagrams. They also restructured the material so that the text was
physically integrated into the diagram. Students in the integrated instruction group
solved the subsequent test problems faster and more efficiently than the split
source group. Similar results were recorded in other studies that instructed
students on the technical area of numerically controlled machine programming.
These results were recorded regardless of whether the split sources of information
were text and diagram or text and text.
These results have been replicated and generalised to several other areas. In a
long term study, Chandler and Sweller (1991) found that students who studied
electrical engineering materials in a split source format did not reach the initial test
performance level of students in the physically integrated instruction group despite
12 weeks of access to the instructional material. The researchers found similar
results in the domain of biology where the integrated instruction group performed
better than the split source group on the majority of test problems despite
spending less time in the acquisition phase of the experiment.
The detrimental learning effects of presenting split source information has even
been extended to the format of scientific journal articles. Traditional scientific
journal articles separate information describing how an experiment is conducted;
information describing the results and information describing the conclusions
drawn into separate sections. Chandler and Sweller (1992) argued that the

127

traditional journal article format is a classic example of split attention since the
reader is required to mentally integrate information from several sources in order
to comprehend the concepts being presented; for example, a conclusion can only
be understood in relation to the data recorded and the method under which this
data was collected. A group of students read an article either traditionally
formatted or restructured so that the method, results and conclusion of a
procedure were presented simultaneously. Students then answered questions on
details of the report. Students in the modified article group performed significantly
better in the test despite requiring significantly less time to read the article.
A split attention effect is not limited to situations where text and diagrams or text
and text refer to each other and are required to be mentally integrated. Another
common instructional situation requires learners to split their attention between
equipment and instructional material describing how the equipment works; for
example, computer software is frequently taught through the provision of a manual
that constantly refers to aspects of the computer or software. Often a learner’s
attention is split between not only the computer and the manual but the manual
itself is composed of split source text and diagrams. This phenomena was
investigated by Chandler and Sweller (1996) and Sweller and Chandler (1994)
who found that a group who learned about several types of computer software
without access to a computer (the group studied a manual that physically
integrated text into diagrams of the computer screen and keyboard) performed
significantly better on the subsequent test of computer skills than a group that
studied a textual manual and used the computer to follow the steps suggested.
Thus, despite having no ability to practice using the software, the modified manual
group, learning under conditions of lowered cognitive load, developed superior
computer software knowledge. Further support for a cognitive load explanation of
these results was provided by Cerpa et al. (1996). The results of this series of
studies suggested that it was not absence of the computer per se that produced
the superior performance of the modified manual group in Chandler and Sweller’s

128

(1996) and Sweller and Chandler’s (1994) studies but the split attention due to
having to mental integrate information from two sources. Cerpa et al. (1996)
developed a self-contained computer based trai ning package about spreadsheet
software: instructions on how to use a spreadsheet were physically integrated into
the software itself. This group was compared to a group using a conventional
computer manual and the software package on a computer. It was predicted that
the integrated computer based training group would outperform the conventional
manual plus computer group because the learner’s attention would be split
between several sources of information in the latter group. The test results
supported this hypothesis. In another study, self ratings of the mental effort
required for learning were collected from the participants; these indicated that the
integrated group required less mental effort to acquire a superior level of learning.
Interestingly, it has been suggested that split attention should be considered in
innovative educational settings such as collaborative computer supported learning
environments (Van Bruggen, Kirschner and Jochems, in press). Collaboration is
achieved in this environment through discussion; discussion can be both
synchronous or asynchronous. The authors suggest, however, that each of these
discussion formats imposes extraneous cognitive load on the learner. Coherent
asynchronous communication requires the learner to refer back to previous entries
in the discussion list and possibly refer to external information sources creating a
situation where the learners attention is split between several sources of
information. Synchronous discussion makes the same demands while imposing
additional external pace constraints on the learner. Van Bruggen et al. (in press)
suggest software that allows viewpoints and ‘rising above’ notes may be useful
tools to reduce split attention in this environment.
The split attention effect is general and robust. It has been shown to occur across
many academic and technical domains using a variety of information sources that
interact such as text and diagram, text and text and text and equipment.

129

Resolution of the split attention effect through integration of information was of
particular significance for cognitive load theory as it illustrated the far reaching
impact a theoretically based theory of instructional design could have. This design
technique had relevance not only for classroom settings but for realistic training
environments as well as instructional design in general.

130

2.3.4

Redundancy effect

Research into the effect of redundant information upon learning originally
developed from an investigation by Chandler and Sweller (1991) to define the
conditions under which physical integration to reduce split attention was useful.
Redundancy also involves information from several different sources but, in
contrast to split attention, the sources of information are self-contained and canbe
understood in isolation. An example of redundancy is text that replicates the
information already contained in a diagram (see Figure 2.4). If the student is
capable of gaining information about the concept from a single, highly intelligible
source of information, the other source of information becomes redundant.
Redundant information is detrimental to learning because it imposes an
extraneous cognitive load. Cognitive resources are required to process the
repeated information; as a consequence, less resources are available for the
essential learning processes of schema acquisition and automation.

131

Figure 2.4: An example of redundant instructional material. The text in thisfigure provides the
learner with the same information as the diagram (from Chandler and Sweller, 1991).

132

Using several realistic content areas such as blood flow diagrams from biologyand
electrical circuitry, Chandler and Sweller (1991) found that when students studied
self-contained diagrams and text which repeated the information contained in the
diagrams, learning was reduced and study times were often increased. This
pattern of results was achieved regardless of whether redundant text was
integrated or remained separate to the diagram. In a surprising result, Sweller and
Chandler (1994) and Chandler and Sweller (1996) replicated the redundancy
effect by finding the computer redundant when learning to use a computer
program, if the program instruction manual was self contained (included diagrams
of screen and keyboard). This effect was achieved only if the instructional material
5

imposed a high cognitive load . Cerpa et al. (1996) also investigated the effect of
redundant information in computer software training. A group learning from a
computer based training program containing physically integrated instructions was
compared to a group which had access to the integrated computer based training
as well as to a hard copy of these training materials and to a group studying a
conventional (non-integrated) manual. Results from this research indicated that the
group that studied the integrated software with no access to the hard copy
materials (this group, therefore, had no access to redundant material) performed
most successfully and also rated their mental effort as significantly less than the
other groups on a subjective rating scale. The efficiency of the instructional
conditions was assessed using the formula devised by Paas and van Merrienboer
(1993) that standardises and then relates the mental effort and the test
performance associated with a particular instructional condition. This procedure
allows a comparison between instructional conditions to be drawn. It indicated that
the performance efficiency of the group with no exposure to redundant material
was superior to the redundancy group.

27

The effect of learning material of varying intrinsic load will be discussed in section 1.4.

133

The redundancy effect is not restricted to visually presented material. Kalyuga et
al. (1999) replicated the redundancy effect using multimedia computer based
materials. Students given a visual diagram, auditory explanatory text and visual
explanatory text which replicated the auditory text, reported increased mental load
(assessed by subjective mental load scales); performed worse on both multiple
choice and fault finding test questions and required a greater number of learning
trials than a group which received auditory explanatory text only with the visual
diagram. Performance efficiency scores indicated the instructional condition with
no redundant material was a significantly more efficient method of instructionthan
the redundancy condition.
A learner’s level of expertise in a domain is a major influence mediating the
negative impact of redundant information upon learning. For example, Kalyuga et
al. (1998) found the negative learning consequences of including redundant
information altered depending on the level of expertise of the learner. Using
material from electrical engineering, it was found that novice learners learnt best
from a physically integrated instruction format that did not require them to split their
attention between two sources of information. This instructional format integrated
explanatory text into a self-explanatory electrical circuit diagram. Thus, the results
indicated that novice students benefited from the presence of redundant material
in their instruction. After intensive training in the principles of electrical circuitry,
however, the same students performed best from an instructional format that
included the self-explanatory diagram only. These results indicate that the text
became redundant as domain knowledge increased. It was concluded that:
(w)hile the theoretical distinction between sources of information
that are or are not intelligible in isolation is clear cut, it is
reasonable to hypothesise that decisions concerning the
intelligibility of modules of information cannot be made without
reference to the learners for whom the information is intended"
(Kalyuga et al., 1998: 14).

134

Yeung, Jin and Sweller (1998) investigated the effects of expertise and the
usefulness of redundant information in the domain of language comprehension
and vocabulary learning. The findings of Kalyuga et al. (1998) were generalised to
this area. As language comprehension ability increased, the participant’s skill at
comprehending a passage that included vocabulary definitions integrated into the
text decreased. Thus, with increasing expertise, these definitions were redundant.
The experiments discussed in the previous paragraphs indicate that certain parts
of instructional material, which were initially beneficial to learning, can become
redundant and detrimental to learning as expertise increases. Schema acquisition
can explain this finding (Kalyuga et al., 1998; Yeung et al., 1998). Explanatory text,
despite merely repeating information available from another source, is usefulto the
novice learner as one source of information serves to explain another (see for
example, the concept of the starter circuit in Kalyuga et al, 1998: 3-4). As schemas
develop in the relevant knowledge domain, the inclusion of the explanation
becomes unnecessary. In fact, the presence of this repetitive information impedes
further learning since limited processing resources are expended upon redundant
information (Kalyuga et al., 1998; Yeung et al., 1998). This process is extraneous
to learning yet it causes less resources to be available for processes such as
schema acquisition and automation that are critical to learning.
The benefits for learning caused by the removal of redundant information have
been shown through test and study times performance (Kalyuga et al, 1999;
Kalyuga et al, 1998; Yeung et al, 1998; Cerpa et al, 1996; Chandler and Sweller,
1991). In addition, measures of cognitive load support the contention that the
presence of redundant information causes an increase in extraneous cognitive
load. The cognitive load caused by redundant information has been assessed
through such measures as subjective ratings of mental load (Kalyuga et al, 1999 ;

135

Kalyuga et al, 1998; Yeung et al, 1998; Cerpa et al, 1996 and secondary task
performance (Chandler and Sweller, 1996).
There is a history of evidence supporting the detrimental effect of redundant
information on learning. For example, Miller’s (1937) investigation of children’s
reading suggested that pictures presented in conjunction with nouns hampered
reading progress. Several more recent studies have replicated this result (e.g. Wu
& Solman, 1994); in addition, the finding has been extended to passages of text
rather than single words. Reder and Anderson (1980, 1982) investigated learning
from whole text book chapters in comparison to chapter summaries. In a robust
result, they reported that students learning from summaries showed superior
retention of the main ideas of the text and this advantage was retained over time.
Their investigations suggested this result was not caused by the reduced amount
of time allotted to studying main points (Reder and Anderson, 1982) but rather was
a consequence of the limited nature of human cognitive processing. It was
suggested that processing capacity was used to learn details from the full text to
the detriment of main point learning (Reder & Anderson, 1980). It was concluded
from these results that the full text was redundant. Mayer, Bove, Bryman, Mars
and Tapangco (1996) replicated Reder and Anderson’s findings using a scientific
text. The group that received the information summary performed significantly
better than the group that received the full text. Performance was assessed by
both free recall and problem solving measures. In fact, the performance of the
summary group on the problem solving task was approximately double that of the
full text group. These results were also used to support the hypothesis that the
limitations of human cognitive architecture impede the ability to learn when a
learner is required to process redundant information.

136

2.3.5

Modality effect

The cognitive load effects previously discussed in this thesis have developedfrom
the assumption that working memory capacity is limited and, in order to maximise
the processing resources devoted to learning, instructional techniques shouldaim
to reduce the extraneous load they impose on learners. In contrast, the modality
effect arises from the assumption that working memory capacity can be expanded.
While the former assumption is based on the concept of working memory as a
unitary entity, the latter assumption takes into account more recent theories of
memory which suggest that working memory is composed of several partially
independent processors that handle information from different sensory modalities
6

such as auditory and visual (Baddeley, 1981, 1990, 1992; Paivio, 1990) . Thus,
according to this assumption, if instructional material uses both a diagrammatic or
written format and an auditory format, the capacity of working memory to process it
will be increased in comparison to its capacity to process the same information if it
was presented in a solely visual or solely auditory format. Some of the evidence
supporting this assumption includes the finding that the ability to carry several
tasks at the same time is improved by the presentation of the tasks in different
modalities (Allport, Antonis and Reynolds, 1972) and that recall is improved if
presentation of stimuli is bi-modal (Frick, 1984).
Although evidence supports the notion that working memory contains processors
for different modes of information, it does not suggest that these processors are
completely separate. If this were true, bi-modal presentation of information would
lead to increases in recall of 100%; while memory increases are apparent, theyare
not of this order of magnitude.

28

A more detailed explanation of these theories is provided in section 1.2.2.1.

137

If the assumption that working memory contains partially independent information
processors differentiated by information modality is held to be true, it follows that
an instructional technique that takes these features of working memory into
account could have benefits for learning. Thus, if the information to be learnt is
presented through both auditory and visual channels, theoretically, the capacity of
working memory to process the information should be increased; thus, learning
will be more efficient. In addition, the use of such a dual mode instruction should
provide an alternative technique for overcoming the extraneous cognitive load
generated by the split attention effect. The extra processing resources available
from a dual mode presentation should alleviate the extra cognitive load generated
by split source materials that require a student to mentally integrate the sources of
information.
Several studies have investigated such an instructional technique; for example,
Mousavi et al. (1995) investigated the use of a dual mode instructional technique
in the area of geometry. Geometry is a domain where traditionally, due to the
nature of its content, instructional resources have often caused the learner to split
their attention between a diagram and explanatory text. Using geometry worked
examples, a group that received a visual diagram and an auditory explanationwas
compared to a group that received a visual diagram and a visual (written text)
explanation and to a group that received a visual diagram, a visual explanation
and the same explanation presented auditorily. The visual/auditory group
consistently solved similar and transfer problems faster than the visual only group
although no test score advantages were reported. The results of several other
studies indicated that superiority of the dual mode group were unlikely to be due to
factors such as students in the visual /visual group having to hold informationfrom
one source in working memory before attending to the other source or students
finding listening easier than reading. In addition, the benefits of a dual mode
instruction were achieved regardless of whether the format of the visual
component of the information was in written or diagrammatic form.

138

The conditions under which dual mode instruction are most beneficial have been
further refined. In areas of low visual search in geometry, Jeung et al. (1997)
replicated the findings of Mousavi et al. (1995): dual mode of instruction was
superior to visual only instruction. However, in conditions of high visual search,
dual mode instruction was of no more benefit than visual only instruction. It was
hypothesised that in this situation the benefits of dual modal instruction were
alleviated by the extraneous cognitive load imposed by the task of coordinating the
audio and visual information. When a visual marker in the form of electronic
flashing was included in the instructional format to facilitate the information search
and match process, the superiority of the dual mode instruction group was
regained.
Tindall-Ford et al. (1997) further explored the benefits of dual mode instruction for
ameliorating the cognitive burden of the split attention effect. Using high element
interactivity content from the technical area of electrical engineering, Tindall-Ford
et al. compared a group that received a visual diagram and auditory explanation
(dual mode group), a group that received a conventional split source visual
diagram and visual text explanation (split attention group) and a group that
received a visual diagram and visual explanation that had been physically
integrated (integrated group). The results indicated that both the dual mode
instruction group and the integrated instruction group outperformed the split
attention group in terms of both transfer test scores and practical test scores. The
performance of the dual mode group and the integrated group could not be
differentiated. These results indicate that the cognitive burden imposed by thesplit
attention effect is solved equally well by instructional techniques that reduce
extraneous load through physical integration or techniques that expand the
capacity of working memory.

139

In a second study, the generality of a dual mode instruction was examined through
the use of a text table rather than a diagram. In this experiment a group that
received the visual information table and an auditory explanation was compared to
a group that received the visual table and a visual (text) explanation. In addition,
this experiment was significant because the cognitive load of the participants was
directly assessed through subjective mental effort ratings. The dual mode group
performed significantly better than the visual only group while rating their mental
effort significantly lower. When the performance efficiency of the instructional
conditions was compared, the dual mode condition was a significantly more
efficient method of instruction then the visual only technique.
If the success of the dual mode instructional technique is due to the fact that
working memory resources are expanded, as cognitive load theory contends, it
follows that dual mode instruction will only be of benefit when cognitive load is
high. Tindall-Ford et al. (1997) assessed this assumption by comparing dual mode
instruction to visual only instruction on both low and high element interactivity
materials. Both test performance and subjective mental load ratings supported this
conclusion indicating that the use of two sensory modalities for instruction expands
the capacity of working memory; the greater processing power that results is of
most benefit in situations where the cognitive load imposed by the instructional
material, the intrinsic load, is high enough to jeopardise working memory’s limited
processing ability. This study is discussed in greater detail in the following section
(section 2.4).

140

2.4 Instruction and element interactivity
Despite the evidence discussed previously that suggests that presenting
instructions using instructional techniques that impose a high extraneous cognitive
load is detrimental to learning, further research indicates that this is only the case
when the intrinsic cognitive load imposed by the instructional content itself is high.
When this situation arises, the total cognitive load imposed upon the learner bythe
content and the method of instruction overburdens working memory and littleif any
learning results. For example, Sweller and Chandler (1994) found that when
students were required to solve a problem where there was minimal interaction
between the information elements (e.g. moving a cursor a set distance on the grid
screen of a computer aided design package) no difference existed between a
group learning from a conventional instructional manual and a computer, a group
that learnt from a self-contained integrated manual and a group provided with the
integrated manual as well as a computer. Thus, this experiment was designed to
assess both the split attention and the redundancy effect. In contrast, when the
participants were required to solve problems that imposed a higher intrinsic load
(e.g. the steps required to draw a line between two absolute coordinate positions
on the grid screen of a computer aided design package) the results indicated a
significant performance difference. In these types of problems the integrated group
performed better. The element interactivity of the material was estimated by
assessing both the number of elements of information that that was requiredto be
processed concurrently as well as the student’s expertise in the domain. Large
differences in the number of simultaneously processed elements existed between
problems that were considered to be low in element interactivity and problems that
were considered to be high in element interactivity.
Similar results were reported by Chandler and Sweller (1996) using the same
experimental groups as Sweller and Chandler (1994). The self-contained manual
141

group performed better on written and practical tasks only when the material
imposed a high intrinsic cognitive load. These researchers also measured the
cognitive load imposed by the learning tasks by measuring the participant’s
performance on a secondary task. While low element interactivity material did not
affect secondary task performance, significant differences were present when the
participants solved problems based on high element interactivity material. In this
case, the secondary task performance of students forced to learn under conditions
of split attention or redundancy were significantly decreased in comparison to
students where the instructional information did not impose an extraneous
cognitive load. In addition the trend of the difference was such that while both the
split attention and the redundancy groups’ performance on the secondary task
decreased dramatically, a minimal performance decrement was recorded for the
integrated manual group. As the intrinsic cognitive load of the content material was
standardised across all groups, this result provides strong support for the notion
that it was extraneous load associated with the instructional formats of both the
split attention (conventional manual and computer) and redundancy (integrated
manual and computer) group that was responsible for performance decrements
experienced by these groups (Chandler and Sweller, 1996).
It is important to note that the low element interactivity material used by Sweller
and Chandler (1994) and Chandler and Sweller (1996) did not consistent of trivial
concepts, it merely did not impose a high intrinsic cognitive load since the
elements did not interact. Skills such as moving a cursor in different denomination
steps, using the grid lock or using the function menus of computer aided design
software are difficult to learn because there are many separate skills to remember.
Each skill can be learnt individually, however, since this learning requires onlyone
or two information elements to be processed simultaneously. As a result, the
intrinsic cognitive load imposed by material of this type is minimal. In order to
undertake high element interactivity functions such moving between two absolute
coordinate positions in computer aided design software, however, novice users of

142

the software are required to hold as many as nine elements of information
concurrently. As a consequence, the intrinsic cognitive load imposed by material of
this nature is much greater than in the material described previously.
The results discussed in the previous paragraphs indicate that instructional
conditions that reduce extraneous cognitive load in situations where the intrinsic
load imposed by the instructional material is high can have benefits for learning.
Similarly, the results of several experiments indicate that instructional conditions
that expand working memory resources can also benefit to learning in this
situation. For instance, Tindall-Ford et al. (1997) compared the performance of a
group that received information in a visual only format (text and diagram) to a
group that received diagrammatic visual information and an equivalent auditory
version of the textual information (dual modality group) using instructional material
that imposed either a high or a low intrinsic load. The low element interactivity
material consisted of a list of electrical symbols and their meaning (see Figure2.5
). Although there were many symbols to be learnt, the information was of low
element interactivity since each symbol could be learnt and understood in isolation
to the other symbols. As each symbol could be processed serially, the working
memory load imposed by this material was minimal. In contrast, the high element
interactivity material consisted of several electrical circuits (see Figure 2.5). To
understand an electrical circuit, all parts of the circuit must be simultaneously
processed since each part of the circuit is related to each other part. The
requirement of simultaneous processing has the effect of imposing a high
processing burden on working memory.

143

LOW ELEMENT

HIGH ELEMENT

INTERACTIVITY MATERIAL

INTERACTIVITY MATERIAL

Figure 2.5: An example of some of the low element interactivity material and the high element
interactivity material used in Tindall-Ford et al. (1997).

144

For low element interactivity material, no difference was found between the visual only and
the dual mode instructional technique in terms of test performance or subjective mental
load. The efficiency of the instructional conditions for this material could not be
differentiated. In contrast, for high element interactivity material, the students in the dual
mode condition showed superior test performance in terms of scores and times as well as
rating their mental effort significantly lower. The researchers also eliminated the possibility
that these results may have been due to the students finding listening an easier task than
reading. The efficiency of the instructional conditions was assessed and the results
indicated that the dual mode condition was a significantly more efficient instructional
technique in a situation where the learner was required to understand high element
interactivity material. These results provide support to the cognitive load interpretation of a
dual mode instructional technique: dual modality instruction expands working memory
capacity and results in greater processing capacity. This is only beneficial to learning in
situations when the intrinsic load of the instructional material is high enough to jeopardise
working memory’s limited processing ability.
The results of these studies that have focussed on information of differing element
interactivity indicate that information can be difficult to learn for several reasons.
Information may be difficult to learn because there is a large amount of information
to be learnt or because there is a large amount of information to be processed
concurrently (Tindall-Ford et al., 1997). Cognitive load concerns arise in the latter
case and it is in situations such as these that the instructional technique used
becomes a critical factor in learning. Instructional formats that reduce extraneous
cognitive load or increase the ability of working memory to process information
benefit learners when the information to be learnt imposes a high intrinsic cognitive
load.

145

2.5 Cognitive load theory: a summary
Cognitive load theory applies knowledge about the structure of human cognitive
architecture to the design of instructional material. The theory contends features of
cognitive architecture and development of expertise such as the limited capacityof
working memory, the virtually limitless storage available in long term memory and
the acquisition and automation of schemas need to be considered when designing
instruction. Further, the theory aims to develop instructional designs that facilitate
learning by providing conditions which aid the processes of schema acquisition
and automation. Since information imposes its own intrinsic load on working
memory, in order to ensure sufficient processing resources are available for
learning, instructions need to be designed so that they do not incorporate features
that are extraneous to learning, yet necessitate the use of cognitive resources.
Cognitive load theory has assessed many commonly used instructional techniques
and found that they impose a high cognitive burden that “interferes with the very
learning intended by the instructional procedure” (Sweller, 1999: 55). These
instructional procedures are cognitively demanding because the instructional
designs themselves incorporate features that are extraneous to learning and,
therefore, “interfere with rather than assist in schema acquisition and automation”
(Sweller, 1999: 55). Cognitive load theory has been used to generate alternative
instructional techniques which, while serving the same instructional purpose as the
conventional procedures, either reduce the extraneous load imposed by the
instructional design or expand the capacity of working memory. These techniques
include goal free problems, increased exposure to worked examples, physical
integration of split sources of information, elimination of redundant information and
the use of several modalities in instruction.

146

Goal free problems prevent learners using means end analysis, a problem solving
strategy that imposes a high cognitive load and directs the learner’s attention to
aspects of the problem that do not facilitate the development of schemas.
Increasing the amount of worked examples a learner studies also prevents theuse
of means end analysis; in addition, this technique directs the learner’s attention to
aspects of the problem that need to be incorporated into a problem solving
schema. The physical integration of mutually referring sources of information
eliminates the split attention effect; this occurs when attention must be split
between two sources of mutually referring information and the learner is forced to
mentally integrate the information sources in order to understand the concept
being presented. If the need to process redundant material incorporated into
instructions is eliminated, more processing resources become available for
learning. If several modalities are used to present instructional material, the
partially independent modality processors existing in working memory are utilised;
this has the effect of expanding the capacity of working memory to process
information. The instructional techniques outlined above are of most use when the
intrinsic cognitive load, caused by the instructional material itself, is high.
The focus of cognitive load theory research on developing instructional designs
that reduce extraneous load or increase working memory capacity is, in part, due
to the previously held belief that the intrinsic load of instructional material is
unalterable (Sweller, 1993; Sweller and Chandler, 1994; Chandler and Sweller,
1996; Sweller, van Merrienboer and Paas, 1998). The next chapter of this thesis
will discuss findings that indicate that this belief may be false. In other words, the
intrinsic cognitive load of instructional material may be changeable.

147

Chapter 3

Pathways to

meaningful learning
3.1

Types of learning

Historically in the field of education, a debate has raged over the necessity of
understanding as a component of learning. Earlier this century, there was strong
support for learning without understanding, that is rote learning: “ for pure practical
efficacy it is better that the rationale should not at the actual time of working be
thought of at all. The pupil should contain his efforts to a rigid application of the rule”
(Ballard, 1915: 12). By the middle of the century, there was a shift of support in the
debate towards meaningful learning, that is learning with understanding.
Experimental evidence increasingly supported this view; for instance, in a large
study involving 4 cities, 41 classes and 1400 students, Brownell & Moser (1949)
compared students who were taught to borrow in subtraction by either a meaningful
or a mechanical (rote) method. The evidence overwhelming supported a meaningful
learning approach.
Ausubel (1968a) contended that rote learnt material and meaningfully learnt material
are represented, organised and stored differently as well being retained and
forgotten at different rates. Rote learning produces "discrete, self contained entities
organizationally isolated" from existing stored knowledge (Ausubel, 1968a: 109). In
contrast, meaningful learning is learning that is anchored to existing knowledge in a
non-arbitrary, substantive fashion.

148

Several factors must be present in order for meaningful learning to occur: the
learner must be disposed to relate the new learning in a meaningful way to his or
her existing knowledge; the relationship between the new concept and existing
knowledge must be plausible (non-arbitrary) and not dependent upon a single set of
words or phrases (non-verbatim); and most importantly, it must be possible for the
learning task to be meaningfully related to existing knowledge (Ausubel, 1968a,b).
For this final criterion to be achieved, the learner’s cognitive structure must already
contain an appropriate and relevant body of knowledge (Ausubel, 1977).
Subsequent research has reinforced Ausubel’s contention about the importance of
prior knowledge for meaningful learning; for example, Dochy, Segers & Buehl (1999:
173) concluded after their thorough review of the literature that “prior knowledge is
indeed an effective aid for learning new knowledge”. They also suggest that
students with little or no prior knowledge in an area have difficulty in learning and
structuring new information because they lack the necessary knowledge
frameworks. Yates and Chandler (1991) label this phenomena the Matthew Effect:
that is, the (knowledge) rich get richer. This idea is reminiscent of Ausubel’s
explanations and can also be understood from a schema perspective: if the learner
does not possess a schema or it is not activated, comprehension and recall are
reduced at the time of learning and beyond (e.g., see Bransford and Johnson,
1972).
Despite the more onerous conditions that must be fulfilled in order to achieve meaningful
learning, this type of learning is more desirable than rote learning since knowledge learnt
in this manner is more easily developed, it is retained better and is forgotten after a
greater interval than knowledge gained through rote learning (Ausubel, 1968a; 1968b;
1977). Meaningful learning possesses these advantages over rote learning because of
the meaningful relationship that is developed between the new knowledge and the
learner’s existing knowledge hierarchy (Ausubel, 1968b). If the learner does not possess
prior knowledge relevant to the learning task, however, rote learning may be

149

unavoidable since "(i)f some existing … set of ideas cannot be utilised for assimilative
purposes, the only alternative is rote learning" (Ausubel, 1968a: 131).

3.2

An alternative path to understanding

There is evidence in the literature that learning in the absence of full comprehension
can ultimately benefit understanding; for example, King & Russell (1966) compared
academically matched students given the task of reading a text from psychology
with instructions to either aim to learn the text word for word or to focus on learning
the main ideas. Both at immediate recall and at recall after a second review of the
text, the main ideas group performed significantly better on measures of recall,such
as number of letters, number of content words identical to the original text and
number of sentences recalled. The groups did not differ statistically in terms of their
memory for the meaning of the passage, identified in terms of their scores on
measures such as number of idea units and sequences of words. Interestingly, by
the second recall (undertaken after a second reading of the text) the trend of the
data had reversed magnitude suggested that the word for word group was
outperforming the main ideas group in terms of understanding the meaning of the
passage. The authors were puzzled by the results, suggesting that the distinction
between rote learning and meaningful learning is not clear cut. The results of this
experiment can be reinterpreted in light of Hoosain’s (1983) work.
Hoosain (1983) was interested in exploring if there is a cognitive foundation to the
traditional Chinese practice of rote learning classical literary passages. Classical
literary passages are complex pieces of text, different to both modern written
Chinese and modern spoken Chinese because of their remote subject matter, use of
archaic words and phrasal structures and their terse, non-repetitive style. In order to
test the influence of rote learning, groups of female high school students were

150

instructed to either learn a text by rote, without trying to understand the ideas, or to
try to understand the ideas in the text without focusing on the exact words. Four text
passages were used in the experiment, differing in complexity of the content (difficult
or easy) and the writing style (traditional literary or modern colloquial style). While
the colloquial texts were approximately twice as long as the literary texts, the ideas
presented in each set of texts were identical since colloquial texts were translations
of the literary texts. The difference in length is testimony to the more descriptive,
verbose style of the colloquial writing style as compared to the literary texts. To
summarise, the experiment compared rote learning to main ideas learning using
texts that differed in complexity and writing style.
The eight experimental groups were tested on their verbatim recall and their
comprehension of the particular text allocated to their group. As might be expected,
the rote memorisation group performed significantly better on the verbatim recall
measure regardless of type of text. Comprehension was assessed through multiple
choice questions based upon the content of the passage. Surprisingly, the
comprehension performance of the rote and main ideas groups across all text types
could not be distinguished. However, comprehension performance main effects for
literary style and passage difficulty were significant: superior performance was
recorded for groups studying colloquial texts in preference to literary texts and for
groups studying the easier passage. When the raw data for comprehension was
analysed, it showed that the rote memorisation group outperformed the ideas group
on both the difficult literary and the difficult colloquial texts. In order to investigate
this effect further, Hoosain replicated the experiment using male high school
students. In this experiment only two groups were compared: a rote memorisation
group and a main ideas group. The groups were compared on their verbatim recall
and comprehension of the difficult literary text used in the previous experiment. A
similar pattern of results to the previous experiment was recorded. The rote
memorisation group recalled significantly more in terms of verbatim reproduction yet

151

their comprehension test performance could not be differentiated from the main
ideas group.
Hoosain concluded the traditional practice of memorising classical Chinese texts
does have a cognitive basis. He suggested "(r)ote memorisation, even with only
partial understanding, could serve to retain the text in memory while waiting for a
future opportunity to unravel meanings" (1983, p.196). Thus, understanding was
able to continue after the initial learning because the student was still able toaccess
a large part of the text in memory. Access to this material in memory may have
given the rote memorisation students an advantage in the test because the
questions may have directed them to parts of the text which they could then reflect
upon. In contrast, students in the main ideas group who did not understand the
meaning of parts of the text during their original learning had no opportunity to
retrospectively acquire this understanding. This conclusion can also provide an
explanation for King and Russell’s (1966) results.
Hoosain’s results and conclusions can be viewed from a cognitive load perspective.
Learning a complex text by rote allowed working memory to process some of the
necessary basic elements that constitute a schema for the information to develop.
This schema helped learners in their subsequent comprehension test performance.
This interpretation is supported by the finding that the rote memorisation group
performed most strongly on the most difficult texts (the difficult literary and the
difficult colloquial text). A cognitive load interpretation would predict that the most
obvious difference between learners who have acquired a schema for the
information and learners who have not would occur when the material to be learnt is
at its most difficult. This is because the difficulty of a text is caused by a high degree
of interactivity between the elements forming the text. As the element interactivity of
learning material increases, the likelihood of working memory being overwhelmed
also increases. If this overload occurs, a schema for the information cannot develop.

152

A lack of a schema further compounds the learner’s ability to process the
information and leads to an inability to comprehend the meaning of the text.
The fact that in both Hoosain’s (1983) and King and Russell’s (1966) research the
groups instructed to learn without necessarily understanding the material displayed
superior performance when tested for comprehension and understanding raises a
question worthy of further investigation: would initially learning some elements of
information, even if comprehension was not possible, be ultimately beneficial to
learning? This question will be explored further in the next section.

153

Chapter 4

Introduction

to the

empirical investigations
4.1 A summary of the research pertinent to
this thesis
The previous chapters have provided a detailed discussion of the theoretical
assumptions and research relevant to the current thesis. These assumptions and
research provide a theoretical framework for the current empirical investigations.
This chapter will summarise this theoretical framework and briefly outline the
experiments which follow.
The aspects of human cognitive architecture relevant to higher intellectual
processes such as learning include the initial processing of information byworking
memory and storage of information in long term memory in schemas. Working
memory is defined by its capacity limitations; it can process only a few elements of
information at any one time (Simon, 1974; Miller, 1956a). This contrasts to long
term memory which has a seemingly unlimited storage capacity (Newell & Simon,
1972). Knowledge is stored in long term memory in cognitive constructs called
schemas. Schemas hold vast amounts of information; they are hierarchically
organised. Schemas not only provide the structure for long term memory but also
form the basis of our intellectual capacity (de Groot, 1966; Sweller and Cooper,
1985) as they reduce the processing burden placed on working memory by
enabling information from different contexts to be recognised and categorised
accordingly.
154

Schema acquisition and automation of the knowledge held in schemas are the
critical factors involved in human learning, understanding and the development of
expertise. Evidence has now emerged to strongly suggest that the differences
between experts and novices is their domain specific knowledge stored in
schemas. Expertise is determined by the extent of schema acquisition (de Groot,
1965; Chase and Simon, 1973), as well as the degree to which these schemas
contain specific information on domain typical problem solutions (Chi et al., 1982;
Larkin et al, 1980 ; Sweller, Mawer and Ward , 1983). With expertise, interacting
elements that would otherwise overwhelm working memory can be incorporatedin
a schema that acts as a single element and imposes a minimal working memory
load. Given these findings, the aim of instruction should be to build knowledge and
expertise through the acquisition and automation of schemas.
Cognitive load theory (see Sweller, 1999 for a recent summary) has its foundations
in the cognitive architecture described above. It uses some aspects of human
cognitive architecture and of the structure of information to provide instructional
designs that are conducive to the acquisition of schemas and their automation. The
theory contends that the amount of mental load placed on working memory by an
instructional task will be the critical factor in determining how effective learning has
been. Working memory load plays such an essential role in learning becauseif the
capacity of working memory is exceeded, schema acquisition and automation will
be impeded.
Cognitive load theory makes a distinction between two sources of cognitive load.
Extraneous cognitive load is generated by the manner in which information is
presented to learners and is under the control of instructional designers. The
intrinsic cognitive load of information is determined by the information itself; more
specifically, it is determined by the extent to which various elements interact. While
all information will impose some degree of intrinsic cognitive load, difficult to

155

understand material is difficult because its information elements interact
extensively and this imposes a heavy load on working memory because these
elements must be processed simultaneously (Halford, Wilson & Phillips, 1998;
Chandler & Sweller, 1996; Marcus, Cooper & Sweller, 1996; Sweller, 1994).
It has been stated previously that the intrinsic load of instructional material is
unalterable (Sweller, 1993; Sweller and Chandler, 1994; Chandler and Sweller,
1996; Sweller, van Merrienboer and Paas, 1998). This is true if immediate
understanding is the goal of the task; however, it will be indicated in the following
paragraphs that intrinsic load can be altered if a learning situation is created where
understanding is temporarily not an objective.
The results of several experiments have suggested that presenting allthe elements
required for understanding does not benefit learning (Hoosain, 1983; King and
Russell, 1966). In addition, research into learning hierarchies has suggestedlower
level skills are prerequisites to higher level skills (Gagne, 1973, 1970; Bloom,
1956) and that learning part of a whole skill in isolation to the whole will ultimately
benefit understanding of the whole if this relationship between these entities is
meaningful and it is made explicit (Schmidt, 1991; Singley & Anderson, 1989).

156

4.2 Learning complex information: a paradox
This combination of cognitive architecture and information structures constitutes
the core of cognitive load theory; the theory can be used to design instruction
intended to reduce extraneous cognitive load and facilitate learning through more
rapid and effective schema construction. Nevertheless, as it stands, there is a gap
in the theory. Very complex, high element interactivity material cannot be
understood without processing all of the elements simultaneously in working
memory. As indicated previously, human cognitive architecture overcomes this
problem by incorporating the elements in a schema or limited number of schemas
that can be processed easily in working memory. Once constructed and stored in
long-term memory, a system of schemas overcomes the problem of a limited
working memory. The difficulty arises when one considers how such schemas can
be constructed in the first instance, if the learner possesses no or few a priori
schemas in the domain. If the material is very complex, consisting of a large
number of elements that are high in element interactivity, it will not be possible to
process all of the elements in working memory simultaneously. But they must be
processed simultaneously in working memory in order to be understood. The
material cannot be understood if only some of the elements are processed in
working memory. Furthermore, for a schema to be constructed, all of the elements
must be processed in working memory. Thus, we have a paradox. Material can be
understood once a schema has been constructed allowing all the elements to be
processed in working memory simultaneously but until that point has been
reached, the elements cannot be processed simultaneously in working memory
and so cannot be understood. It seems to follow that for novices learning very
complex material, the initial stages of schema construction must occur in an
environment in which the material cannot be understood.

157

Most instructional design is intended to facilitate understanding and accordingly,
instructional designers present their material in a manner that potentially permits
understanding. That means incorporating all of the elements required for
understanding in the instructions. As indicated above, for very complex material
with many interacting elements, attempts by learners to assimilate all of the
elements will inevitably fail due to working memory limitations. Understanding is
impossible until after appropriate schemas have been acquired and attempts to
facilitate understanding by presenting all the required elements simultaneously,
will, according to cognitive load theory, also fail. Alternative modes of presentation
may be required.
Cumulatively, the strands of research described in the previous section suggest a
possible alternative to solve the paradox of learning complex information. This
alternative instructional technique is the isolated-interacting elements approach.

158

4.3 An isolated–interacting elements instructional
technique
Consider the instructional material of Figure 4.1 intended to teach electrical
engineering students how to conduct a test of insulation resistance. In order to
understand this material, the learner must simultaneously consider the following
elements: the aim of the test, the required setting of the volt meter, the required
setting of the appliance switch, the location of the earth lead and the line lead
during both phases of the test and the criteria by which to judge the voltmeter
readings as safe. For novices, these elements far exceed working memory limits.
While an expert electrical engineer can process all of these elements
simultaneously in working memory via previously acquired schemas, a relative
novice (e.g., first year apprentice or trainee), not possessing the requisite
schemas, can only hold and process a few of the elements simultaneously in
working memory. If only a few of the elements are processed in working memory
simultaneously, the material cannot be understood because the elements interact.
Accordingly, this material, designed to facilitate understanding, must inevitably fail
in its aim with both understanding and learning inhibited. This type of instructional
procedure will be referred to as the "interacting elements" procedure. It is
characterised by the inclusion of all elements required for understanding but at the
cost of an impossibly high working memory load.

159

This test aims to check that there is no “leak” in the f low of current f rom either the active orneutral wires of the
appliance to the f rame of the appliance, which would cause the f rame of the appliance to become electricaly
active.

appliance and a second including the frame of the appliance and the neutral of the appliance. Very large
measures of insulation resistance (ideally “inf inite” resistance) indicates that there are no leaks in theflow of
current to each circuit. A megger meter measures the resistance of the circuit.

2. The appliance is switched “on” to allow current generated f rom the megger meter to f low into each circuit.
Make sure the switch is “on”.
3. To measure of the resistance of the f irst circuit the megger meter is connected into the circuit. Placethe
line lead on the frame of the appliance.
4. Place the earth lead on the active pin of the appliance’s plug.
5. The test button of the megger meter introduces 500 v olts into the circuit. Press the test button.
6. A measurement of insulation resistance of the circuit can be taken f rom the needle of the meter, with a
reading of at least 1 Megohm (1,000,000 ohms) being suf f iciently large to indicate that the active-framecircuit
possesses an acceptable lev el of insulation. This ensures that no current leaks to the appliance’s f rame.
Read the resistance from the meter. The required result is a reading of at least one Megohm.
7. To complete the test of insulation resistance the resistance of the second circuit must be measured. The
second circuit is set up between the neutral point of the appliance and the appliance f rame. Remove the
earth lead from the active pin and place it on the neutral pin.
