Figure 4.1: An example of interacting elements instructional material.
160

In contrast, consider the alternative instructional material consisting solely of the
figure and text of Figure 4.2. Note that this text is the same as the text in bold type
in Figure 4.1. These materials are intended to be learned element by element
without a high degree of understanding. These instructions include the isolated
elements of information that form the concept yet none of the interactions between
the elements that allow the learner to understand the concept holisticly. They will
be referred to as the "isolated elements" version of the instructional material.
Unlike the interacting elements procedure, students who studied this set of
instructions would easily be able to hold and process the isolated elements in
working memory. The cost is reduced understanding but the potential benefit is an
increased probability of learning because the reduced working memory load
permits necessary elements to be held and processed in working memory. Once
these materials are learned (albeit with limited understanding), it may be possible
for learners to process the interacting elements materials in working memory
allowing full understanding to occur. Previous study of these materials will have the
effect of reducing the working memory burden on the learner as information about
isolated elements included in the interacting elements instructions will alreadyhave
been retained in a partial schema for the concept. The acquisition of this should
allow the learner to be in a position to understand the full concept when he or she
is exposed to the interacting elements instructions in the second phase of learning.
It is predicted that studying isolated elements materials followed by interacting
elements materials will be of more benefit to learning than repeated exposure to
the interacting elements materials.

161

1. Set the meter to read 500 volts.
2. Make sure the switch is "on".
3. Place line lead on the frame of the appliance.
4. Place the earth lead on the active pin of the appliance's plug.
5. Press the test button.
6. Read the resistance from the meter. The required result is a reading
of at least one Megohm.
7. Remove the earth lead from the active pin and place it on the neutral pin.
8. Press the test button again.
9. Read the resistance. A reading of at least one Megohm is again required.

Figure 4.2: An example of isolated elements instructional material.

This result is predicted despite the fact that there is too little information for
understanding to occur with isolated elements but understanding potentially can
occur with the presentation of interacting elements. It is predicted that these
isolated elements of information will act as a body of knowledge that further
learning can anchor to and be subsumed by. At the second learning phase, when
the instructional material contains both isolated and interacting elements of
information, meaningful learning can be achieved because Ausubel’s conditions for
this type of learning have been met: the new learning can be related to previously
162

acquired knowledge in a non-arbitrary and substantive manner. In effect, it is
suggested that for complex materials, our cognitive architecture may dictate that
sometimes, a degree of learning must occur before understanding, and that our
instructional designs should reflect this imperative.
To summarise, the proposed instructional design aims to fill a gap that currently
exists within cognitive load theory. This theory cannot explain how schemas and
understanding of very complex concepts are developed by novices. These
processes require all the elements of the concept to be processed simultaneously
in working memory, yet, working memory’s limitations preclude this from occurring
in the case of a concept with high interactivity between the elements. The
proposed instructional design aims to facilitate learning in this situation by
attempting to show that initially learning about a concept without full
comprehension will ultimately aid the student’s ability to comprehend the concept
by providing him or her with a knowledge framework to which the new learning can
be meaningfully related.
The predicted benefits of an isolated-interacting elements instructional approach
follows only under circumstances where the knowledge base of the learners is
such that they are unable to process all of the interacting elements in working
memory. A learner who has the ability to process the interacting elements
simultaneously possesses this ability as a result of their previously acquired
domain schemas; thus, a learner in this situation will gain little if any benefit from
an instructional procedure whose aim is to facilitate the development of
rudimentary domain schemas.

163

4.4 An introduction to the experiments conducted
in this thesis
This thesis reports upon five experiments. Overall, these experiments seek to
examine the usefulness of an isolated elements-interacting elements instructional
technique. The experiments have used comprehensive instructional material from
several domains. The knowledge of the learners after instruction has been
assessed in multiple ways through both written and practical tests. In addition, the
experiments seek to provide support for a cognitive load hypothesis. To this end,
measures of mental load have been collected and analysed.
Experiment 1 investigated whether studying isolated elements materials followed
by interacting elements materials will be of more benefit to learning than repeated
exposure to the interacting elements materials when learning about complex
information. This experiment used electrical engineering materials that were highin
element interactivity.
Experiment 2 aimed to further define the conditions under which an isolatedinteracting elements instructional technique is beneficial for learning. If learners
have acquired sufficient schemas to process the required elements and their
interactions simultaneously in working memory, the need for an isolated elements
stage in the instruction process may be unnecessary. Experiment 2 replicated
Experiment 1 exactly except for the expertise level of the participants. Using more
experienced learners, it aimed to investigate whether the isolated–interacting
elements instructional technique would benefit learners who already possess
domain relevant schemas.

164

Experiments 3 and 4 were designed to replicate and extend the findings of
Experiments 1 and 2. Novice learners (Experiment 3) and relatively more expert
learners (Experiment 4) were instructed about a highly complex electrical circuit.
This material used system as opposed to procedurally based information and as
such aimed to extend the generality of the instructional technique.
Experiment 5 was designed to replicate and generalise the findings of Experiment
1 and 3. It also aimed to investigate the theoretical underpinnings of the isolated interacting elements approach. The participants of this experiment were
inexperienced learners in the domain of pneumatics. The students of Experiment 5,
however, had previously participated in either Experiment 2 or 4, which had used
instructional content from electrical engineering, as expert learners.
In the following chapters, each of these experiments will be described and
discussed in full. These empirical details will be preceded by appropriate
theoretical reasoning and specific hypotheses. Each experiment will be followedby
a discussion of results in terms of cognitive load theory and other findings.

165

Chapter 5

Assimilating

complex

information:

learning

isolated

elements then interacting elements
5.1

Experiment 1

The first experiment used two groups. The isolated-interacting elements group
studied isolated elements instructions at phase one followed by instructions
involving interacting elements at phase two. The initial learning phase was
intended to provide the opportunity to develop a database of knowledge, albeit of
discrete, organisationally isolated units. It was assumed that at this point, learners
have sufficient memory capacity to be able to learn these isolated element steps
but insufficient capacity to understand the reasoning behind the tests. The second
learning phase, when the interacting version of the materials was studied, was
intended to provide the forum to decipher meanings; to understand the relations
present between elements and to establish an anchorage between the more
complex information presented during this phase to the existing knowledge
elements. It was hypothesised that the initial period of learning using the isolated
elements instructions would allow the isolated-interacting elements group students
to develop rudimentary schemas that would assist them in learning the interacting
elements instructions during the next instruction phase.

166

The isolated-interacting elements group was compared to a more conventional
method of instruction for promoting understanding, the interacting elements only
group where instructions containing interacting elements were presented at both
phases of instruction. It was expected that the interacting elements only group
would perform more poorly at both phases. Compared to the isolated-interacting
elements group, the working memory resources of the interacting elements only
group would be overwhelmed because of the many elements they were requiredto
mentally integrate due to the high element interactivity of the material.
To summarise, we predicted that first providing inexperienced students with
isolated elements instructions (e.g., instructions of Figure 4.2) then presenting
them with interacting elements instructions (e.g., instructions of Figure 4.1) would
lead students to gain a superior understanding of the information than the
traditional approach of immediately presenting students with the complete version
of material explaining a complex concept.

Method
Participants
Twenty-two first year industrial trade students enrolled in a training course at a
Sydney company participated in the experiment. The students were from a varied
educational background but all had completed at least Year 10 of High School.
They were enrolled in either a one month basic industrial skills course (9 students)
or a six month manufacturing and engineering trainee-ship course (13 students).
Students were tested within their first month of training so the electrical material
covered at this point in both of the courses, was very similar. They understood
electrical principles at a basic level, such as how to locate the neutral, active and
earth pins of an electrical appliance. However, none of the participants had directly
studied the electrical safety tests used in the experiment. Students were excluded

167

from the study if they had completed further studies in the field of electrical
engineering; if English was not their first language; or if more than forty eight hours
elapsed between the two learning sessions of the study (e.g., due to illness of a
student).

Materials
The instructional materials used in the experiment consisted of information on
three electrical safety tests that are performed on 240 volt electrical appliances
using a volt meter. The three tests check various electrical circuits of the appliance
to ensure that it is operating safely. In this experiment the electrical applianceused
was an electrical kettle. The three tests were to check the: insulation between the
frame and the active and neutral points of the appliance (the Insulation Resistance
test); insulation between the frame and the earth point of the appliance (the Earth
Continuity test) and the insulation of electrical extension leads (the Insulation
Resistance of a Flexible Lead test).
For a novice learner, understanding why and how to do each of the electrical safety
tests was a complex task as the material is high in element interactivity; for
example, to understand the tests of Insulation Resistance of a Flexible Lead, the
learner must simultaneously consider what the test aims to achieve, the required
setting of the volt meter, the on/off status of the appliance, the placement position
of both leads of the volt meter, the criteria set for safe and unsafe readings, the
consequences of an unsafe reading and finally, the changes made to the
procedure to test the second circuit and thus complete the test. To understand fully
the concept of insulation resistance of a flexible lead, all the elements of the test,
their relations and implications have to be considered simultaneously. The element
interactivity of the material is high and so the intrinsic cognitive load of the
concepts is also high.

168

Two sets of introductory instructional material were developed: one set presented
isolated elements that had to be learned without emphasis on the interactions
between them (isolated elements condition) while the other placed anemphasis on
the interactions between elements (interacting elements condition). The
instructional materials were designed in line with Mayer and colleagues’ cognitive
theory of multimedia learning; for example, the instructional material contained a
combination of non-redundant verbal and visual material presented simultaneously
(Mayer & Moreno, in press; Mayer, Steinhoff, Bowler & Mars, 1995). Each set of
instructional materials consisted of a three page booklet. Each page of the booklet
described a different electrical safety test and contained a numbered diagram with
a separate section of correspondingly numbered text points below the diagram.
Both sets of instructions were designed to introduce and demonstrate the
equipment and procedures used in the three electrical safety tests described
above. A copy of the isolated elements instructions has been included in Appendix
1; a copy of the interacting elements instructions has been included in Appendix2.
The isolated elements version of instructions explained the procedural steps of the
electrical tests; for example, referring back to the instructional material illustrated in
Figure 4.1, the step by step procedure for performing an insulation resistance test
of an electrical appliance is displayed in the red text. The isolated elements
instructions included no text other than the bold text. Each step of the test
procedure can be learned in isolation without reference to previous steps. The
interacting elements instructions replicated the isolated elements version of the
information (red text in Figure 4.1), but also included other information relevant to
the tests such as the aim of the test and the logic, in terms of electrical circuit
theory, behind each step of the test (see normal text in Figure 4.1). The interacting
elements instructions therefore provided all the information for gaining a
meaningful understanding of the electrical test as a whole.

169

An analysis of the element interactivity of each of the electrical safety tests has
been included in Appendix 3. The analysis estimates the number of elements that
would need to be simultaneously processed for a first year electrical trainee with
only a very limited electrical knowledge to fully understand the test. Encoding
information from working memory should be much easier with the isolated
elements version of the instructional materials than with the interacting elements
approach because the isolated elements instructions have fewer interacting
elements than the interacting elements instructions and so should impose alower
working memory load. The difference between the two conditions is illustrated
clearly in the documents included in Appendix 3, where an estimation of the
number of elements that interact, in both the isolated and interacting elements
versions of the instructional material for the Insulation Resistance test, have been
included.
Although it is easier to hold the information from the isolated elements version in
working memory, it does not follow that it is possible to process all the information
about a test simultaneously, even with the isolated elements version of the
instructions. It may not be possible, given the nature of this material, to assimilate
all the elements of the isolated elements version in one reading. (It is certainly not
possible for the interacting elements version.) However, it is more likely that
information from the isolated elements version could be more readily encoded into
long term memory due to the reduced number of elements.
Cognitive load was measured in two ways: through the time required for instruction
and through subjective ratings of task difficulty (see Paas & van Merrienboer,1993;
1994). Subjective ratings were used as they “provide a powerful …(measureof) the
subjective experience of workload” (Gopher & Braune, 1984: 529) since students
have little difficulty assigning a numerical value to the imposed mental workload. In
addition, subjective ratings are easy to implement, are non-intrusive on the primary
task (i.e., learning the training materials) and correlate highly with more objective

170

measures of mental load (Moray, 1982; O’Donell & Eggemeier, 1986). The
subjective mental load rating measure used was a seven point Likert type scale.
Above the scale was the instruction "How easy or difficult was it to learn and
understand the electrical tests from the instructions you were given? Place across
on the rating scale below". Each number on the scale was labelled with a
description ranging from extremely easy (1) to extremely hard (7). A copy of the
subjective mental load rating scale used in the experiment has been included in
Appendix 4.
The test material consisted of test items and equipment for both written and
practical tests. Each test item was designed to be objective and was marked as
either correct or incorrect. The written test consisted of twenty three items. Some
problems had several parts so the test was marked out of a total of thirty-one
points. (It was possible to score a total of eleven marks on items relating to the
Earth Continuity test; nine marks from items relating to the Insulation Resistance
Test; eight marks from items relating to the Flexible Lead test and three marks
from items testing information that was provided in both the Insulation Resistance
test and the Flexible Lead test). A copy of the written test used in the experiment
has been included in Appendix 5.
There were two categories of questions in the written test: those tapping low
element interactivity knowledge about the test procedures and those tapping high
element interactivity knowledge of the electrical testing procedures and electrical
circuitry. Low element interactivity questions were factually based, asking about a
specific part of, or reason behind the procedure of a specified electrical test, for
example: "What do you set the volt meter to in the Earth Continuity test?" or "How
is power introduced to the circuit when testing for Insulation Resistance?". This
category of questions was low in element interactivity because they required only
the recall of factual information which could be processed as single element or a

171

few elements of information. This category contained eighteen questions. Each
correct answer to these items was worth one mark.
High element interactivity knowledge questions required the students to practically
apply their factual knowledge of the electrical tests. The application of knowledge
required the student to be able to hold the whole concept in mind simultaneously,
thus these questions could be considered high in element interactivity. Questions
involved tasks such as nominating the appropriate test to be used, judging the
appropriateness of a given test result and deciding what consequences mayarise
or what action was required to be taken due to this test result. Some examples of
applied knowledge questions include: "An extension cord is tested for insulation
resistance. The result is Neutral-Earth circuit = 0.5 megohms; Active-Earth circuit =
3 megohms. Would you pass this lead as safe? Explain your answer." or "A hair
dryer is tested and the resistance reading is 2.9 ohms. A fault occurs. Given this
information, where will the excess electrical current, from the power surge, go?
Why does the excess current go to this point?". There were several diagrammatical
questions where information about the situation had to be inferred from the
diagrams. Alternatively, responses such as the placement of the meter leads or the
expected test reading, also had to be physically represented in the correct
locations on diagrams. This section also included two questions whichrequired the
students to apply their knowledge of the electrical testing procedure to an electrical
appliance different from the one they had studied. Specifically, students were
required to apply knowledge of safety testing an electrical kettle to testing an iron
and a hair dryer. This category of questions required a holistic understanding of
each electrical test because the components of the test needed to be understood
and applied in their entirety to a new scenario. This category contained five
questions, some with several parts. Each correct answer to each item or part was
worth one mark; total marks possible for this category of questions was thirteen.

172

The practical test consisted of two items: both items required transfer of knowledge
to a novel situation. Specifically, students were required to apply knowledge of an
electrical kettle to safety tests of a fluorescent light. The students were required to
perform an Earth Continuity test and an Insulation Resistance test to a fluorescent
light. The equipment provided for the test was a fluorescent light and a volt meter.
Key parts of the light were labelled, e.g. Earth (E), Active (A) and Neutral (N). The
volt meter was set to 1000 Volts, a setting not required by either test: therefore in
order to correctly perform the test the student had to reset the meter correctly. The
performance of each electrical test was marked either correct or incorrect, so the
practical test was scored out of a total of two marks. The element interactivity of
knowledge tapped by the practical tests was high as students had to remember the
entire test procedure and apply it to a novel situation.

Procedure
All the students were randomly assigned to either the isolated-interactingelements
instruction or the interacting elements only group with 11 students in each group.
They were tested individually, in a quiet room. The students were informed that
they were to be given some electrical safety testing instructional material which
would be followed by a written and practical test and that this session was to be
repeated within 48 hours. Students were able to ask questions at any time during
the experiment.
In the first experimental session, the students in the isolated-interacting elements
instruction group were given the isolated elements instructional booklet while the
students in the interacting elements only group were given the interacting elements
instructional booklet. An unlimited time was allowed to read through the
instructions and this time was recorded by the experimenter. All students were
provided with a metal electric kettle and a volt meter. They were instructed to
perform the tests with this equipment as they read through the instructions. At the

173

completion of the study phase, the students were provided with a subjective mental
load rating scale, the format of which was explained to both groups. They were
asked to rate the mental effort involved in understanding all of the electrical tests
described in their training booklet on the scale described above.
The test section of the experiment followed. The students were asked to complete
the written test, described in the materials section, which was common to both
groups. They were allowed an unlimited time to complete the test. Students were
prohibited from reattempting any question once they had attempted it. The
instructional material was not available during the test period. The practical test
followed. Students were required to attempt both parts of the practical test in a set
order (Earth Continuity and then Insulation Resistance).
The second phase of the experiment was held within forty-eight hours of the first
phase. The procedure of the second phase of the experiment was identical to the
first, apart from two differences. 1) In the practical section, the order in which the
two electrical safety tests were performed by the students was reversed (that is
Insulation Resistance test followed by Earth Continuity test). 2) All students studied
the interacting elements instructions. Therefore to summarise, over both periods of
instruction the isolated-interacting elements instruction group studied the isolated
elements instructional material followed by the interacting elements instructional
material while the interacting elements only group was presented the interacting
elements instructional material twice.

174

Results and Discussion
The test score variables under analyses were written test scores, divided into low
element interactivity knowledge scores and high element interactivity knowledge
scores, and practical test scores. Means and Standard Deviations for these
variables are provided in Table 5.1. Other variables analysed included mental load
ratings and instruction times. Means and Standard Deviations for these variables
are also provided in Table 5.1.

Experiment 1.

Low and high element interactivity knowledge scores were analysed separatelyby
2 (Instruction Condition) x 2 (Test Phases) ANOVAs with repeated measures on
the second factor. The results for low element interactivity knowledge question

176

scores indicated there was no difference between the instructional groups, F(1,20)
= 2.65, MSe = 288.82. (The .05 level of significance is used throughout this paper.)
The test phases main effect indicated a significant difference, F(1,20) = 11.77, MSe
= 180.10, demonstrating an improvement over the two instructional phases. There
was no significant interaction, F(1,20) = 0.87, MSe = 180.10. The results for high
element interactivity question scores indicated there was a significant difference
between the instructional conditions in the expected direction, F(1,20) = 7.06, MSe
= 203.87, with the isolated-interacting elements instruction group performing at a
superior level. The test phases main effect was also significant, F(1,20) = 9.50,
MSe = 147.53, demonstrating an improvement over time. There was no significant
interaction, F(1,20) = 2.67, MSe = 147.53.
There were 2 practical tests. Each test was scored correct or incorrect and the
results of the two tests were added together to give a practical score out of two
points. These scores were assessed by a 2 (Instruction Condition) x 2 (Test
Phases) ANOVA with repeated measures on the last factor. Results indicated a
possible difference between the instructional conditions, F(1, 20) = 4.03, MSe =
0.14, p = 0.058 favouring the isolated-interacting elements group. There was no
improvement over time, F(1, 20) = 0.20, MSe = 0.11, nor was there a significant
interaction, F(1, 20) = 1.80, MSe = 0.11.
These results suggest that as the application of knowledge was increased, through
the need to apply knowledge to either entire situations with their interacting
elements (high element interactivity knowledge question scores) or in application of
the knowledge to a realistic, practical transfer task, differences in performance
emerged, suggesting superiority of performance by the isolated-interacting
elements instruction group.
The time taken by the participants for instruction were assessed by a 2 (Instruction
Condition) x 2 (Test Phases) ANOVA with repeated measures on the last factor.

177

Results indicated a possible difference between instructional conditions in thetime
taken for instruction over both phases, F(1, 20) = 3.33, MSe = 98161.07, p = 0.08.
An inspection of the means indicated that trend was toward the isolated-interacting
elements group requiring less instructional time in total than the interacting
elements only group. This measure also showed a possible difference in the time
taken over the two instruction phases, F(1, 20) = 3.88, MSe = 56831.73, p= 0.06.
There was no significant interaction, F(1, 20) = 1.92, MSe = 56831.73.
Mental load ratings showed a significant instruction condition main effect in the
expected direction, F(1, 20) = 4.74, MSe = 2.11, suggesting that the isolatedinteracting elements instruction group found their instructions less demandingthan
the interacting elements only group over both phases. In phase one this difference
was to be expected given the disparity in the amount of information in the different
sets of instructions. At phase 2 however, when both groups received the same
interacting elements version of the instructions, (the interacting elements only
group for the second time), a difference is still maintained (see Table 1). In fact, the
mean of the isolated-interacting elements group at Phase 2 is still well below that
of the interacting elements only group at Phase 1. There was no improvement
over time, F(1, 20) = 0.26, MSe = 0.77, nor was there a significant interaction, F(1,
20) = 0.72, MSe = 0.77. The lack of a significant interaction also suggests that the
difference in mental load ratings between groups was similar through both
instructional phases.
Paas and van Merrienboer (1993;1994) devised a method of combining
performance measures with subjective mental load ratings to investigate the
relative efficiency of instruction conditions. Instructional efficiency increases when
learners demonstrate either a superior performance than would be expected from
their level of mental effort or require less mental effort to achieve a given
performance score. Paas & van Merrienboer suggested:

178

combined measures (of performance and mental effort) may provide a
better insight into the cognitive consequences of training and task
environments

and thus

improve attempts

to design optimal

environments (1993: 742).
Performance can be measured by an objective test score relevant to the learning
task; subjective mental effort ratings require the learner to translate the perceived
mental effort required by the task into a numerical value. Efficiency (E) is
calculated by transforming both mental effort (M) and performance scores (P) to
standardised z scores. These z scores for each instruction condition are then
combined in the following manner to give a single efficiency score for each
condition:
E=

/M-P/
square root ( 2).

The sign of the relative condition efficiency depends on the relation between metal
load and performance scores. It is calculated according to these rules: If M-P<0,
then E is positive; if M-P>0, then E is negative. The greater the value of E, the
more efficient the instruction condition.
It was hypothesised that the isolated-interacting elements instruction group would
be more efficient than the interacting elements only group. In other words, it was
expected that the isolated-interacting elements instruction group would achieve a
greater performance for less mental effort invested. The mean and standard
deviations for normalised (Z) mental effort and performance scores and the
condition efficiency scores for each group are shown in Table 5.2.

Practical score efficiency measures were analysed in a 2 (Instruction Condition) x2

The general methodology introduced by Paas and van Merrienboer (1993; 1994)
can be adapted to indicate instructional efficiency using any two measures that can
be related in a coherent fashion. For example, it is reasonable to propose that an
instructional procedure that requires little student time but results in high
performance scores is more efficient that one that requires more time but results in
lower performance scores. Performance can be measured by a test score relevant
to the learning task; learning times can be measured by the amount of time
students spent learning from the materials. Learning times have also been used as
a indirect measure of mental load related to a training task (see Chandler &
Sweller, 1991). Learning time and performance score are standardised and
efficiency (E) then can be calculated using a version of the Paas and van
Merrienboer formula:
E=

/I-P/
square root (2)

where E = efficiency, I = instruction time and P = performance score on a test.
Using this procedure, it was hypothesised that the isolated-interacting elements
instruction group would be more efficient than the interacting elements onlygroup.
In other words, it was expected that the isolated-interacting elements instruction

183

group would achieve a greater performance for less instruction time invested. The
mean and standard deviations for normalised (Z) mental effort and performance
scores and the condition efficiency scores for each group are shown in Table 5.3.

between the factors, F(1, 20) = 0.02, MSe = 0.62. These results are also
represented in Figure 5.2 above.
The results for practical question efficiency scores also indicated a significant
difference between the instructional conditions favouring the isolated-interacting
elements instruction group, F(1, 20) = 6.56, MSe = 1.20. No difference was
suggested over the test phases, F(1, 20) = 3.24, MSe = 0.61. There was no
significant interaction between the factors, F(1, 20) = 0.01, MSe = 0.61. These
results are also represented in Figure 5.2 above.
The results from measuring instructional condition efficiency through both mental
effort and performance and instructional time and performance indicate that the
isolated-interacting elements method of instruction was superior in terms of its
efficiency on all measures of performance compared to the interacting elements
only instructional technique. In other words, the isolated-interacting elements
method of instruction has resulted in a much greater performance per unit of
mental effort or per unit of instruction time for all measures of test performance
using this group of students.
The results from the written test and the subjective mental load measures support
the hypothesis that the isolated-interacting elements instruction group performed at
a superior level to the interacting elements only group because this method of
instruction imposed a lower cognitive load. We assume that in the initial learning
phase, the isolated-interacting elements group of students did not fully understand

188

the complex concept of electrical safety testing, but they acquired the rudimentary
schemas. By obviating the need to process all interacting elements required for
understanding in working memory, students may have been able to acquire at least
some of the necessary elements, resolving the paradox of learning without
processing all interacting elements required for understanding in working memory.
Subsequently, the interactions between these elements could be learned in the
second phase allowing a more complete understanding of the material. Incontrast,
the interacting elements only group may have acquired few elements in either the
first or the second phase because the interacting elements may have exceeded
working memory capacity.
Students in the isolated-interacting elements method of instruction gained a greater
comprehension of the topic at the most meaningful level of understanding by the
end of training. Their superior performance was most evident where application of
their knowledge to a novel setting was required, a task requiring holistic
understanding of the information. This learning was achieved despite a single
exposure to the interacting elements explanation compared to the repeated
exposure of the interacting elements only group. The isolated-interacting elements
method students required less mental effort in order to attain this level of
understanding. In addition, this group achieved this level of performance after less
time studying the instructions than the interacting elements only instructional
technique.
These results can be explained from a cognitive load perspective. Novice students
often have difficulty acquiring complex concepts because their working memory is
overwhelmed. In order to allow novices to process the interacting elements of
complex material, initially they may need to be presented with information on the
topic that has had the intrinsic cognitive load or difficulty of the material, artificially
reduced. The student is able to isolate elements that constitute the topic, forming a
rudimentary schema without full understanding. At the next learning stage, when

189

presented with a complete picture of the complex topic, students who have
acquired this partial schema are much better able to process in working memoryall
of the elements and construct the more complete and complex schemas required
for a full understanding of the topic.

190

5.2

Experiment 2

The results of Experiment 1 pointed to the success of the isolated-interacting
elements method of instruction over interacting elements only instructions for
inexperienced learners. Cognitive load theory suggests the performance of the
novice learners was improved by having an initial stage of learning using isolated
elements instructions, thus artificially reducing the intrinsic load of the material.
This procedure allowed the formation of rudimentary schemas. At phase 2,
learners were able to more easily process the interacting elements information
presented because the partial schemas they had developed in phase 1 reduced
their working memory load.
If schema formation was the key to the success of the isolated-interactingelements
method of instruction with inexperienced learners, that method of instruction may
be of little or no benefit to more experienced or expert learners. These learners, by
definition of their expertise, already possess more sophisticated schemas in the
given area and so may be able to process the interacting elements in working
memory without undue difficulty. The purpose of Experiment 2 was to investigate
the success of the isolated-interacting elements method of instruction compared
with the interacting elements only method, over two learning periods using
participants with relatively more expertise than those of Experiment 1. Specifically,
Experiment 2 attempted to further define the conditions under which the isolatedinteracting elements method of instruction is successful. Therefore, Experiment 1
was replicated in all respects, except, in the expertise of the participants.
Experiment 2 used students who were considerably more expert than those of
Experiment 1. They were electrical and mechanical apprentices who had
completed almost half of their first year electrical training. At this point of their
training, all apprentices had demonstrated competency in many aspects of their
191

domestic and industrial electrical wiring courses. Thus, they had a sound
knowledge of all basic electrical concepts and the general area of electrical testing,
but they had not previously been directly exposed to the instructional materialused
in the study. In accord with our earlier theorising, the results of Experiment 1
indicated inexperienced learners benefited from isolated elements instructions
followed by the interacting elements instructional format, rather than receiving only
the interacting elements format. For the more experienced learners of Experiment
2, it was proposed that there would be no advantage in providing isolated elements
instructions followed by the interacting elements format. It was hypothesised that
these more expert learners would already possess many of the rudimentary
electrical schemas that isolated elements instruction helps to form. Therefore, the
benefit gained from this method of instruction may be negated using more
experienced learners.

Method
Participants
Twenty-five first year electrical

and

mechanical apprentices taking an

apprenticeship training course at a Sydney company participated in the
experiment. All of the students had completed at least Year 10 of high school and
were enrolled in various first year courses at trade colleges. At the time of testing,
all apprentices were well into their eight month training course in industrial and
domestic electricity (students had completed a mean of 8.32 units from a 21 unit
course). As stated, at this stage of their course all apprentices had demonstrated
their competency in all basic aspects of electrical testing. They were excludedfrom
the study if they had completed further studies in the field of electrical engineering;
if English was not their first language; or if more than forty eight hours had elapsed
between the two learning sessions.

192

Materials
The instructional material used in this experiment was identical to that used in
Experiment 1.
Procedure
The procedure used in this experiment was almost identical to that used in
Experiment 1. The only difference was in the allocation of participants to
instructional groups. Participants were matched for apprenticeship type (i.e.
electrical, mechanical, dual apprenticeship, fitter-turner, tool maker) before being
randomly assigned to either the isolated-interacting elements instruction group or
the interacting elements only group.

and Standard Deviations for these variables are provided in Table 4. Other
variables analysed included mental load rating and instruction time. Means and

Low and high element interactivity knowledge questions were analysed separately
using a 2 (Instruction Condition) x 2 (Test Phases) ANOVA with repeated
measures on the second factor. The results for low element interactivity knowledge
194

question scores indicated there was no difference between the instructional
groups, F(1,23) = 0.07, MSe = 462.92. The test phases main effect indicated a
significant difference, F(1,23) = 16.94, MSe = 105.74, demonstrating an
improvement over time. There was no significant interaction, F(1,23) = 0.03, MSe =
105.74. The results for high element interactivity question scores did not indicate a
difference between the instructional conditions, F(1,23) = 0.00, MSe = 682.07. The
test phases main effect indicated a significant difference, F(1,23) = 13.97, MSe =
187.42, demonstrating an improvement over time. There was no significant
interaction, F(1,23) = 0.28, MSe = 187.42. Practical scores were assessed by a 2
(Instruction Condition) x 2 (Test Phases) ANOVA with repeated measures on the
last factor. These results suggested there was no difference between the
instructional conditions, F(1,23) = 0.01, MSe = 0.55. There was no improvement
over time, F(1, 23) = 0.06, MSe = 0.32, nor was there a significant interaction, F(1,
23) = 0.58, MSe = 0.32.
The results from the written and practical tests suggest there are no differences in
test performance between the two instructional conditions. Thus, regardless of the
type of test, no performance differences were observed between the groups,
although the test of both groups tended to improve over time.
Subjective mental effort rating results did not indicate a difference between the
instructional groups, F(1,23) = 0.97, MSe = 1.69. There was no improvement over
time, F(1, 23) = 0.17, MSe = 0.46, nor was there a significant interaction, F(1,23) =
2.71, MSe = 0.46. These results suggest that these participants required the same
degree of mental effort to learn, regardless of their instructional condition. Of
interest, was the finding that the degree of mental effort required for learning did
not alter over time.
The only significant differences to be found related to instruction time. There was a
significant difference between groups in instruction time, F(1,23) = 5.79, MSe =

195

43048.96, in time taken over the two instructional phases, F(1,23) = 10.158, MSe =
8699.05 and a significant interaction between the factors, F(1,23) = 60.36, MSe =
8699.05. In order to assess the interaction effect, a test of simple effects was
carried out at each phase. At Phase 1, a significant difference was found between
the instructional groups, t(23) = -5.66 with the isolated-interacting elements group
requiring less learning time. At Phase 2, when the groups studied thesame version
of the experimental material, no difference was found between the groups, t(23)=
0.95. These differences between groups were due entirely to the isolatedinteracting elements group requiring less time to read their instructions in Phase 1.
Instruction condition efficiency measures were calculated by combining mental
load and performance scores according to the method devised by Paas and Van
Merrienboer (1993;1994). The performance variables used were high element
interactivity knowledge questions and low element interactivity knowledge

As noted previously, the calculation of relative efficiency allows two instruction
conditions to be compared. These results indicate when instruction condition
efficiency is measured in terms of performance per unit of mental effort, the
teaching techniques cannot be distinguished from each other. When efficiency is
measured in terms of performance per unit of instruction time, an overall difference
does not exist; however, at phase one students in the isolated-interactingelements

204

condition do show greater performance per unit of instruction time. These results
can be explained by the smaller amount of time required for instruction by the
Isolated-interacting elements group when studying the isolated version of the
materials in phase one.
The results from Experiment 2 contrast sharply with those of Experiment 1, where
instruction by the isolated-interacting elements method significantly improved test
performance. The level of expertise of the participants was the only identifiable
difference between the two experiments. This observation lends credence to the
hypothesis that the isolated-interacting elements method of instruction benefits
learners through assisting the process of schema construction. This hypothesis
explains why the isolated-interacting elements method of instruction showed
distinct performance advantages for novice learners (Experiment 1), who
previously had only very primitive schemas in the area. The more expert learners
in Experiment 2, already in possession of schemas for the general area of
electrical circuitry, learnt equally effectively, regardless of the instruction method
used.

205

Chapter 6

Assimilating complex

information: a replication

6.1

Introduction to Experiments 3 and 4

Experiments 1 and 2 investigated the viability of the isolated-interacting elements
instruction condition, a method of instruction that aims to increase understanding
by reducing intrinsic cognitive load due to element interactivity. Experiment 1
demonstrated that students in the isolated-interacting elements instruction
condition achieved superior performance and required less mental effort to
understand the material than the interacting elements only instruction group
receiving the interacting elements instructions at both phases. Experiment 2
examined learners who were relatively more expert and the results suggested no
performance benefits were to be gained from the isolated-interacting elements
method of instruction. We suggest that the isolated-interacting elements method of
instruction is more effective for novice learners because isolated elements
instruction at phase 1 allows critical elements to be processed in working memory
and so promotes the development of partial schemas that enhance knowledge
acquisition and more comprehensive schema development at the second phase of
instruction. These results were not obtained in Experiment 2 because students had
sufficiently comprehensive schemas to allow them to process all of the interacting
elements from the interacting elements instructions in working memory. They did
not need to have the instructions presented as isolated elements.

206

While the differing results of Experiments 1 and 2 were theoretically coherent, they
could have been due to the particular procedures and methods used. There was a
heavy emphasis on procedural learning in Experiments 1 and 2 and it could be
argued that the sequential and directive nature of this type of information may have
influenced the results. It is important to replicate the results of Experiments 1and 2
using materials that place a greater emphasis on conceptualisation rather than on
procedures. Accordingly, Experiments 3 and 4 used materials requiring a higher
degree of conceptualisation than Experiments 1 and 2. Identical materials and
procedures were used for both experiments but the students of Experiment 3 had
considerably less background knowledge than those of Experiment 4. We
predicted that the isolated-interacting elements instruction condition would be
superior to the interacting elements instruction condition in Experiment 3 but that
this difference would disappear in Experiment 4.

6.2

Experiment 3

Experiment 3 compared the isolated-interacting elements and interacting elements
only conditions using apprentices who had only a limited background in electrical
principles. As in Experiment 1, it was predicted that the isolated-interacting
elements condition would be superior to the interacting elements only condition.
The experiment used material depicting an electrical circuit used to power an
industrial oven. This material was very complex, containing more elements of
information and more interactions between the elements than the materials
employed previously in Experiments 1 and 2. It can be regarded as particularly
high in element interactivity because all of the elements that form a circuit relate to
and in turn effect, other circuit elements.

207

Method
Participants
Eighteen first year industrial trade students enrolled in a training course at a
Sydney company participated in the experiment. All of the students had completed
at least Year 10 of High School. They were selected from students enrolled in one
of the following courses: a six month manufacturing and engineering trainee-ship
course (13 students) or the practical section of a Year 11 school electronics course
(5 students). Students were tested within their first fortnight of training so both
groups had little electrical knowledge and what had been covered, with respect to
simple electrical concepts, was very similar for both courses. Both groups had
been introduced to very basic electrical principles (e.g., A represents active, N
neutral and current flows from active to neutral) but none of the participants had
ever studied the electrical circuit or any similar circuits to that used in the
experiment. Students were excluded from the study if they had completed further
studies in the field of electrical engineering; if English was not their first language;
or if there were more than forty eight hours between the two learning sessions of
the study.
Materials
The participant information sheet recorded demographic information such as age,
years of schooling, qualification being studied and stage in current course.
Participants were also asked details of any previous experience with electrical
training.
The instructional materials used in the experiment consisted of information
describing the electrical circuit used to power an industrial oven. The oven circuit
consisted of two interconnecting circuits: the power circuit and the control circuit.
The power circuit is formed by switches and fuses which connect and power a
three phase motor. The power circuit is connected into the main circuit: the control

208

circuit. This circuit controls the function and sequence of events required to bake
goods in the oven. The function and workings of the oven circuit were impossible to
understand without reference to the whole circuit; for example, in order to
understand the purpose and function of the oven’s timer, the students were
required to consider the interplay between the timer, the conveyor, the motor and
various switches. The material is high in element interactivity because
simultaneous processing of many elements is required to understand the oven
baking process and electrical system.
Two sets of instructional materials were developed to introduce the electrical
circuit: an isolated elements version and an interacting elements version. . Both
sets of instructions were designed to introduce and demonstrate the function and
sequence of events in an industrial oven baking process. The instructional
materials were designed in line with Mayer and colleagues’ cognitive theory of
multimedia learning (Mayer & Moreno, in press; Mayer, et al., 1995); for example,
the instructional material contained a combination of non-redundant verbal and
visual material presented simultaneously. Each set of instructions provided the
student with a diagram and text describing the electrical circuit that operated the
oven. The isolated elements version of the instructional materials aimed to
artificially reduce the element interactivity of the material by focusing only on
individual elements of information, not on the interconnections between these
elements. Each element of the circuit was represented as an autonomous unit in
the sequence of events, that together formed the oven cycle.
In contrast to isolated elements version, the interacting elements version of the
instructional materials presented material at its original level of element
interactivity, therefore, the interactions between elements were emphasised. This
version of the oven circuit also described the oven process and so text was
incorporated which was virtually identical to that used in the isolated elements
version. The difference between the two versions lay in the emphasis the

209

interacting elements version placed upon the cause and effect relationships that
drove the system; for example, these instructions highlighted how a switch closing
allows a coil to energise which then causes the forward contact switch in the power
circuit to close, allowing the motor to start and move the conveyor in a forward
direction so the goods are taken toward the heating elements for baking.
Theoretical information on electrical principles and practise are also includedin the
interacting elements version of the materials. An analysis of the element
interactivity of the electrical circuit has been included in Appendix 6.
In a similar manner to the instructional materials of Experiments 1 and 2, the
isolated elements instructions included fewer elements than the interacting
elements instructions. Nevertheless, the instructional material is very complexso it
may be the case that there are too many elements to be processed simultaneously
in working memory even for the isolated elements condition. The materials only
can be processed by forming partial schemas in long-term memory to reduce
working memory load. This process should be easier for the isolated elements
instruction condition because of the reduction in the number of elements compared
to the interacting elements instruction condition.
The isolated elements version was presented on a single A4 (21 X 29.5 cm) sized
page. Numbered text points were integrated into the circuit diagram. The
interacting elements version was presented in an identical format to the isolated
elements version except that it was on an A3 size page (42 X 29.5 cm). This larger
diagram was necessary because of the difference in the amount of text contained
in the interacting elements version. A copy of the isolated elements instructions
has been included in Appendix 7; a copy of the interacting elements instructions
has been included in Appendix 8.
Both groups also received a single, A4 (21 X 29.5 cm) sized page that depicted a
diagram representing the oven baking process. The diagram included key features

210

of the oven such as the motor, the heating elements, the conveyor and the oven
control panel realistically rather than as electrical circuitry. The diagram used these
features to show how the electrical circuit runs the oven baking process. The
diagram was accompanied by this text, "This circuit enables goods to be baked in
an oven. The goods are on a conveyor, which is driven by a 3 phase motor. The
conveyor transports goods to the oven’s heating elements where it stops and
cooking occurs for a prescribed time. At the end of the set cooking time, the
conveyor is reactivated and the goods are moved away from the cooking elements
back to their original position". The aim of this diagram was to provide the students
with holistic background information on the oven process in order to enable them to
gain an understanding of how the electrical circuitry combined to enable the oven
baking process to occur. A copy of this diagram has been included in Appendix 9.
Another diagram, depicting only the oven electrical circuit, was available to
students during testing. Switches and symbols were labelled on the diagram but no
textual description was included. It was presented on an A4 (21 X 29.5 cm) sized
page. A copy of this diagram has been included in Appendix 10.
The subjective mental load rating measure used a nine point Likert type scale.
Above the scale was the instruction "How easy or difficult was it to learn and
understand the electrical circuit from the instructions you were given? Place a
cross on the rating scale below". Each number on the scale was labelled with a
description ranging from extremely easy (1) to extremely difficult (9). The number
of points on the mental effort Likert scale were increased from seven in Experiment
1 and 2, to nine in this experiment, in order to give the students a greater range of
options to describe their subjective mental load when learning. A copy of the
subjective mental load rating scale used in the experiment has been included in
Appendix 11.

211

There were two categories of questions in the written test: those tapping low and
high element interactivity knowledge. Both types of questions included textual and
diagrammatical test items. Low element interactivity questions asked about a
specific part or fact of the electrical circuit. For example: "How can the operation of
the circuit be stopped manually?" or "What stops Coil R from overheating?". The
questions were low in element interactivity because the questions involved only a
single section of the circuit and the information required to answer the questions
could be learnt in isolation from the rest of the circuit. Answering the question
required only at most one or two elements of information to be processed
simultaneously. This section of questions tapping low element interactivity
knowledge included four test items with the total marks possible for this categoryof
questions being eight. Some questions contained sub-parts; for example, "What
three actions need to be performed by someone using the oven for the baking
process to begin?". This question required three responses, each response was
worth one mark.
High element interactivity questions dealt with issues such as the progression of
events in the circuit or the structure and function of the oven circuit. These
questions required students to have a holistic understanding of the circuit and its
processes. In order to answer the questions, the students had to simultaneously
refer to many elements of the circuit.. For example: "Circle the option that
completes the sentence correctly. When the goods initially reach the heating
elements, Limit switch 1 is signalled. The top of double pole switch 1.....?" or "For
the conveyor to start moving the goods away from the heating elements, what has
to change in this circuit? Place a cross on the diagram on all the item/s in the
diagram that need to be different. Indicate what change is required". The
information required to answer each question involved the simultaneous
application at least four elements of information. There were eight test items in the
high element interactivity category of questions and the total marks possible for this
category of questions was twenty-seven. Some questions contained sub-parts; for

212

example, "When the goods are baking, which switches are open?". A correct
answer to this question would identify seven open switches. Each correct response
was awarded one mark. A copy of the written test used in the experiment has been
included in Appendix 12.
Procedure
All the students were randomly assigned to either the isolated-interactingelements
instruction group or the interacting elements only group. All students were tested
individually, in a quiet room. There were nine students in each group. The students
were informed that they were to be given instructional material regarding an
electrical circuit which would be followed by a written test and that this session was
to be repeated within forty-eight hours. Students filled in an information sheet at
this stage that asked for biographical and educational details. Any students
considered to have prior knowledge (see criteria outlined in the "participants"
section) were eliminated at this stage. Students were able to ask questions at any
time during the experiment.
In the first experimental session, the students in the isolated-interacting elements
instruction group studied the isolated elements instructional booklet while the
students in the interacting elements only group were given the interacting elements
instructional booklet. Both groups also received the schematic diagram of theoven
baking process. Students were allowed a maximum of ten minutes to readthrough
the instructional material. If a student finished before this time, they were asked to
review the information for the remaining time. This procedure was used in order to
provide additional control by equalising study times rather than allow unlimited
times as occurred in Experiments 1 and 2. At the completion of the study phase,
the students were provided with a subjective mental load rating scale, the format of
which was explained to both groups. They were asked to rate the mental effort
expended in understanding the oven circuit and to translate this to a numerical
value.

213

The test section of the experiment followed. The students were asked to complete
the written test, which was common to both groups. The instructional materials
were not available to the students during this test period. The circuit diagram
(labelled circuit elements with no text description) was available to the students at
this stage but was removed for diagrammatical questions that required the
students to find faults in the circuit. At all stages of the test period, students were
prohibited from reattempting questions after they had attempted them and from
reviewing previously completed test answers.
The second phase of the experiment was held within forty-eight hours of the first
phase. The procedure of the second phase of the experiment was identical to the
first, except that all students studied the interacting elements instructions.

Results and Discussion
The test score variables under analysis were low and high element interactivity
knowledge scores and mental load rating. Means and Standard Deviations for
these variables are provided in Table 6.1.

The results for high element interactivity question scores indicated a possible

1.81. Tests of simple effects were used to explore the interaction. No difference
was found between the instructional conditions at phase 1, t(16) = 0.936. At phase
2, a significant difference was indicated between the groups, t (16) = 2.43, with the
isolated-interacting elements group performing at a superior level.

significant interaction, F(1, 16) = 0.50, MSe = 0.88.

219

Results for high element interactivity question efficiency scores indicated a
possible difference favouring the isolated-interacting elements instruction group
with respect to performance efficiency, F(1, 16) = 3.34, MSe = 1.86, p = 0.09. A
significant difference was indicated between the test phases, F(1, 16) = 9.52, MSe
= 0.28, demonstrating an improvement over time for both groups. A significant
interaction was indicated between the factors, F(1, 16) = 4.55, MSe = 0.28. Tests
of simple effects was used to explore the interaction. At phase 1, no difference was
indicated between the instructional conditions, t(16) = 1.17. At phase 2, a
significant difference existed between the instructional groups, t(16) = 2.11, with
the isolated-interacting elements group displaying superior instruction condition
efficiency by phase 2 of the learning process. These results are also represented in
Figure 6.1 above.
As predicted, the results of Experiment 3 do replicate and support the findings of
the previous experiment with novice learners (Experiment 1). The major difference
favouring the isolated-interacting elements instruction condition occurred during
phase 2 using questions testing for high element interactivity knowledge. In a
seeming paradox, for these students, the “understanding” or interacting elements
instructions of phase 2 were only effective after studying material during phase 1
that did not emphasise understanding. Interacting elements instructions in phase 1
reduced rather than facilitated the effectiveness of Interacting elements instructions
in phase 2.
The isolated-interacting elements instructional condition has now been established
as a superior means of information delivery using both procedural and more
conceptually complex material. The data support the theoretical mechanism
proposed to explain the success of the isolated-interacting elements instructional
condition. Learning by the isolated-interacting elements instructional condition
reduces the working memory load required to process a complex concept.

220

Learning isolated elements of information at Phase 1 frees memory capacity at
Phase 2, providing the learner with the ability to focus on understanding the
complex relations between the elements of a concept.

221

6.3

Experiment 4

The results of Experiments 1 and 3 indicated the superiority of the isolatedinteracting elements method of instruction over the interacting elements only
instructional technique for inexperienced learners. The theoretical explanation
generated from a cognitive load theory perspective suggests the isolated interacting elements instruction condition reduces working memory load during the
isolated elements phase resulting in the acquisition of partial schemas that permit a
more complete understanding during the subsequent high cognitive load, high
element interactivity phase. Experiment 4 used more experienced learners who
could be expected to have previously acquired the partial schemas that reduce
working memory load sufficiently to allow understanding. If interacting elements
instructions can be assimilated we can expect that isolated elements play no role
when using more experienced learners thus eliminating an advantage for a
isolated-interacting elements instruction condition.
Experiment 4 replicated the design of the previous experiments using the materials
and procedure of Experiment 3 but with a sample of more experienced learners.
The participants involved in this study were industrial apprentices, experienced in
the area to be studied and so it was predicted that no performance differences
would be obtained between the two instructional conditions. It was expected that
these more expert learners would not benefit from the isolated-interactingelements
method of instruction as the interacting elements instructions should not
overburden working memory.

222

Method
Participants
Eighteen first year electrical and mechanical apprentices enrolled in an
apprenticeship training course at a Sydney company participated in the
experiment. All of the students had completed at least Year 10 of High School and
were enrolled in various first year courses at trade colleges. The participants were
tested more than three quarters of the way through their eight month training
course in industrial and domestic electricity (students had completed a mean of
28.55 units from a 34 unit course). Apprentices were excluded from the study if
they had completed further studies in the field of electrical engineering, if English
was not their first language, or if there were more than forty eight hours between
the two learning sessions.
Materials
The instructional materials used in this experiment was identical to that used in
Experiment 3.
Procedure
The procedure used in this experiment was virtually identical to that used in
Experiment 3. The only difference was in the allocation of participants to
instructional groups. Participants were matched for apprenticeship type (i.e.
electrical, mechanical, dual apprenticeship, fitter-turner, tool maker) before being
randomly assigned to either the isolated-interacting elements instruction group or
the interacting elements only group.

223

The test score variables under analysis were low and high element interactivity
knowledge scores and mental load rating. Means and standard deviations forthese
variables are provided in Table 6.3.

Results for high element interactivity question efficiency scores indicated no
