In a pattern of results similar to those observed in Experiment 2 in which more
experienced learners also participated, there were no significant differences
between groups on either performance, subjective mental ratings measures or
instructional efficiency. These results are in line with our predictions. It can be
concluded that there is no performance benefit for expert learners using the
isolated-interacting elements method of instruction. This experiment has also
generalised the lack of effect gained by using the isolated-interacting elements
method of instruction for more expert learners because the same pattern of results
has occurred using different and arguably, more complex material. The lack of a
isolated-interacting elements instruction condition effect for expert learners has
now been established using both procedural and more heavily conceptuallybased
material.
We suggest that expert learners will not gain any advantage from an instructional
condition intended to more readily permit processing in working memory. The
processing and working memory capacity advantages that novice learners gain
from an initial exposure to instructional material which had a reduced intrinsic load
had no effect on this participant sample because the learners were alreadycapable
of processing the complex concepts in their entirety.

229

Chapter 7

Assimilating complex

information: theoretical support
7.1

Introduction to Experiment 5

Experiments 1 and 2 used identical experimental material with vastly different
results. This pattern of results was replicated in Experiments 3 and 4. In
Experiments 1 and 3, performance benefits were associated with studying isolated
elements instructions followed by interacting elements instructions. This
instructional technique was compared to a more conventional method of instruction
for promoting understanding where instructions containing interacting elements
were presented repeatedly. In contrast, in Experiments 2 and 4 when the same two
instructional techniques were compared, no differences between the two
instructional conditions were apparent. It is our contention that the disparity in the
results that emerged from the experiments was due to a difference in the expertise
or prior knowledge level of the two participant populations. Experiments 1 and 3
used two groups of students who were new to the concepts of electrical
engineering, the topic area employed in the experiments. The samples used in
Experiments 2 and 4 however, had completed considerably more domain specific
electrical training than their counterparts in Experiments 1 and 3 respectively.
Experiment 5 aimed to directly explore the hypothesis that the critical factor in the
success of the isolated-interacting elements method of instruction is the expertise

230

level of the students used. To this end, Experiment 5 aimed to partially replicate
the findings of Experiments 1 and 3 which had used samples of learners who were
inexperienced in the knowledge domain investigated. Therefore, Experiment 5
used a sample of students with little experience in the material to be studied;
however, the sample used consisted of the same students who had participated in
Experiments 2 and 4 as expert learners. This apparent contradiction is explained
by three factors: the domain specific nature of expertise, the different learning
materials employed by each of the experiments and the participants’ previous
experience with these subject topics.
As stated previously in chapter 1, expertise is not generic. It is developed only in a
specific knowledge area with experience and practise (De Groot, 1965; Chase &
Simon, 1973a,b; Chi, 1978). In Experiments 2 and 4, the materials were drawn
from the area of electrical engineering, in particular, electrical appliance safety
testing and electrical circuitry. In contrast, Experiment 5 used materials from
mechanical engineering, namely pneumatic or air powered mechanical systems.In
Experiments 2 and 4, the participants could be regarded as experienced learners
because of their previous exposure to the electrical material used for instruction in
the experiments. In Experiment 5, these same students could be considered
novice learners because they lacked knowledge about the pneumatic concepts
used in the learning material.
The results from Experiments 2 and 4 suggested that the participants in these
experiments did not benefit from the isolated-interacting elements method of
instruction. Experiment 5, by using different instructional material, aimed to
demonstrate that the isolated-interacting elements method could be effective with
these same students, provided they were inexperienced in the knowledge area
under investigation. Theoretically, this contrast can be explained by the contention
that the isolated-interacting elements instruction method benefits the inexperienced

231

learner because the process of schema construction is facilitated through an initial
reduction of the cognitive load of the learning material.

232

7.2

Experiment 5

It was hypothesised that Experiment 5 would replicate the findings of Experiments
1 and 3 and show a superior performance by the group taught by the isolatedinteracting elements method of instruction. It was expected that these students
would achieve this performance using less mental effort and would require less
learning time.

Method
Participants
Forty-two first year electrical and mechanical apprentices involved in a training
course at a Sydney company participated in the experiment. All of the participants
had completed at least Year 10 of High School and were enrolled in various first
year trade courses at trade colleges. The majority of the participants had received
no exposure to the area of pneumatics but ten students had received limited
exposure to pneumatics; all of these students had received the same training
(which did not directly deal with the pneumatic system used in this experiment)
held approximately five months prior to the experiment. These participants were
distributed evenly between the experimental groups. Half of the students in
Experiment 5 had participated in Experiment 2 of this thesis. A large majority of the
remaining students had participated in Experiment 4.
Students were excluded from the study if they had completed further recognised
studies in the field of pneumatics other than stated above; if English was not their
first language or if more than forty eight hours had elapsed in between the two
learning sessions. Students were also asked details of any previous experience

233

with the subject area of pneumatics. If they indicated previous knowledge of
pneumatics they were asked to provide details of their experience and self rate
their pneumatic knowledge as high, medium or low.
Materials
The instructional material used in the experiment described an indirectly controlled
pneumatic circuit for a single acting cylinder. For novice students, the pneumatic
circuit was complex because the material was very high in element interactivity.
The information is high in element interactivity because in order to understand the
system as a whole, one has to consider how the parts of the system interact; for
example, in order to fully understand the concept of indirect pneumatic control of a
single acting cylinder, the student had to simultaneously consider the role of the
pushbutton and how this interacted with the 3/2 way valve, the air pilot line, the air
supply sources, the control valve and the position of the cylinder. A student would
be unable to understand how the system worked by merely considering a single
element, such as the cylinder. An analysis of the element interactivity of the
pneumatic circuit has been included in Appendix 13. The analysis estimates the
number of elements involved in the system for a first year apprentice with limited
pneumatic knowledge.
Two sets of instructions were developed: an isolated elements version and an
interacting elements version. Each set of instructions provided information about an
indirectly controlled pneumatic circuit. The instructional materials were designed in
line with Mayer and colleagues’ cognitive theory of multimedia learning (Mayer &
Moreno, in press; Mayer, et al., 1995); for example, the instructional material
contained a combination of non-redundant verbal and visual material presented
simultaneously. Each version of the instructional material consisted of a booklet
containing two diagrams on separate pages. The first diagram illustrated the
pneumatic circuit in the off position. In the off position there is no air pressure in the
circuit and the cylinder’s piston is retracted. The second diagram showed thecircuit

234

in the on position. In this position the cylinder’s piston is extended because of the
air pressure in the circuit. Each diagram contained textual information describing
the circuit which was integrated into the diagram. These text points were numbered
in the sequence the student was to follow. The diagrams indicated the presence or
absence of air pressure through colour. A colour legend was included to allow the
students to decipher this information. A copy of the isolated elements instructions
has been included in Appendix 14; a copy of the interacting elements instructions
has been included in Appendix 15.
The isolated elements version of the instructions provided the student with a
rudimentary outline of the purpose and sequence of events in the pneumatic
circuit. The instructions contained a basic explanation of the components of the
pneumatic circuit and an outline of the events occurring in the circuit; for example,
information such as: “An air pilot line connects the control valve and the
Pushbutton valve” provides an explanation of the circuit components; while “Air
flows into the chamber of the single acting cylinder from the control valve’s air
source. The cylinder piston extends” provides information on the sequence of
events occurring in the circuit. The order of events in the circuit was also indicated
by the presentation of the explanatory text in chronological point form.
The interacting elements version of the instructions contained a detailed
explanation of the circuit components and a description of the cause and effect
relations involved in the system. In comparison to the isolated elements version of
the instructions, the majority of the textual points in the interacting elements
version dealt with several elements of the circuit (including details of their complex
structure) and their relations to each other; for example, in reference to the control
valve, the isolated version of the instructions explains, “The control valve rests in
the Off position so no air flows through the valve”. In contrast, the interacting
elements version states “In the Off position, Port 2(A) bleeds air from the cylinder
chamber through the valve to port 3(R) where it is expelled to the atmosphere”. It is

235

clear from this example the complexity of explanation is increased in the interacting
elements version through an increase in the number of elements describedin each
text point (the internal structure of the valve is also referred too) but also because
this version describes how the elements (the cylinder and the valve) relate in the
pneumatic system. Some points of the explanation, however, were identical; for
example, an initial statement: “The pneumatic circuit is in the initial rest position
with the cylinder piston rod retracted” was the same in both versions.
Cognitive load was measured in two ways: through the time required for instruction
and through subjective ratings of task difficulty (see Paas & van Merrienboer,1993:
1994). The subjective mental load rating measure used was a nine point Likert type
scale. Above the scale was the instruction “How easy or difficult was it to learnand
understand the pneumatic circuit from the instructions you were given?”. Each
number on the scale was labelled with a description ranging from extremely easy
(1) to extremely difficult (9). A copy of the subjective mental load rating scale used
in the experiment has been included in Appendix 16.
The test material consisted of a fault finding task. The task consisted of a diagram
of a pneumatic circuit similar to the one used in the instructional phase of the
experiment.

The major difference between these circuits, however, was the

presence of eight faults within the pneumatic system used in the fault finding task.
The students were asked to identify the system faults, thus, the task aimed to
identify the students who held a complete knowledge of how the pneumatic circuit
functioned and was connected. The students were asked to mark the faults they
had identified in the circuit on the diagram and to provide an explanation why each
of the components they had identified was faulty.
There were two categories of faults in the pneumatic circuit used in the fault finding
task: those tapping low element interactivity knowledge about the circuit and those
tapping high element interactivity knowledge about the circuit and pneumatic

236

systems. A low element interactivity fault indicated a fault which, in the circuit’s
current state of work, did not impact upon the ability of the circuit to function; for
example, the four low element interactivity faults involved misplaced internal valve
and cylinder components. High element interactivity faults in the fault finding task
were more directly concerned with the flow of air through the system. The high
element faults involved more fundamental changes to the system that would effect
the ability of the pneumatic circuit to function in its current state; for example, the
faults involved aspects of the system such as: an incorrect connection point
between the cylinder and the rest of the system; an extra air source; an incorrect
connection between a valve and the source of compressed air and an incorrect
connection between two valves. These types of faults would not allow the circuit to
run normally.
Each of the test items in the fault finding task was designed to be objective and
could be marked as either correct or incorrect. There were four faults in each of the
categories. Each fault was worth one mark. Therefore, both the low element
interactivity fault category and the high element interactivity fault category were
scored out of a total of four marks. A copy of the fault finding task used in the
experiment has been included in Appendix 17.

Procedure
Students were matched for apprenticeship type (i.e. electrical, mechanical, dual
apprenticeship, fitter-turner, tool maker) and experience with pneumatics before
being randomly assigned to either the isolated -interacting elements instruction
group or the interacting elements only group. There were twenty students in the
interacting elements only group and twenty two students in the isolated-interacting
elements instruction group.

237

The students were informed that they were to be given instructional material in
regard to a pneumatic circuit which would be followed by a written test and that this
session was to be repeated within forty - eight hours. All students were tested
individually, in a quiet room. Students filled in an information sheet. Any students
who did not meet the student criteria (see criteria outlined in the participants
section) were eliminated at this stage. Students were able to ask questions at any
time during the experiment.
In the first experimental session, the students in the isolated-interacting elements
instruction group studied the isolated elements instructional booklet while the
students in the interacting elements only group were given the interactingelements
instructional booklet. Students were allowed an unlimited time to read through the
instructional material; this time was recorded by the experimenter. At the
completion of the study phase, the students were provided with a subjective mental
load rating scale, the format of which was explained to both groups. They were
asked to rate the mental effort they had expended to understand the pneumatic
circuit and to translate this to a numerical value.
The test section of the experiment followed. The students were asked to complete
the written test, which was common to both groups. The instructional materials
were not available to the students during this test period. At all stages of the test
period students were prohibited from reattempting questions after they had
answered them and from reviewing previously completed test answers.
The second phase of the experiment was held within forty-eight hours of the first
phase. The procedure of the second phase of the experiment was identical to the
first, except that all students studied the interacting elements instructions. To
summarise, over both periods of instruction, the isolated-interacting elements
group studied the isolated elements instructional material followed by the

238

interacting elements instructional material. The interacting elements only group
was presented with the interacting elements instructional material twice.

Results and Discussion

element interactivity fault score and low element interactivity fault score; mental
load rating and instruction time. Means and Standard Deviations for these
variables are provided in Table 7.1.

Low and high element interactivity fault scores were analysed separately by a 2
(Instructional Condition) x 2 (Test Phases) ANOVA with repeated measures on the
second factor. The results for low element fault finding score suggested there was
no difference between the instructional conditions, F(1,40) = 0.85, MSe = 1.20.
There was no indication of a difference between the test phases, F(1,40) = 0.54,

240

MSe = 0.41. There was no significant interaction between the factors, F(1,40) =
1.12, MSe = 0.41.
The results for high element interactivity fault finding scores did not indicate a
difference between the instructional groups, F(1,40) = 0.66, MSe = 1.95. A
difference was found between the test phases however, F(1,40) = 17.13, MSe =
0.38, demonstrating an improvement in performance over time. The interaction
between the factors was significant, F(1,40) = 5.20, MSe = 0.38. In order to assess
the interaction effect, a test of simple effects was carried out at each phase. At
phase 1, no difference was indicated between the instructional conditions, t(40) =
0.16. At Phase 2, however, results indicated a significant difference between the
instructional groups, t(40) = 1.81, with the isolated-interacting elements
instructional condition performing at a superior level.
Subjective mental effort scores were analysed in 2 (Instructional Condition) x 2
(Test Phases) ANOVA with repeated measures on the second factor. Results
indicated no difference between the instructional groups, F(1,40) = 0.03, MSe =
4.78. The test phases main effect indicated a significant difference, F(1, 40) =
23.30, MSe = 0.40, demonstrating a reduction in the subjective mental effort rating
of both groups over the two instructional phases. There was no significant
interaction, F(1,40) = 1.60, MSe = 0.40.
The time taken by the students during instruction was analysed in 2 (Instructional
Condition) x 2 (Test Phases) ANOVA with repeated measures on the second
factor. Results indicated a significant difference between the instructional groups in
the time taken for instruction over both phases, F(1,40) = 16.45, MSe = 11506.33.
This result suggested that the isolated-interacting elements instruction group
required significantly less total instruction time. The results for instruction time did
not indicate a difference in test performance between the test phases, F(1,40) =
0.028, MSe = 8734.44. The interaction between both factors was significant,

241

F(1,40) = 14.36, MSe = 8734.44. A test of simple effects was carried out at each
phase in order to assess the interaction. At Phase 1, a significant difference
between the instruction groups was found, t(40) = 5.69; this result indicates that the
isolated-interacting elements group took less time to study the instructions at
phase 1 than the interacting elements only group. It should be noted that the
groups were studying the different versions of the instructions during this phase. At
Phase 2, when the groups studied the same instructions, no difference was
indicated between the groups, t(40) = 0.56. An inspection of the means indicated,
however, that the isolated-interacting elements group required less instruction time
at both time phases (see Table 7.1). This result was achieved despite Phase 2
being the first exposure of the isolated-interacting elements group to the interacting
elements version of the instructions.
Instruction condition efficiency measures were calculated according to the method
devised by Paas and Van Merrienboer (1993; 1994). Variables investigated
included the condition efficiency measures for high and low element interactivity
fault finding question scores. Table 7.2 shows the mean and standard deviations
for normalised (Z) mental effort and performance scores and the condition
efficiency scores for each group.

Results for low element interactivity fault finding time performance efficiencyscores
suggested a significant difference between the instructional conditions in terms of
performance efficiency, F(1, 42) = 8.56, MSe = 1.31, favouring the isolatedinteracting elements instruction group. The test phases main effect did not indicate
a difference, F(1, 42) = 0.30, MSe = 0.59. A significant interaction was indicated
between the factors, F(1, 42) = 11.49, MSe = 0.59. In order to assess the
interaction effect, a test of simple effects was carried out at each phase. At Phase
1, a significant difference was found between the instructional groups, t(42)= 4.86
with the isolated-interacting elements group showing superior performance
efficiency. At Phase 2, no difference was indicated between the groups, t(42)= 0.49. These results are also represented in Figure 7.2 above.
As predicted, the results of Experiment 5 do replicate and support the findings of
Experiments 1 and 3. By the end of training, students in the isolated-interacting
elements method of instruction displayed a greater comprehension of the system
at the most meaningful level of understanding. This superior performance was
achieved using significantly less total training time and despite only a single
exposure to the interacting elements explanation of the system. In comparison, the
interacting elements only group had repeated exposure to the full, complex
concept through the interacting elements version, yet, this reduced rather than
increased their performance on the most difficult questions. A comparison of the
groups in terms of performance efficiency when this measure is assessed as a
factor of performance per unit of study time indicated the isolated-interacting
elements instructional condition was a superior instructional technique.
The importance of Experiment 5’s results, however, go beyond a confirmation of
the “isolated-interacting elements instruction method” effect. The majority of the
students included in this experiment had participated in either Experiment 2 or 4.
Results of Experiments 2 and 4 suggested these particular students had gained no

250

benefit from the use of the isolated-interacting elements method of instruction; yet
the results of this experiment, using the same learners, suggested the opposite.
The difference in the success of the isolated-interacting elements method of
instruction across these experiments creates direct evidence for the importance of
the level of prior knowledge in the success of the isolated-interacting elements
instruction method. This method of instruction benefits learners with little
experience in an area because it permits students who have not acquired the
critical schemas needed to permit processing of material in working memory, to
begin to do so. Participants in Experiments 2 and 4 did not benefit from this type of
instruction because in the subject areas chosen for the experiment the students
already possessed sufficient schematic knowledge to process the material in
working memory. The key difference in Experiment 5 was that the learners were
inexperienced in the selected field and so required an isolated elements
presentation in order to be able to process the material in working memory. The
consequence was improved performance when instructed using the isolatedinteracting elements method of instruction.

251

Chapter 8

General discussion

8.1 The paradox of learning complex concepts
This thesis has investigated what seems to be a paradox of learning: some
material which is very high in element interactivity cannot be processed in working
memory with understanding until after it has been stored in schematic form in long
term memory. The thesis has investigated this paradox using a cognitive load
perspective. Cognitive load theory is based on the assumption that features of
human cognitive architecture have important implications for how we learn and
understand: notably, a limited capacity working memory or processing centre; an
ability to store immense amounts of schematically organised knowledge in long
term memory, which allows vast quantities of information to act as a single
information element, alleviating the processing burden on working memory.
Expertise develops as schemas are constructed and automated. The theoretical
assumptions underlying the experiments discussed in this paper werederivedfrom
this framework.
The structure of the information to be learnt also has critical implications for
learning. Information is comprised of a number of elements which the studentmust
learn and understand. If the information elements do not interact, they can be
processed separately in working memory with little cognitive burden. However, if
the elements forming the information interact extensively, working memory must
process the elements simultaneously. Under some circumstances, such as when
material is complex and so high in element interactivity, this task may be

252

problematic given that working memory can only deal with a small number of
elements at a time. This difficulty may be mitigated if the learner alreadypossesses
a schema for at least some of the information. While understanding of high element
interactivity material can follow schema construction because all relevant elements
are incorporated in the schema and so can be processed in working memoryas a
single element, a paradox was evident because it was not clear how the elements
that constitute a complex schema could be processed in working memory b efore
the schema had been constructed. This paper undertook to investigate an
instructional technique, the isolated-interacting elements instructional technique,
developed to combat this paradoxical situation.
It was proposed that initially, the element interactivity of complex material had to be
artificially reduced in order to enable a schema or partial schema for the
information to be developed. That reduction could be accomplished by presenting
the material as isolated elements that could be processed in working memory. The
consequence of the reduction in element interactivity of material may result in an
initial decrease in the student’s capacity for understanding. Over the longer term
however, it was hypothesised that the promotion of schema construction would
lead to an increase in the learner’s understanding. The isolated-interacting
elements instructional technique had two stages: firstly, students studied
instructions detailing only the individual information elements forming a concept; in
stage two, learners studied a version of the instruction that expounded all the
information elements and how they interact. In comparison, the interacting
elements only instruction condition took a more conventional approach to fostering
full comprehension by presented all the information for understanding at both
phases.

253

8.2 Summary of results
The experiments reported in this paper provided powerful evidence that for certain
groups of learners, information is better learnt through an instructional method that
presents isolated elements instruction followed by interacting elements instruction.
The first experiment demonstrated that when novice students are required to learn
complex information, the isolated-interacting elements method of instruction was
superior to the conventional method. Students in the isolated-interacting elements
instructional group demonstrated superior performance to the conventional
learning group on a knowledge test and practical task. In addition, the isolatedinteracting elements group rated their perceptions of the mental effort required to
achieve this performance as lower. When the relative efficiencies of the
instructional conditions were calculated, the results suggested the isolatedinteracting instruction condition was significantly more efficient than the interacting
elements only condition both in terms of performance per unit of mental effort and
performance per unit of instructional time required.
The finding of performance superiority of students studying in the isolatedinteracting elements group was replicated in Experiment 3 using different students
who were also novices to the content domain. This experiment used different,more
complex content material from the domain of electrical engineering. At phase 2,
after both instructional groups had studied the interacting elements instructions in
the final training phase, the isolated- interacting group performed better on the
most difficult test items that demanded a full understanding of the complex
relationships governing the electrical system the students had studied.
In contrast to the results of Experiments 1 and 3, the results of Experiments 2 and
4 seemed to contradict the success of the isolated-interacting elements
instructional technique. In Experiments 2 and 4, (using the experimental material
254

from Experiments 1 and 3 respectively), learners using the conventional full
interacting elements only method of instruction performed as well as the isolatedinteracting elements instructional group. The contrast of these results to those of
Experiments 1 and 3 can be explained by the differing levels of expertise of the
learners who had participated in each of the experiments. The learners in
Experiments 2 and 4 had more experience in the relevant knowledge domains than
the learners from Experiments 1 and 3. It was expected that the isolated-interacting
elements method of instruction, which allows schema acquisition in order to
facilitate learning and understanding, would be of little use to learners who already
possess rudimentary schemas and so may not experience a heavy cognitive load.
While an instruction time measure favoured the isolated-interacting elements group
due to the reduced amount of instructional material this group had to study in
phase 1, the subjective mental effort ratings and performance efficiency measure
of the instructional conditions did not differ, providing credence to the explanation
this thesis has provided.
The results of Experiment 5 provide significant theoretical support for the claim that
expertise is the key factor implicated in the beneficial effects associated with the
isolated-interacting

elements

instructional

technique.

Participants

from

Experiments 2 and 4 who had previously not demonstrated any differences
between isolated only and isolated-interacting elements instruction conditions,
were given materials to study in Experiment 5 from the field of pneumatics. This
domain was quite unfamiliar to these learners. In Experiment 5, it was found that
students performed better if they had studied the isolated elements version of the
instructions followed by the interacting elements version in comparison to repeated
exposure to the interacting elements instructions. The achievement of this
performance superiority also required less total learning time. The results of
Experiment 5 replicated those found in Experiments 1 and 3. The results provide
strong theoretical support to the claims of this thesis since the same participants
who did not benefit from isolated-interacting elements instruction in a domain in

255

which their expertise had been identified a priori did benefit from this same
approach in a domain in which they were novice learners.
The cumulative evidence of the five experiments reported in this thesis suggests
an alternative instructional technique can be used to circumvent the paradox of
learning about complex concepts. That is, when novice learners are required to
learn about complex concepts that are high in element interactivity, they will benefit
from isolated elements instruction followed by interacting elements instruction. This
method of instruction facilitates the construction of schemas by initially artificially
reducing the intrinsic cognitive load of the material to be learnt. Although this
restricts and perhaps prohibits understanding from occurring initially, aspects of
human cognitive architecture seem to dictate that when the concept to be learnt is
high in element interactivity and the learner possesses few if any schemas in a
domain then a degree of learning must occur before understanding. Further, the
results of Experiments 1, 3 and 5 strongly indicate that by the end of training,
students who have studied isolated-interacting elements instruction actually
understand more about the complex concept to be learnt than students who have
been exposed to all the elements of information repeatedly. The students have
gained this knowledge using less mental effort and less total instruction time. The
students who studied only the interacting elements managed to understand very
little about the concept because their working memories were overwhelmed
causing an obstruction to schema acquisition.
It has been further shown in Experiments 2 and 4 that the benefits associated with
the isolated-interacting elements instructional technique are limited to novices in a
particular content domain. Experts, who by definition already possess schemas in
a particular domain, can already hold the interacting elements of the concept in
working memory simultaneously and so do not unduly benefit from an instructional
technique that facilitates schema development.

256

8.3 Theoretical and methodological issues arising
from the research

8.3.1

Intrinsic cognitive load

It has been previously stated that intrinsic cognitive load is unalterable (Sweller and
Chandler, 1994; Sweller, van Merrienboer and Paas, 1998) because this type of
cognitive load reflects the internal structure of the material itself, that is, the number
of information elements and the degree of interaction between them. This
proposition is true, keeping in mind that understanding is an essential concomitant
of learning. Nevertheless, while understanding must be one of the ultimate goals of
learning, it may not be necessary or indeed, possible, as an intermediate goal.The
isolated followed by interacting elements approach suggests that when dealing
with very complex information, in order to allow novice students to process
interacting elements, the intrinsic cognitive load of the material should initially be
artificially reduced. Technically, this is achieved by eliminating the interactions
between the information elements. A large degree of interaction between the
information elements is the foundation of a concept’s intrinsic cognitive load since
this interaction requires information elements to be processed simultaneously. If
the interactivity between the elements is reduced, discrete information units are
essentially created. Serial rather than simultaneous processing of information
elements can then be undertaken, substantially reducing working memory load.
Students can study the concept at the original level of intrinsic load in a
subsequent learning phase of the isolated-interacting elements instructional
condition, at which point full understanding becomes possible. The success of the
isolated-interacting elements instructional condition is assessed by testing
students’ understanding of this version of the material.

257

8.3.2 Relationship to the redundancy effect
The experimental design used in the current experiments bears a close similarityto
that used to demonstrate the redundancy effect (Chandler & Sweller, 1991; 1996;
Kalyuga, Chandler & Sweller, 1998; Mayer, Bove, Bryman, Mars & Tapangco,
1996; Reder & Anderson, 1980; 1982; Sweller & Chandler, 1994; Yeung, Jin &
Sweller, 1998; Kalyuga, Chandler & Sweller, 1998; 1999) which occurs when
students presented additional, redundant information perform more poorly than
students presented less information. The similarities between the redundancy
experiments and the experiments described in this thesis are superficial, however,
due to substantial differences in the nature and construction of the experimental
material. In the case of redundancy experiments, both the material containing the
redundant information and the material with redundancy removed, are fully
understandable in their own right. Frequently, the redundant material simply
consists of a repetition of the basic information in a different form; for example, a
fully intelligible diagram might be redescribed in words (Kalyuga, et al., 1998;
Chandler & Sweller, 1991) or a full text may supply detail and background
information that a summary condition eliminates (Mayer et al., 1996; Reder and
Anderson, 1980; 1982). In the current experiments, the isolated elements
information was difficult or impossible to understand because critical relations
integral to gaining a holistic understanding of the concept were omitted in order
that the information could be processed in working memory. Notwithstanding this
distinction, it needs to be born in mind that both the redundancy effect and the
results of the current experiments can be explained by assuming that a set of
reduced materials can facilitate learning by reducing cognitive load.

258

8.4 Application of the research & future directions
The results of the research presented in this thesis have significance for
instructional design in the traditional classroom or paper based format as well as
for more recent educational technologies. Anecdotal evidence suggests that an
informal isolated–interacting elements approach to instruction for novices has been
utilised in classrooms for many years. The approach described by the experiments
presented in this thesis formalises this procedure, yet, it also has a much wider
application for the field of education given the rise of multimedia and computer
based instruction in this field.
The technology underlying computer based instruction supports and facilitates the
use of an isolated-elements instructional design due to the instructional designer’s
ability to control the sequence of instruction in this medium. Computer designed
and presented instruction can easily provide and enforce multiple phases of
instruction. During these multiple phases of instruction, the element interactivity of
the to-be-learnt concept can be manipulated; for instance, initially the element
interactivity of information could be restricted, then the interacting elements version
of the instruction could be presented at a point designated either by the instructor
or by the learner. The learning outcomes of user control in comparison to instructor
control over the ability to access more complex instructional information poses an
interesting question for future research. In addition, the ideal sequencing of
information from isolated elements to interacting elements would also be worthyof
further exploration; for instance, it may be the case that some middle stage of
instruction between the isolated elements and the interacting elements versions of
the instructions would be beneficial to learners’ understanding of the concept.

259

The results presented in this thesis have further application to instruction presented
and designed on computer. The results suggested that students with some
knowledge in a domain are able to cope with the interacting elements version of
the instructions immediately. Computer based instruction has the ability to
discriminate between learners through posing some type of knowledge
assessment before a concept is presented. Different paths of instruction could be
made available depending on the learner’s results on the assessment. One version
could be based on the interacting elements only instructional technique reported in
this thesis. The concept to be learnt about would be presented in its full complexity.
This path would be for the relatively more expert learners in the domain. Another
learning pathway could be provided for inexperienced learners; this would be
based upon the isolated-interacting instructional technique presented in this thesis
and would contain multiple phases of instruction where information presented
about a concept is increased in complexity across the phases.
Future research could also investigate other potential measures of cognitive load.
In the current experiments, cognitive load was assessed through subjective ratings
of mental effort and time taken for instruction. It has been suggested (Bannert,
2000, pers. comm.) that a record of the number and content of students’ questions
may be another fruitful method of assessing the cognitive load imposed during
instruction. Although the procedure of the experiments described in this thesis
allowed for and responded to students’ questions during instruction, no formal data
of this measure were collected. It would certainly be of interest in the future to
assess whether, as a cognitive load explanation would hypothesise, students in the
interacting elements only condition required more support in order to comprehend
the instructions than the isolated-interacting group.

260

8.5 Concluding comments
The conclusion that understanding and learning of very complex information can
be facilitated by presenting the information in a manner that initially precludes full
understanding is likely to be seen as highly controversial by some. Nevertheless,
from a theoretical perspective, there is a degree of inevitability about this
conclusion. We know that: (a) working memory is very limited; (b) complex material
involves more interacting elements than can be processed simultaneously in
working memory unless they have been incorporated into a schema; (c)
understanding requires all interacting elements to be processed simultaneouslyin
working memory. If these three propositions are valid, understanding cannot occur
until after appropriate schemas have been constructed. Instructional designs
intended to encourage students to understand very complex material prior to the
construction of appropriate schemas will fail. The experiments of this paper provide
empirical support for these conclusions.

This test aims to check that there is no "leak" in the flow of current from either the active or neutral
wires of the appliance to the frame of the appliance, which would cause the frame of the appliance
to become electrically active.
To achieve this two circuits are set up; the first including the frame of the appliance and the active
of the appliance and a second including the frame of the appliance and the neutral of the
appliance. Very large measures of insulation resistance (ideally "infinite" resistance) indicates that
there are no leaks in the flow of current to each circuit. A megger meter measures the resistance of
the circuit.
1. By setting the meter to read 500 volts, a larger than normal voltage will be introduced into each
circuit in order to test the appliance under a heavy load. Set the meter to read 500 volts.
2. The appliance is switched "on" to allow current generated from the megger meter to flow into
each circuit. Make sure the switch is "on".
3. To measure the resistance of the first circuit the megger meter is connected into the circuit.
Place the line lead on the frame of the appliance.
4. Place the earth lead on the active pin of the appliance's plug.
5. The test button of the megger meter introduces 500 volts into the circuit Press the test button.
6. A measurement of insulation resistance of the circuit can be taken from the needle of the meter,
with a reading of at least 1 Megohm (1,000,000 ohms) being sufficiently large to indicate that the
active-frame circuit possesses an acceptable level of insulation. This ensures that no current can
leak to the appliance's frame. Read the resistance from the meter. The required result is a reading
of at least one Megohm.
7. To complete the test of insulation resistance the resistance of the
circuit must be
measured. The second circuit is set up between the neutral point
appliance and the
appliance frame. Remove the earth lead from the active pin and place it on
neutral pin.
8. A reading of the resistance of the second circuit can be taken by pressing the test button of
megger meter and introducing 500 volts into the neutral-frame circuit. Press
button again.
9. To pass the test, the second circuit is also required to have a safe level
insulation
which indicates current has not leaked from this circuit. Read the
A
one Megohm is again required.

This test aims to check that in the event of a fault, the frame of the appliance does not become
electrically active from current which should be directed to the earth. This can be achieved by
setting up a circuit including the frame of the appliance and its earth point and measuring the
resistance between the two points. Resistance is a barrier which can keep current in the circuit.
Ideally, this circuit should have zero resistance.
1. A megger meter is connected into each circuit to measure the resistance. The ohms setting is
used to measure the very small resistances expected of this circuit. Set the meter to read ohms.
2. To set up to measure the resistance of the circuit, place the earth lead of the megger meter on
the earth pin of the appliance's plug and then
3. Place the line lead on the frame of the appliance.
4. When the test button of the megger meter is pressed, a measurement
the resistance of the
circuit can be taken from the needle of the meter. Press the test button.
5. A resistance reading of less than 2 ohms is sufficiently small
that there Is no
dangerous barrier in the earth-frame circuit, blocking current from
the resistance from the meter. The required result is a reading of less

The Insulation Resistance of a Flexible Lead Test

This test aims to check that there is no "leak" in the flow of current from either the active or
neutral wires to the earth wire of the flexible lead. This would cause current to flow through the
earth wire back to the power source and could be potentially dangerous. To achieve this two
circuits are set up; the first including the earth of the flexible lead and the active of the flexible
lead and a second including the earth of the flexible lead and the neutral of the flexible lead.
Very large measures of insulation resistance (ideally "infinite" resistance) indicates that there
are no leaks in the flow of current to each circuit. A megger meter is connected into each circuit
to measure the resistance.
1.. By setting the meter to 500 volts, a larger than normal voltage will be introduced into each
circuit in order to test the appliance under a heavy load. Set the meter to read 500 volts.

2. To measure the resistance of the first circuit the megger meter is connected into the circuit.
Place the earth lead from the meter on the earth pin of the flexible lead and then
3. Place the line lead on the active pin of the flexible lead.
4. The test button of the megger meter introduces 500 volts into the circuit in order to test it.
Press the test button.
5. A measurement of insulation resistance of the circuit can be taken from the needle of the
meter, with a reading of at least 1 Megohm (1 ,000,000 ohms) being sufficiently large to indicate
that the earth-active circuit possesses an acceptable level of insulation. This ensures there is
no leak in the flow of current to earth. Read the resistance from the meter. The required result
is a reading of at least one Megohm.
6. To complete the test of insulation resistance the resistance of the second circuit must be
measured. The second circuit is set up between the neutral and the earth points of the lead
therefore remove the line lead from the active pin and place it on the neutral pin.
7. A reading of the resistance of the second circuit can be taken by pressing the test button of
the megger meter and introducing 500 volts into the neutral-earth circuit. Press the test button
again.
8. To pass the test, the second circuit is also required to have a safe level of insulation
resistance which indicates current has not leaked from this circuit Read
resistance. A
reading of at least one Megohm is again required.

Appendix 3:

An analysis of the element
interactivity of the instructional
material used in
Experiments 1 & 2.

The elements of interacting information have been estimated for each of the
electrical safety tests. These estimates represent what could reasonably be
supposed to be the case for novices electricians.

Earth Continuity testIsolated Elements version of Instructions
1 . The meter must be set to ohms.
2. The earth lead of the meter must be placed to the earth pin on the appliance
plug.
3. The line lead of the meter must be placed to some point on the metallic frame of
the appliance.
4. The test is performed by pressing the test button
5. The result is taken from the meter and checked against the criteria of less than
two ohms.

Interacting Elements Version of Instructions
1 . The test checks that, in the event of a fault, the appliance frame will not carry
current which should go to earth.
2. The test measures resistance.
3. Resistance is a barrier which can stop current leaving a circuit.
4. This circuit should ideally have zero resistance.
5. Resistance is measured by a megger meter.
6. The meter must be set to ohms.
7. Ohms are units that are able to measure very small levels of resistance.

s. The earth lead of the meter must be placed to the earth pin on the appliance
plug.
g. The line lead of the meter must be placed to some point on the rnetallic frame
of the appliance.

10. The test is performed by pressing the test button.
11 . A resistance reading can be taken from the meter and checked against the
criteria of less than two ohms.
12. Although zero ohms is ideal, a result of less than two ohms is an acceptable
level of resistance for the circuit to possess.

Insulation Resistance test
Isolated Elements version of Instructions
1 . The meter is set to 500 volts.
2. The appliance is switched "on".
3. The line lead of the meter must be placed to some point on the metallic frame
of the appliance.
4. The earth lead of the meter must be placed to the active pin on the appliance

plug.
5. The test is performed by pressing the test button and then checking the result
meets the criteria of at least one megohm.
6. Change the earth lead to the neutral pin of the appliance pin.
7. Test this circuit by pressing the test button again and checking the result is at
least one megohm.

Interacting Elements Version of Instructions
1 . The test checks that there is no leak in the flow of current through the appliance
circuit that would cause the appliance frame to become electrically active.
2. To establish that there is no leaks, two circuits have to be tested: the circuit
between the active point and the appliance frame and the circuit
neutral point and the appliance frame.

the

3. The test measures the insulation resistance of the circuit. If the reading is high,
it follows that current is not being leaked from the circuit.

4. Ideally a circuit should have infinite resistance but a safe criteria of at least 1
megohm has been set.
5. The megger meter is set to 500 volts.

6.

This setting means the circuits are tested under a larger than normal voltage.

7. The appliance is switched "on".

8. This is so current from the megger meter can flow through each circuit.
9. To set up the first circuit, the line lead of the meter must be placed to some
point on the metallic frame of the appliance.
10. The earth lead of the meter must be placed to the active pin on the appliance

plug.
11 . The test is performed by pressing the test button which introduces 500 volts to
the circuit.
12. The result must meet the criteria of at least one megohm to ensure that no
current is being leaked to the appliance frame.
13. One megohm is equal to 1 000 000 ohms.
14. The resistance of the second circuit must be measured to complete the test.
15. The earth lead is moved onto the neutral pin of the appliance plug.

16. Test the second circuit by pressing the test button again and checking the
result is at least one megohm.

Appendix 6:
An analysis of the element
interactivity of the instructional
material used in
Experiments 3 & 4.

The elements of interacting information have been estimated for each versions
of the oven circuit. These estimates represent what could reasonably be
supposed to be the case for novices electricians.

Isolated Elements version of Instructions
1. There are two circuits for the oven: a power circuit & a control circuit
2. When oven door shuts, the oven door limit switch is closed.
3. Main isolator switch is connected in circuit with the motor.
4. The main isolator switch must be turned on.
5. The baking process begins when Start is pressed
6. Pressing start activates Coil F.
7. Switch F1 immediately closes.
8. Start button can now be released.
9. Coil F closes the Forward contact switch in the power circuit.
10. The motor starts, moving the conveyor and the goods to baked toward the
heating elements.
11. Limit switch 1 is a double pole switch. The moving conveyor activates it.
12. The top of Double pole switch 1 opens while the bottom of Double pole
switch 1 closes.
13. The forward contact switch opens.
14. The timer is activated.
15. At the end of the set baking time, switch T1 closes
16. Coil R is energised
17. Switch R1 closes
18. Switch R2 opens
19. Switch T1 opens
20. The Reverse Contact switch in the power circuit closes
21 . The motor starts, moving the conveyor and the baked goods away from the
heating elements.
22. Limit switch 2 opens when the conveyor reaches its original position.
23. The Reverse Contact switch in the power circuit opens
24. TOLs are line line with the coils and the motor to stop overheating.
25. Pressing the stop button will open the circuit.
26. Pressing the emergency stop push button will open the circuit.

Interacting Elements Version of Instructions
1. Current flows from active to neutral and can only flow through a completely
closed circuit
2.There are two circuits for the oven: a power circuit & a control circuit
3. When oven door shuts, the oven door limit switch is closed.
4. Main isolator switch is connected in circuit with the motor.

5.Main isolator switch controls the flow of current to the motor
6. The main isolator switch must be turned on to start the oven baking.

7. The power circuit needs a separate switch to the control circuit as the motor
may be in a separate location to the oven and/or conveyor

8. The baking process begins when Start is pressed.
9. Depressing the start button creates a closed circuit
1 0. Current can flow through and activate Coil F.

11 . Activated Coil F immediately closes Switch F1

12. This creates a holding contact .
13. Start button can now be released
14. Because current is supplied to coil F through he holding contact created by

F1.
1 5. Coil F closes the Forward contact switch in the power circuit.

16.The closed Forward contact switch allows current to activate the motor
17. The motor starts, moving the conveyor and the goods to baked toward the

heating elements.
18. Limit switch 1 is a double pole switch. The moving conveyor activates it.

19. The top of Double pole switch 1 opens stopping the supply of current to
coil F.
20. This causes the Forward contact switch to open, stopping the motor and
the conveyor
21 . This switch opens as a safety measure so the motor is supplied with

current to go forward and reverse at he same time

22. The bottom of Double pole switch 1 closes, supplying current to the Timer
23. The timer is activated. It has a pre set baking time to cook the goods.

24. At the end of the set baking time, switch T1 closes
25. Switch T1 is activated by the timer

26. The closure of T1 creates a closed circuit so current can flow to

R

27. Coil R is energised
28. Coil R immediately closes Switch R1
29. Switch R1 acts as a holding circuit, maintaining the supply of current to

Coil R
30. Coil R opens Switch R2
31 . Current is no longer supplied to the timer

32. The de-energised timer opens Switch T1
33. Coil R closes the Reverse Contact switch in the power circuit
34. The motor starts, moving the conveyor and the baked goods away from the
heating elements.
35. Limit switch 2 opens when the conveyor reaches its original position.
36. This stops the supply of current to Coil R
37. This causes the Reverse Contact switch in the power circuit to open
38. This stops the supply of current to the motor, stopping the conveyor
39. TOLs are line line with the coils and the motor to stop overheating.
40. If overheating occurs, the TOLs open, stopping the supply of current
41 . Pressing the stop button will open the circuit.

42. Pressing this button will stop the oven or the conveyor.
43. This button is accessed by the oven operator
44. Pressing the emergency stop push button will open the circuit.
45. This button will stop any part of the oven process as active cannot flow to
neutral.
46. This button is probably not located at the actual oven but in a more general
position in the factory

Appendix 13:
An analysis of the element
interactivity of the instructional
material used in Experiment 5.

Isolated Elements version of Instructions
1. A single acting cylinder is indirectly controlled because it operates at a high
speed.

2. There are two sources of air supplying the circuit.
3.

Valves control air supply. This circuit has two valves.

4.

When the pneumatic circuit is at rest the cylinder's piston is retracted and no

air flows through the circuit.
5.

One valve is controlled by a pushbutton. Pushing this button moves the circuit

into the On position.

6.

When the button is pushed, air flows from the pushbutton valve to the control

valve.
7. Air from the Control valve supply flows to the cylinder chamber,causing the
piston to extend to date stamp boxes on a production line.
8. When the pushbutton is released the flow of air around the circuit stops, the
cylinder's piston retracts and the state of the circuit reverts back to the Off position.

d

II
I

Interacting Elements Version of Instructions
1. A single acting cylinder is indirectly controlled because it operates at a high
speed.
2. There are two sources of air supplying the circuit.
3.

Valves control the air supply of the circuit through their switching positions.

Each switching position is configured differently to channel the flow of air.
4. The 3/2 way push button valve has 3 openings or ports and 2 possible
switching positions. Pushing the button puts in the valve into the On position.
5. When the pushbutton valve is in the Off switching position, the supply of air
through port 1P is blocked and air from the circuit is expelled into the atmosphere
through port 3R.
6. The circuit has a second valve, the control valve. It also has two switching
positions.
7. Air supplied by the pushbutton valve through a pilot line (12Z) to the control
valve directs the choice of switching position.
8. When the control valve is in the Off position, no air comes through the pilot line
( 1 2Z) and the control valve's air supply through port 1P is capped.
9.

When the control valve is in the Off position, air is bled out from the cylinder to

the atmosphere through the control valve's port 3R.
1 0 All these factors result in the retracted position of the cylinder's piston.
11 . When the pushbutton valve is in the On position, air flows through port 1P into
the valve and then into the pilot line (12Z) supplying the control valve, forcing it
into the On position
i 2. When the Control valve is in the On position, it supplies air through port 1P,

from its own source, to the cylinder.
13. This causes the cylinder's piston to extend and date stamp

on a

production line.
1 4. When the pushbutton is released, the pushbutton valve moves
position, causing air supply through the pilot line to stop.
1 5. The air that forced the control valve into the On position is

Off

atmosphere

port 3R in the pushbutton valve causing the control valve to

move back into

Off position.

16. Air is no longer supplied to the cylinder from the control valve. Air from the
cylinder is bled to the atmosphere through the Control Valve.
17. These factors cause the cylinder's piston to retract.
